A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

This funding proposal aims to mainstream and operationalise a sustainable river-basin approach for watershed management in the Gandaki River Basin in Nepal through climate adaptation measures across the entire basin.

The project will be achieved through the planning and implementation of climate change adaptation measures across the entire Gandaki River Basin, which is divided into 14 sub-basins. The project will adopt a multi-level district and municipal (administrative/local boundary) based approach applied over the past 50 years in Nepal to bring about sustainable development at the local level. This approach has been successful in creating more holistic river basin-wide level that cuts across political/administrative boundaries.

On completion in 2026, the Gandaki River Basin will be used as a model to showcase how climate-resilient development in large river basins can occur throughout Nepal.


Climate change

Nepal's climate varies widely across seasons and geographical regions. Meteorological data from weather stations show clear increases in mean and maximum temperatures and the climate is predicted to change significantly in the future, with an increase of 1°C by 2035, 2°C by 2065, and up to 4°C by 2095 compared to the period 1986-2005. Rainfall is projected to increase by 7% between the 2000s and mid-2090s based on the Metreological Service of Nepal.


Rainfall

Most rain (80%) is received during the monsoon, and consequently, the winter months are dry. The average annual rainfall has increased from about 1500 mm in the 1960s to over 2300 mm by 2014. Rainfall trends (decreasing) in average rainfall over the last 80 years. By using the PRECIS model, Pandey et al. (2014) make detailed projections of future precipitation for Nepal and predict that there will be a further increase in rainfall.

The results indicate monsoon precipitation could increase by 2% to 30%, 2050 to 2069; and by 45% to 2090. Annual precipitation is projected to increase from about 1700 mm in the 1980s to over 2000 mm by 2090, with a period of increasing rainfall between 2030-2060 and decreasing rainfall after 2060.

Rainfall trends (decreasing) in average rainfall over the last 80 years. By using the PRECIS model, Pandey et al. (2014) make detailed projections of future precipitation for Nepal and predict that there will be a further increase in rainfall.


Water resources

Water resources for ecosystems like forests, grasslands and wetlands and agriculture during the winter to summer period are limited due to low rainfall. The project proposes to address this issue by engaging with key stakeholders and community members during the Feasibility Study (FB). These projections underscore the risk of occurrence and magnitude of extreme events such as severe droughts, floods and landslides during the

## Monsoon period. This significantly impacts already vulnerable communities, increasing high recovery costs, straining limited financial resources and placing fragile development gains at risk.

### As indicated in the Table 14 on page 41 of the FS, derived from the MFOP Report, the total rainfall distribution on an annual basis is expected to remain largely unchanged under RCP 4.5 (2006–2035) and RCP 8.5 (2006–2035). However, there is a potential for an increase in the intensity of precipitation as indicated by increased in extreme wet days ranging from 21–47% under RCP 4.5 and 39–75% under RCP 8.5. This will have significant impacts on the GRB (Ghaghara Basin & Sivalik region) and an increase in consecutive dry days (CDD). The changing rainfall patterns will affect water availability, particularly during the pre-monsoon season when there is a greater demand for water from agriculture and smaller water sources that are expected to increase in discharge. The more significant precipitation changes are seen under RCP 8.5 with increases of up to 75% in extreme wet days compared to RCP 4.5 (Table 12, pages 10–13) and the CFS for the Nepal as a whole; however they are representative of the GRB which is reflected in Table 14 of the FS.

### During the monsoon period 80% of the annual total rainfall occurs, an increase in rainfall for RCP 4.5 (RCP 6 -2006 to 2035) and 13.6% (RCP 8.5 – 2006 to 2035) is significant; and will likely lead to an increase in flooding and landslides, particularly during the pre-monsoon season when there is a greater demand for water from agriculture and smaller water sources that are expected to increase in discharge. This results indicate a drop in precipitation for the winter (near term – RCP 4.5) and pre-monsoon season (RCP 4.5) and an increase in extreme wet days under RCP 8.5. The decrease in precipitation is consistent with the decreasing trend that the rainfall season is getting shorter. This is also consistent with the decreasing trend of snow cover observed over the Himalayan Mountains. As a result, there will be less water available for agriculture during the pre-monsoon season; it is likely that watershed recharge capacity will be less compared to historical levels for the pre-monsoon season. The increase in extreme wet days under RCP 8.5 will lead to an increase in flooding and landslides with a greater demand for water from agriculture and smaller water sources that are expected to increase in discharge, particularly during the pre-monsoon season when there is a greater demand for water from agriculture and smaller water sources that are expected to increase in discharge.

### A new study by Kirschbaum, D. et al. at the High Mountain Areas has applied a near-global landmodel-based case of the Landslide Hazard Assessment for Situation Awareness (LHASA) with satellite and GCM precipitation estimates. The results indicate that there will be an increase in frequency which will affect landslide activity in the Himalayan Mountains. This is consistent with the decreasing trend observed over the Himalayan Mountains. The study also indicates a decrease in snow cover, particularly during winter months, which could lead to potential landslide activity (by 30–70%). In addition, the largest changes to landslide activity in the future scenario of 2051-2080 are expected under RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5 with an increase in frequency of landslides. The most significant risk factor is triggering large lakes outflow floods (GLOFs) which could inundate communities and infrastructure.

### Monsoon: the impact of climate change on the water balance and hydrological regime of the snow-dominated Kalganbasin Basin, which is an important sub-basin of the GRB, was modeled in 2007. The results are indicative of a significant increase in discharge during the pre-monsoon season under RCP 4.5 (18–36%) and RCP 8.5 (29–48%). Increase in discharge is projected to be maximum during the pre-monsoon under RCP 4.5 and the monsoon under RCP 8.5. A significant increase was also reported by Immerzeel et al. & Bhattachar Jha in a study of the Langtang Basin, Nepal. The authors concluded that the increase in discharge is mainly attributed to precipitation and temperature (causing snowmelt). Immerzeel et al. also reported that higher temperatures are likely to increase evapotranspiration and increase snow ice melt.

### Modeling results, using a 10km X 10km grid show these will lead to significant changes in the basin’s water balance and hydrological regime. The increase in discharge is expected at the outlet of the basin with snowmelt contribution largely affected by climate. Consequently, water availability in the basin is not significantly affected as there are no major lakes or reservoirs within the basin. However, the increased contribution of runoff components in stream flow: a case study from Langtang Basin, Nepal. Hydrol. Meteorol. 9 (1):74–84.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 PAGE 4 OF 80

#### Apparent, and projected climate change problems have created several adaptation needs in the GRB, the crucial adaptive measures are: a) increased irrigation water availability for reduced water availability for irrigation and drinking; landslides and soil erosion in the upstream; flood, inundation and sedimentation in the downstream; harmful forest fires and forest degradation due to drought; b) loss of biodiversity and ecosystem services; c) lack of capacity and knowledge of farmers various barriers to address their adaptation needs. The project has identified lack of information and knowledge on climate change impacts and adaptation measures as a major barrier to addressing these adaptation needs, weak social license, low capacity and capability, and lack of access to finance and markets as the major barriers to adopting climate-resilient practices and technologies. The project also identified the need for an improved system of planning and implementing intervention measures on a political and technical basis needs to be shifted to an ecosystem-based approach in line with the GRB’s vision.

#### Given the identified climate change adaptation needs and barriers, it was concluded that this project should adopt an ecosystem-based approach to address these adaptation needs and reduce the vulnerability of communities to climate change. The project identified eight intervention areas. Interventions are designed to: 1) improve climate resilient agroforestry practices; 2) enhance forest restoration and rehabilitation; 3) promote community-based watershed restoration based actions for reducing impacts of landslides and floods; 4) enhance technical capacity of GRB communities in the implementation of climate change adaptation measures; 5) establish a participatory, evidence-based mechanism for planning, restoration, monitoring, and maintenance of ecosystems; 6) incorporate ecosystem-based approaches into the existing GRB management system; 7) promote community-based natural resource management system for climate-resilient River Basin Management.

#### Climate Change Adaptation

12. The project will significantly improve the resilience of communities and enterprises to the GRB. There will be 100,000 hectares (ha) of forest land restored and rehabilitated through the project and 250,000 households (1,052,500 people or 60% females) indirectly benefiting from the project implementation. The major benefits in implementing this project will be: a) restoration and rehabilitation of degraded forest land; b) reduction of crops lost due to climate change impacts (floods and landslides); c) restoration of 101,000 ha of forest and grassland vegetation; d) creation of 25,000 ha of new forest and grassland vegetation; e) rehabilitation of 94,000 ha of degraded forest land; f) reduction in the loss of 6,000 ha of grassland and 150 freshwater sites; landslides and flood protection of 2,500 ha for forested areas; g) creation of 37,000 ha of new forest and grassland vegetation; h) restoration of 100 ha of community forest land and 100 ha of community grassland; and construction of 70 km of long rural road providing access to the project area. The project will also provide livelihoods for 25,000 households through fish ponds. In addition, there will be 34,512 ha paddy and 69,743 ha wheat lands under climate resilient practices, and stress tolerant varieties of crops will be introduced to reduce the vulnerability of communities to the rate of abandoned agricultural land). On the climate governance front, climate change measures will be included in at least 80% of GRB’s policies and strategies. The project will also ensure that 150,000 households (60%) and disadvantaged group aware of the climate threats and related appropriate responses; 50,000 households participating in forest restoration and 66,000 Community Forest User Group (CFUG) members (women 60%) participating in ecosystem management.

# PROJECT PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Climate change and context (max: 1000 words, approximately two pages)

### Climate rationale

#### Climate change predictions in Nepal

- **Temperature:** The temperature varies from cool summers (7°C) and seven winters (under-zero) in the north (Himalayas) to tropical summers (exceeding 37°C) and mild winters (-7 to -2°C) in the south (Terai). Nepal has five seasons: summer, monsoon, autumn, winter, and spring.
- **Precipitation:** The precipitation varies from 100 mm/year in the western Terai to over 4500 mm/year in the eastern Terai. It ranges from Central Asia in winter and forms the northern boundary of the monsoon wind patterns. Eighty per cent of all the rain falls during the monsoon season (June–September). The rainfall varies from 300 mm in Mustang to 3,454 mm in Phaphara (Kaski). The winter months are dry and all regions receive less than 50 mm/year.
- **In recent study:** A recent study published by the Ministry of Forests and Environment (2019), it has predicted that both precipitation and temperature will increase over time.

#### References

- **Reference source:** Reference source note: The data used in this document are based on average temperature and precipitation in the period 1956–2000. With the predicted changes in temperature and precipitation, the South Asia region is expected to be most affected by climate change.
- **South Asia sub-regions with high proportion of vulnerable species:** The South Asia region has the highest proportion of 'vulnerable' species of all the Asia sub-regions.

### MOFE (2019) has recently conducted detailed analysis of climate change scenarios for Nepal for the medium- and long-term periods in the context of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP)

#### Climate change projections

- **Temperature:** The temperature is projected to increase by 3.7°C for RCP 4.5 and 6.4°C for RCP 8.5 with respect to the reference period by the end of this century.
- **Precipitation:** The precipitation is projected to decrease in the medium-term period (2010–2049) and increase in the long-term period (2050–2100). The precipitation is expected to increase by 2.3% and 5.8% respectively for RCP 4.5 and 8.5.

#### References

- **Reference source:** Reference source note: The data used in this document are based on average temperature and precipitation at RCP 4.5 and 8.5 for a period from 2016–2049 and 2036–2065.

## Total precipitation on an average annual scale is slightly increasing (less than +5%) across the GRB districts, however, intensity of precipitation is increasing as indicated by increase in extreme wet days by 21-34%, whereas occurrence of dry spells is also increasing. The number of rainy days has decreased in all regions except Terai and other activities like in Mountains, and increasing in cumulative dry (CD) C, with a reduction in the number of rainy days.

### Most studies have shown that climate patterns in the GRB have already changed and is predicted to change significantly in the future. The World Bank, information from the Department of Hydrology and Meteorology (DhM), and documented climate change scenarios for Nepal are used as sources of summary of climate change data, as follows:

#### Temperature
- Mean annual temperatures are projected to increase between 1.3°C by the 2050s and 1.8-3.9°C by the end of this century with this warming expected to occur more rapidly during the summer months.
- Both average mean and max temperatures are projected to increase.
- The number of hot days is expected to double by the 2060s with reference period (1975–1999)!

#### Precipitation
- Monsoon precipitation increases by 2% in 2030, by 2050 and 2060, and by 20% if by 2080.
- The number of wet days is expected to increase by 10%, exacerbating flooding and landslides conditions.
- Precipitation in highlands decreases by 9% (5%), exacerbating droughts.

#### Exposure
- Expected to increase approximately by 7% in the near future (2016–2043) under RCP 8.5.
- Expected to decrease by about 10% for the far future (2044–2100), with more precipitation falling than the lower basins at lower elevations. Higher evaporation might lead to decline in the natural grassland coverage and the grass yield in the upper part of the Kali Gandaki basin.

**References:**
- DhM, 2017; Observed Climate Trend Analysis of Nepal (1971–2016). Department of Hydrology and Meteorology. Kathmandu.
- Climate Change Scenarios for Nepal. Ministry of Forests and Soil Conservation, 2018.
- World Bank. (2017) Climate change country profile for Nepal. Retrieved 17 March 2019 from https://www.worldbank.org/en/topic/climatechange/overview

## Climate change problems and adaptation needs to be addressed

The projected increase in precipitation will lead to an increase in the magnitude of extreme events such as winter droughts, flash floods and landslides. Increases in monsoon rainfall will lead to the salination of soils which increases the cost of irrigation and reduces yields. The increased frequency of extreme weather events will affect communities, incuring high recovery costs, straining limited financial resources and placing fragile development gains at risk.

The projected increase in temperature is expected to have a number of adverse effects on agriculture (see Annex B for summary of consultation) who noted increased frequency of landslides and increased volume of soil erosion; increases in the intensity and duration of droughts; increased evaporation rates leading to reduced water availability resulting in drying out of water sources and wells; increased heat affecting human and livestock health; increased pest attack on crops; increase in the number of weeds; increased incidence of plant diseases; reduction in agricultural production; and appearance of new insects (mojokotias) and vectors (culex pipiens) at higher altitudes. All these will have a negative impact on food security, which is already a major concern for many households as their livelihoods are being threatened[2]. These experiences are projected to be exacerbated with climate change more often.

## Baseline information

### For community resilience outcome
The current agricultural and livestock management systems are highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The majority of the population in the study area is smallholder farmers and their livelihoods are based on subsistence agriculture. Households grow limited varieties of crops, which are mostly rainfed. The average household size in the study area is 5.4 people. The per capita income of the region has been low for many years, with an average annual growth rate of 3.8% over the last decade (World Bank, 2016). The average annual GDP growth rate was 7.9% (31).

### For ecosystem resilience outcome
The forest area in the study area is dominated by Mangalsi. The area of forest has been increasing in the Mid Hills region due to afforestation and reforestation activities (e.g., by Manaslu). The focus on climate adaptation, Almost 72.4 per cent of households solely depend on forests to fulfill their household energy needs. This is because they are unable to afford other sources of fuel such as kerosene or LPG. Focus on rangeland management remains ignotic. ## Context

### Nepal is highly vulnerable to climate change and its impacts.

In 2017 Nepal was ranked in the top of world risks associated with climate change. The effects of climate change have adverse impacts on people, natural ecosystems and agricultural land, and the ecosystem services they provide. Climate change, in conjunction with other factors such as population growth, urbanization, deforestation, and overgrazing, threatens the GRI's biodiversity, agriculture productivity, and increases risks to the livelihoods, health, and well-being of vulnerable groups.

### Changes in precipitation will be the primary source of vulnerability to climate change in Nepal. The changes in water availability will have significant implications for biodiversity and forestry, agriculture, increase in average annual rainfall by 0.7 mm/decade is the most likely scenario, with an upper envelope of 15 mm/decade.

### In addition to precipitation impacts, increasing temperatures could increase the rate of glacial melt from the Himalayas, leading to increased flooding in low-lying areas and riverine communities, particularly villages and areas by breaking rivers and other structures. This will also have implications on the timing of water availability for irrigation.

### The lower overall per capita income (USD 446), high poverty index (31%), low human development index (0.482) and high vulnerability to climate change make Nepal one of the most vulnerable countries in Asia, making it difficult for people to search for better employment prospects elsewhere. Furthermore, due to migration of male-most members, the entire responsibility falls on women and children who are left behind.

### The outmigration of the male-labour force and declining agricultural productivity of rural agriculture in rural areas has led to a decline in the number of farmers and their families. This has resulted in an increase in female-headed households in agriculture leading to women and their dependents (children and elderly people) living in rural areas depending on food aid.

### The project plan is to intervene in the agricultural improvements to retain the labor force in the basin. Besides, it will also address the vulnerability of the population by providing them with livelihood opportunities through incorporating climate change issues with adaptive measures based on the outcomes of this project. Success of this project will be measured against the following indicators.

### Target regions/areas: This target region is designed for improving climatic resilience of vulnerable communities and improving their livelihoods in the face of climate change impacts. The target areas are 12 Provinces (see Annex 15 [Map] for details on the geographical location). These include 4, 5 in Province 3; and 3 in Province 5) (See Annex 15 [Map] for details on the geographical location).

### A major river basin in Nepal is the Gandaki River Basin. The Gandaki River Basin has been severely affected by snow fed rivers. Changes in climate have impacted these rivers and their basins. The Gandaki River Basin was selected as a pilot project area because of its vulnerability to climate change impacts, particularly from the Himalayan rivers; USAID supported PANI project is working on the Karnali and Mahakali Rivers, FAO is developing proposal on the Koshi River.

### Care has been taken in the design of the project to ensure no overlap between FP 118 FAO Nepal and the GRB project. The project will cover the same communities as well. All FAO PANI project, project's input will cover the basin.

### Demographics

The population census of 2011 estimated a total population of 5,131,932 (1,712,558 households) in the GGRB which is 19.4 per cent of the total population of Nepal. The GRB is home to 2,427,854 indigenous people and 60,000 non-indigenous people. Indigenous peoples are referred to as Janajati and 59 Janajati groups have been officially recognised by the government. The average size of households in the GGRB is 5.1 persons per household compared with an average of 4.8 persons in GRB. Ethnic groups have lower literacy levels (68.03 per cent) compared to the national average of 69.73 per cent.

### Economy

Agriculture is the mainstay of the Nepalese economy. It contributes almost one-third of total GDP and provides employment for about two-thirds of the population. Agriculture remains a major export earner, providing revenues for the country. However, insufficient production of key crops (such as rice and maize) to meet domestic demand has led to importation of these commodities. The agricultural sector in the GGRB has seen some recent years has been associated with farming practices highly dependent on weather conditions, insufficient irrigation facilities, lack of access to markets, and poor infrastructure. This is reflected by a high level of crop failure and abandonment.23 Agriculture is dominated by small-scale farms of less than two hectares (ha).24 The GGRB is one of the most vulnerable areas in Nepal with respect to climate change. Livelihoods of livestock for people in the GRB, around 74 percent of the population, mostly marginal farmers, still depend on agriculture and animal husbandry.25 The Gross Domestic Product (GDP) comes from this sector.

A brief description of agriculture in different regions is provided below:

**Mountain region:** The farming system in the region is mainly potato, maize and millet based. The predominant agricultural practices are rainfed cultivation of potatoes, maize, millet and wheat. Other crops include rice-fed crop cultivation (e.g., potato, banana, buckwheat), and temperate fruits (e.g., apple and pear). Around 29% of the area is under irrigation. This region has a high population density with a large number of people. High population migration rates to lower altitude areas, scarce road infrastructure, and minimal educational opportunities.

**Mid-hill region:** The farming system is mainly maize and wheat based. Crops are grown in upland terraces and irrigated fields. Major crops include rice (main crop), maize, millet, wheat, chickpea, lentil, oilseed, mustard, and temperate fruits, particularly citrus, are the main crops grown in land bar, whereas rice and wheat are common in land dairy. Roads and commercial vegetable production are rapidly growing in places nearby market centers in this zone.

**Swath and Terai region:** The Swathi and Terai regions are the majority of upcountry in the country and a key corridor for trade between India and Nepal. The farming system is mainly rice based, with wheat as an important secondary crop. The highly fertile soils allow for the cultivation of rice (the main crop) in the region), wheat, chickpea, lentil, oilseed, mustard, and temperate fruits are also grown here. Rice is a major cash crop in this region. In common, Farmers in this zone also rely on livestock production - mainly cattle, goats, and buffalos.

### **41**


#### **National Adaptation Programmes of Action (NAPAs)**

The NAPA is a country-led process for developing and implementing a National Programme of Action to adapt to climate change. It is a participatory process in which all stakeholders are involved in the development, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of the programme. The NAPA is a strategic document that sets out the priorities and actions required by countries to address the adverse impacts of climate change.

The NAPA is developed through an iterative process involving government officials, civil society organizations, private sector representatives, and other stakeholders. It is based on the country's national development plan and takes into account the specific needs and vulnerabilities of different sectors and communities within the country.


#### **Green Climate Fund (GCF) Projects in Nepal**

The GCF has supported several projects in Nepal that are either currently under implementation or just recently completed in the GRB from which many lessons and models can be customised or replicated for this basin. These projects include: Forest Conservation, Community Based Natural Resource Management, Livestock Extension, Soil and Water Conservation, Forest management, and National parks and wildlife conservation. The GCF has also supported several projects that are currently under implementation (see Annex 2a Section 4 Baseline Efforts for details). The important projects in the GRB include: GEF supported Drought Resilient Agriculture Project (DRAP), Climate Change Adaptation Project (CCAP), and the Nepal Himalayan Water Resources Management Project (NHWMP). UNDP implemented Supporting Developing Countries to Integrate the Agricultural Sector into National Strategies and Plans for Climate Change Mitigation and Adaptation (SADC) project, which is currently under implementation. The GEF supported project on Reducing Vulnerability and Increasing Adaptive Capacity to Respond to the Impacts of Climate Change and Variability in the Himalayan Agriculture Sector; and the ADB supported Himalayan High Mountain Agribusiness and Livelihood Improvement.


From several projects recently completed or under implementation in the GRB, a summary of projects is provided for reference. These are summarised as follows:

* **Himalayan Water Resources Management Project (NHWMP):** This project contributes significantly to preventing landslides and protecting watersheds that also contribute to reduce climate shocks.
* **DRAP:** This project uses a market-based solution to climate change adaptation in mountainous regions to enhance ecosystem services and agricultural production.
* **CCAP:** This project is based on an integrated water resource and ecosystem-based approach.

50. In order to enhance adaptive capacity and reduce vulnerability to climate change by bridging these gaps, there are several adaptation options that can be considered. These include: i) Awareness raising; ii) Capacity building; iii) Research and development needs. There are five types of barriers identified in the GRS which impede climate change adaptation, listed below:

* Limited technical and financial capacity for communities to adapt existing livelihood practices in agriculture, livestock production, fisheries and forestry.
* Lack of awareness among stakeholders about climate change including increasing frequency and intensity of rain, floods, landslides and droughts. As women, Dalit and poor ethnic groups are dependent on forests, agriculture and water resources for their livelihoods, they have a greater vulnerability to the impacts of climate change. This barrier is based on inadequate data and information to inform good decision making; lack/inadequate availability of adaptation technologies and services; lack of awareness about climate change and its impact.
* Limited capacity in the translation of technology into practice (education, skills). This limited capacity also affects the ability of stakeholders to adapt to climate change. The barriers are: i) Lack of technical knowledge; ii) Lack of access to information; iii) Lack of appropriate financing and risks perceived by investors related to climate variations that do not ensure a return on investment.
* Limited access to resources for adaptation, including drought and flood tolerant varieties, or nature-based solutions which will improve the access to water resources. This barrier is based on lack of awareness about climate change and its impact; lack of appropriate financing and risks perceived by investors related to climate variations that do not ensure a return on investment.
* Limited management techniques as this resource is mostly affected by climate change with substantial impacts on economic activities, including livestock production, agriculture and fisheries. This barrier is based on inadequate data and information to inform good decision making; lack/inadequate availability of adaptation technologies and services.

One of the key barriers of access to relevant climate resilient technology is the current low level of income, which leads to limited access to resources for adaptation, including drought and flood tolerant varieties or nature-based solutions of land.

### B

**54. Limited participation in decision-making process:** Women, indigenous peoples, Dalits, ethnic groups, disabled and others often have limited access to decision making. This affects how these communities and groups are able to adapt to climate change and its related events. It is therefore critical to achieve resilience at the basin level by ensuring that women, indigenous peoples, Dalits, ethnic groups, disabled and other vulnerable groups having access to data and information as any other stakeholders.

**56. Lack of market and financial access:** Farmers are highly vulnerable to climate change and its related events. This has a direct negative effect on commercial activities in the region and livelihoods, mainly the ones that are critical for poverty reduction. The project will address this by mainstreaming climate resilience into existing markets and transport systems, which need to be made more resilient to climate change. The GRB project will remove the risk of climate change-related market failures through the development of a robust value chain, including improved transport networks, markets and living areas. These solutions will also protect natural resources such as water, preserving access for future generations.

**57. Project design:** The project has designed and adopted the "Mainstreaming and operationalizing a river basin approach sustainably in Nepal" (GRB) to address climate change impacts on agriculture and livelihoods in the Gandaki River Basin". The Gandaki River Basin is being used as a model to showcase how climate resilient development can be achieved. This project has been designed based on the experience of the GRB project, which was initiated by GIZ in 2013. The project design is based on a multi-stakeholder approach and a wide level that cuts across political/administrative boundaries. The Government of Nepal's commitment to establishing a river basin approach for sustainable development has been recognized at national and sub-national levels, and the project's approach to the Gandaki Basin offers a model for scaling-up this approach in other basins as well.

**58. Project approach:** The project approach is to enable riparian states to mainstream climate change adaptation and implementation of development in collaboration with its upstream-downstream stakeholders. The project serves as a vehicle for GIZ's experience in the Gandaki River Basin, which has been recognized by the Government of Nepal, which was approved by the high-level committee to manage all watersheds in Nepal following its establishment by the National Water Act 2015. The project will be implemented through a phased approach and in a harmonized manner, without significant evidence of progress at a river basin scale, let alone nationally.

## B

### 60. ADGN in relation to the paradigm shift objective, clearly recognises the opportunity for realising transformative change in shifting from a piecemeal approach in addressing management and governance of the GRB to a strategic integrated river basin model for enhancing the adaptive capacity of communities. The project will support the Government with the establishment of the Gandaki River Basin Office for developing this strategic framework within which would be nested the project's ecosystem based adaptation initiatives. This would ensure that the project activities are in line with the national and provincial level plans, as well as the local plans with the relevant provincial governments in the Gandaki River Basin. At each level, the plans will integrate the ecological, social and economic dimensions of the basin.

### 61. The project will support the Government with the establishment of the Gandaki River Basin Office for developing this strategic framework within which would be nested the project's ecosystem based adaptation initiatives. This would ensure that the project activities are in line with the national and provincial level plans, as well as the local plans with the relevant provincial governments in the Gandaki River Basin. At each level, the plans will integrate the ecological, social and economic dimensions of the basin.

### 62. This will address in correcting the prevailing political/administrative boundary-based designing and implementing approach, where the upstream and downstream parts of the river basins are disconnected and thus are not oblivious to the impacts of climate change on their respective communities and are not able to implement adaptation measures. As a result, the project activities in the upstreams are impacting communities in the downstreams. The major problems in the basin include flooding, inundation, sedimentation, salinization of agricultural lands and worsening water quality.

### 63. The Gandaki River Basin Management Framework will encompass a larger landscape comprising several political units (districts) and municipalities with common goals and objectives for river basin management and water resource management goals and objectives. In river basins, solutions to address climate change threats can only be done in an integrated manner where the upstream and downstream parts of the river basins are managed together in a coordinated and inclusive manner. In the new paradigm—river basin approach, an holistic framework will be developed for the Gandaki River Basin which will bring all stakeholders from upstreams together and plan for the GRB. All related districts (15) and municipalities (151) are to come together and plan for sub-basins. As all communities in the landscape are involved, it will ensure the resilience of the entire basin thereby

#### B

**64. The river basin approach makes the coordination between the states and municipalities easier and effective. Additionally, it will allow for a more efficient use of resources by involving local communities in conservation activities by using nature-based solutions such as bioengineering and natural infrastructure. This will ensure that ecosystem services are maintained and restored while also providing economic benefits to the communities. The river basin approach has been well documented from various experiences that ecosystem-based approaches, including natural infrastructures, can provide a cost-effective solution for climate change adaptation in water resources management. For example, watershed restoration can protect sources of drinking water and reduce the need for subsequent investments in infrastructure. In addition, it will also help to improve the quality of water by reducing pollution as a result of more investments, as an effect of an enabling environment that will do-the-actor and attract investors in the basin. This will provide communities with services that protect farmers (through financing and insurance) and, in turn, lessen the losses due to climate change.**

#### 65. The Project Steering Committee will be a strategic advisory body for developing the Gangetic River Basin Management Strategy Framework. It will be chaired by the Secretary, Ministry of Forests and Environment and governed by the steering committee rules and procedures. The project will also work with various government agencies at both the federal level and the provincial level to implement the Prime Minister's developed programme and initiatives. The project will also engage with other stakeholders such as private sector representatives from private or public sectors, civil society organizations, and local communities. Representatives will include the Department of Hydrology and Meteorology, Federations of Chambers of Commerce and industry, and other relevant stakeholders in the basin. The project will also work on cross-sectoral systems challenges such as addressing the Water-Food-Energy nexus that would involve Ministry of Agriculture, Ministry of Energy, Water Resources and Irrigation, Nepal Planning Commission, etc.

#### 66. There are seven watershed management plans at provincial level. These watershed management plans will guide the preparation of provincial joint planning documents (JPDs) for each province. The JPDs will be prepared by the communities for joint planning and implementation of climate change activities. As the joint plan is a joint activity, it will involve all stakeholders in the basin. The provincial joint planning document will also be a linkage between the watershed management plans of the provinces and the sub-watershed management plans at the district level. This will ensure that there are no duplications of activities. Similarly, a provincial joint planning document is also developed at the time of joint planning; hence the chances of duplication of resources is avoided, plus this ensures the monitoring of the activity at the time of implementation.

### Theory of change

#### 67. Given the climate change problems, adaptation needs, barriers for adaptation, and the interventions required to overcome these barriers, the project proposes a river basin approach that will be adopted with the national as well as local level stakeholders that the project should adopt the river basin approach and work in three broad thematic areas; namely, (i) climate resilience infrastructure, (ii) community resilience, and (iii) governance for leading to climate governance. The project will also focus on the following distinct improvements in the climate resilience of communities and ecosystems within the GRB.

#### 68. In order to ensure that the project is aligned with national priorities and that climate risk reduction remains government priority at least for the project period and communities are willing to participate and support in project implementation, the project will also work closely with the Ministry of Education and Training. The public schools and training centres will include climate change issues in their regular training programmes on climate change adaptation and mitigation.

#### 69. The project proposes six activities for community resilience, five activities for ecosystem resilience, and 18 activities for governance leading to climate governance. These activities are grouped into three components (Component 1), two components for climate resilience infrastructure (Component 2), and three outcomes for governance leading to climate governance (Component3).

#### 70. The project proposes that the river basin approach will be a key component of the overall system which will ensure the whole basin is climate resilient (including built and natural infrastructure as well as livelihoods) and attract more investments in lower risks. The approach will pave the way for replication in many other basins in

# Project description (max. 2000 words, approximately 4 pages)

## The Project

This is a climate change adaptation project designed to improve the climate resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems in Nepal under Green Climate Fund. The project is designed to shift planning and implementation of climate change adaptation measures from using political boundaries such as districts and municipalities as a basis for decision-making towards ecosystem-based approaches that are more effective at identifying, assessing, and implementing climate change adaptation measures linking all the impacted communities in the upstream and downstream areas.

As Nepal is in political transition and is just experiencing the federal system of governance, the administrative structure is still evolving. The country has limited capacity to respond to climate change because its adaptive capacity for climate change is weak. In addition, limited market mechanisms, structures and capacity mean vulnerable people have limited access to resources that could help them adapt to climate change. This project will focus on improving agricultural land use to provide ecosystem goods and services. Limited knowledge and awareness of the effects and impacts of climate change amongst vulnerable people in their preparedness to respond to it has made them more resilient.

### Project components

The GRB project is designed with three components:

* **Component 1: Ecosystem resilience:** Building climate resilience of the livelihoods of communities in vulnerable areas of the GRB, through improvements in agricultural production, forest based enterprises, and integration of climate change adaptation measures into existing policies and plans. The component aims to implement improved actions for coping with extreme climate events, and measures for increasing water availability and water use efficiency.

* **Component 2: Ecosystem resilience:** Building climate resilience of the vulnerable ecosystems in the GRB through improvements in forest-based enterprises, biodiversity conservation, and ecosystem services. The component aims to implement conservation and restoration actions, forest landscape restoration and biodiversity conservation activities.

* **Component 3: Climate governance - Strengthening institutional frameworks and governance of climate change adaptation measures:** This component will strengthen the institutional framework at national level for implementing climate change adaptation measures through policy development, institutional mechanisms for planning, restoration, monitoring, and maintenance of ecosystems, incorporating ecosystem-based approaches into government policies and plans, and establishing a river basin knowledge management system.

### Outcomes and Outputs

* **Ecosystem resilience:** All three components have an outcome which will be contributed to by two to three project outputs.
* **Component 1: Community resilience**
    * Outcome 1: Climate resilience of livelihoods of the vulnerable communities through adapting to climate change sustainably.

## In order to achieve this outcome, the project will enhance the ability of communities to utilize available resources sustainably and, to respond to, withstand, and recover from the adverse impacts of climate on their livelihoods. The overall objective is that the capacity of vulnerable communities in the GRB to adapt to climate change must be strengthened.

* **Agriculture:** Agriculture is highly affected by changing precipitation patterns and the subsequent impact on water supplies. Agriculture in the GRB is mostly based on rain-fed systems with only about 35 per cent of agricultural land under irrigation. The majority of households are small farmers who depend heavily on rainfall, making water availability due to climate change. The low level of irrigation leads to high risk of crop failure due to drought and low yields. This project will focus on improving the resilience of vulnerable communities in terms of water sources.

* **Most households are smallholders and their livelihoods are based on semi-subsistence agriculture. Households grow limited varieties of traditional crops/vegetables such as paddy, wheat, barley, maize, etc., with an increasing trend towards cash crops like tomatoes, potatoes, onions, garlic, etc. The majority of these crops become increasingly vulnerable, with shifts in agricultural production zones, loss of local varieties such as 'Mandi', and reduction of quality, 'muscle' or 'meat' etc., and reduction of agricultural productivity leading to increasing food insecurity.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 25 OF 80

## Landslides and undercutting by rivers have destroyed productive agricultural land in the mountain and high hill districts. Likewise, flooding and increased sedimentation has reduced the productive capacity of agricultural land throughout the country. In the trans-Himalayan region alone, landslides and floods caused an estimated loss of 648 ha of agricultural crop land were lost between 1971 and 2007. Cultivation in sloping lands leads to a high risk of soil erosion.

### Decreased precipitation and snowfall in the trans-Himalayan region has resulted in a decline in the productivity of pastoral livestock, which is the main source of income for many households. This has led to increased vulnerability effects on communities that practice transhumance grazing. As a result, transhumance herders are migrating to urban centers where they have no access to land and are unable to sustain their livelihoods.

### The decreasing productivity and production in the climate extreme years (drought, flood and inundation) have led to outmigration from hill and mountain districts with farmers abandoning their agricultural land. The rate of land abandonment is increasing due to lack of alternative income sources for these communities.

This outcome will be achieved through the achievement of the following two outputs:

Output 1.2: Interventions to improve water availability and water use efficiency from irrigation systems and improved water storage

### Output 1.2.1: Interventions to cope with climate change for coping with extreme events

#### 83. The project has recognised that communities in GRB have been practicing various agroforestry systems using indigenous trees, shrubs and grasses which are drought tolerant and can be used as a source of income and socially desirable. Agroforestry has also proven to be a best option for taking back unused and underutilized land and converting it into productive land. The project will promote the use of agroforestry systems as a result of decreasing productivity due to climate change. Agroforestry will create spatial and structural complexity in the landscape, which will provide food and agricultural products through mixtures of tree crops and food crops in agricultural land.

#### 84. Degrade agricultural lands damaged by landslides and floods also need to be restored for enhancing resilience of livelihoods. The project will use a bioengineering approach that constitutes the use of barnyard check dams, popularly known as "chaur" or "khati".

## B

### Component 2: Ecosystem resilience

#### Outcome 2c: Strengthen climate resilience of ecosystems

- **Monocultures:** Monocultures, such as plantations and grasslands, are vital particularly for land stabilization, water and nutrient cycling, and provisioning of ecosystem goods including fuelwood, forage, and fruits. These ecosystems are also important for the maintenance of biodiversity and provision of other ecosystem services.
- **Forest-dependent species:** Large numbers of forest dependent people are organized into more than 8,000 Community Forest User Groups (CFUGs) in Nepal. CFUGs are responsible for managing forests on behalf of their members who own the land. The majority of these groups are located within the protected areas of forest.
- **Impact of climate change:** Within these groups, forest management is generally lacking and there is an absence of focus on issues related to climate change. Climate change has serious effects on rangelands including increases in pests, drying of water sources, and loss of biodiversity. The impact of climate change on forests is also significant as the distribution of many tree species becomes fragmented and disappear from Chitwan, Tanahu and Nawalparasi districts.
- **Subtropical broadleaf forests:** Subtropical broadleaf forests are found in the Terai region of Nepal. These forests provide important habitat for birds, mammals, reptiles, amphibians, insects, and other forest-dependent wildlife species.

#### Outcome 2d: Natural ecosystem restoration

- **Invasive plant species:** Invasive plant species including Mikania, Lantana, Eupatorium, Ageratum and Echinacea are also seen posing a threat to biodiversity. In Outcome 2c, it is therefore addressed to these issues by stakeholders confirmed during consultation that invasive plants have become a major problem in the Terai region.
- **This outcome will be achieved through the achievement of the following two outputs:**

**Outcome 2f: Natural ecosystem restoration based actions/aimed interventions for reducing impacts of landslides and floods**

#### B

**Outcome 1: Strengthened climate governance and institutional framework to sustain climate resilience**

1. **Strengthening climate governance**: The project will strengthen the climate governance structure and institutional arrangements.
   - For effective implementation of adaptation measures, the climate change adaptation measures need to be mainstreamed in the most significant federal and provincial policies and plans to guide policy.

#### Outcome 2: Technical capacity of GRB communities enhanced in maintaining and supporting climate resilient ecosystems

1. The project has identified that the restored ecosystems need to be maintained for sustained supply of ecosystem goods and services.
   - This includes a lack of skills in dealing with climate change risk and vulnerability assessment methods, as well as limited access to information on climate change adaptation technologies.

2. To address these challenges, the project will:
   * Build the technical capacity of user groups to advance natural-based solutions on ecosystem restoration, management, and conservation.
   * Build technical capacity of stakeholders and support for local institutions for stewardship and sustainability will assist in maintaining restored ecosystems.
   * Build technical capacity of stakeholders and support for local institutions for stewardship and sustainability will assist in maintaining restored ecosystems.

3. A. As there is limited human and technical capacity with the GRB communities in this project, the need to create a new Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) that supports future interventions on agriforestry, forestry, wetlands, and agroforestry systems in vulnerable communities will be enhanced.
   - This will help to enhance capacity of GRBs and NGOs in vulnerable communities in maintaining climate resilient ecosystems.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 23 OF 80

## Local government plans. Likewise, climate change adaptation measures need to be included in local policies and plans as well, to create the enabling condition for interventions on the ground. It becomes necessary that key institutions be supported to ensure that they are able to integrate climate change considerations into their planning and decision making, making the climate change patterns, climate risks and vulnerability information and knowledge sharing existing within the communities available to them. This is a pre-requisite for achieving Outcome 3 in this project.

This outcome will be achieved through the achievement of the following three outputs:

### Output 3.1: Community-based mechanism for planning, restoration, monitoring, and maintenance of ecosystem services

106. This output is to ensure that the project adopts appropriate processes of planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of climate change adaptation measures in the GBR. It is important to note that this will be a phased approach and should be undertaken at the planning stage of a restoration, with all key stakeholders. Ecosystem restoration adapts the principles of ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) by focusing on the maintenance of ecological processes and services. There is need for a technical assistance for community based planning and development of this specific management structure and to ensure that it is integrated into the existing governance structures. This will be achieved through monitoring and maintenance programmes through the local and regional management structures to maintain restored ecosystems and their associated benefits. The project will also support the establishment of a network of users of the ecosystems in target areas, and need to link upstream and downstream vulnerable communities through climate information sharing.

### Output 3.2: Ecosystem-based climate change adaptation approaches incorporated into government policies & plans

107. This output is to ensure that ecosystem-based climate change adaptation approaches be incorporated into government plans and policies. Specifically, and in line with the national policy on climate change (NPPC), it will be ensured that climate change adaptation needs are a focus of this output. In order to justify the need to allocate adequate budget for ecosystem management, there is a need to establish an integrated plan for ecosystem-based climate change adaptation at all levels. This will be done in the new federal governance system, all local government bodies are required to prepare an Integrated Development Plan (IDP) that includes climate change adaptation measures and EBA into their Integrated Development Plans.

### Output 3.3: Knowledge management established for climate resilient Water Resource Management

108. The project has identified that a National and GBR level system for collating data and information on global best practices, lessons learned, evidence from the field and scientific knowledge on ecosystem- and community-based adaptation (EBA) is required to support the implementation of this project. This will be achieved through the development and associated mobile phone application to facilitate access to updated data for tracking changes in ecological and socio-economic conditions. The project will also establish a national level platform for sharing information, and to establish climate change adaptation knowledge sharing and learning structures within key clusters to facilitate capacity building.

**Rationale for the selection of interventions**

109. It is evident that many livelihoods and ecosystems of local communities in the GBR are subjected to a range of climate change and non-climate factors. It has also been shown that many of the non- climate factors have been exacerbated by climate change, and that these factors have created new challenges which have emerged from past and ongoing projects involving ecosystem restoration. However, crucial gaps, barriers and constraints exist in terms of the capacity of local communities to adapt to these changes. These are further highlighted in Section 8.2). It is increasingly evident that such pitfalls - which frequently require through engagement with local communities - can be avoided by ensuring that climate change adaptation considerations are a priority for local communities to adapt to climate change. Therefore, it was concluded that this project should adopt an ecosystem-based approach (EBA) as the primary strategy for addressing these challenges and barriers faced by the most vulnerable GBR communities to climate change. Based on review of literature on climate change policies and practices in the region, a set of interventions were developed. These are further described below.

110. The proposed interventions basically deal with improving the resilience of agriculture and other natural resources through the following: (i) Improving water availability for agricultural crops by introducing flood and drought tolerant variety of crops; improving the water availability for the agricultural crops by creating small irrigation channels and water harvest techniques in the dry winter season and maintaining soil

## Output-wise Activities

### Outcomes

#### Outcome 1: Climate resilient agricultural and livelihood improvement actions implemented for coping with adverse effects of climate change

##### 1.1. Establish climate resilient agroforestry practices

- **Selection criterion/Methodology**
    - Best agriforestry option will be selected on the basis of:
        * Suitability in the changing climatic condition/particular location
        * Evidence of local existence and good for soil conservation
        * More productive than conventional agricultural land
        * Suitable to bring back the abandoned agricultural land into production
        * Low labour requiring
        * Quick establishment
    - There will be at least 5,000 households selected for agriforestry plantation (100 ha of land) in the Terai and Inner Terai. Out of this, 2 ropani* land will be selected. On the whole, there will be 500 hectares of land under agroforestry development.
    - There will be a training manual developed for capacity building of the farmers on climate resilient agricultural practices and agroforestry development. The manual will be approved by the PMU.

##### 1.2. Construct small nature-based structures (perch dams) with plantations of grass and trees

- **Selection criterion/Methodology**
    - Perch dams will be constructed by the landowners in the uplands and floods in the down-streams.
    - The dam sites will be selected on the basis of soil type, slope gradient, and 15 dams with plantation of trees and grasses will be constructed. Community participation will be ensured to decide the type of construction.
    - However, it is important that the type of trees and grasses to be planted on the dams

##### 1.3. Promote drought and flood tolerant varieties of wheat and rice

- **Selection criterion/Methodology**
    - Drought and flood tolerant varieties will be identified with National Rice Research Programme and drought tolerant varieties of wheat will be identified in consultation with the Department of Agriculture, Nepal Agricultural Research Council.
    - The seeds for the varieties will be provided by the Agriculture Development Section of the Provincial Government and the Agriculture Development Section of the Local government and
    - Seeds of the tolerant varieties for demonstration will be provided to Project will promote private sector Agrovet to supply seed for commercial cultivation on cost basis.

* 1 ropani = 500 square meter

## Output 1.2: Interventions for water availability and use efficiency, improved water distribution systems and improved water quality to be implemented

### 1.2.1: Reconcile of the Water Model for Entire Ganga River Basin

#### 1.2.2: Construct small scale irrigation systems with community participation

With the technical support from IIMR and the Nepal Department of Hydrology and Meteorology, a separate model will be reconciled for the entire basin of the Ganga river.

The model will be used to analyze water balance, hydrological data etc., and will be used as a tool for water resource assessment tool (WRAT).

Consultation with local communities for small scale irrigation schemes will be done in consultation with the Provincial and local governments. Priority will be given to those villages where there is a possibility of promoting surface irrigation through pocket.

Pocket for the small irrigation will be selected on the basis of number of households, it should be at least 100 households with at least 100 rooftop land (one household one roof top).

Pocket area for micro scheme will be smaller having potential to induce water harvesting and rainwater harvesting (5-7 rooftops per household).

The main support to establish the scheme will be Water User's Group (WUG) to ensure operation and maintenance of the schemes.

WUG will be formed by the community members of the selected site. The WUG members will be capacitated by the project on the operation and maintenance of the conservation ponds and water resources.

### 1.2.3: Establish water harvesting systems (conservation pond, water reservoirs) and promote water use efficiency through drip and sprinkler irrigation and the use of waste water

Water harvesting systems will be done in consultation with the Provincial and local governments. Priority will be given to those villages where there is a possibility in the post monsoon and winter.

Selection of site for conservation pond or water reservoir will be based on community decision made by the WUGs. The WUG members will be capacitated by the project on the operation and maintenance of the conservation ponds and water resources.

### 1.2.4: Improve water availability through construction and maintenance of water holes in community grasslands

Selection of location for the construction of waterholes will be done in consultation with the Provincial and local governments.

The focus will be given to community grasslands with potential for water harvesting.

Waterholes will be constructed at 30 vulnerable community grasslands. The WUGs will be formed by the community and capacitated by the project for the operation and maintenance of the waterholes.

The WUG members will be capacitated by the project on the operation and maintenance of the conservation ponds and water resources

## Output 2: Natural ecosystem restoration based approach for reducing impacts of droughts and floods

### 2.1.1: Construct climate resistant green dams (15), non-diversion check dams (30) and diversion check dams (6) to be done in consultation with the Provincial and local governments.

Priority will be given for agricultural lands to protect them from flash floods.

In the selected locations, communities will form the user groups and they will plant shrubs and trees along the dams with the project support. User groups will also plant grasses and trees and grasses for plantation to establish 8 km long green belts.

- Location for the construction of structural support for rural roads will be determined in consultation with the Provincial and local governments.
- CFUGs will identify the degraded forest areas (2,500 ha) and decide the plantation sites along the rural roads (70 km).

- Community Forest Ecology Unit will be established in invaded community forests and community grasslands.

- Monitoring and evaluation plan will be developed to prevent re-invasion or biocharming.

- The field school model will be maintained in the local government level.

- The project will support 50 local governments selected by the provincial government to address climate change issues in their Integrated Development Plan (IDP).

## B

### 3.3.2 Capacitating Provincial governments of BC in creating and operating online platform and associated apps for climate change adaptation

#### 3.3.2.1 Capacitating Provincial governments of BC in creating and operating online platform and associated apps for climate change adaptation
The appropriate location for system establishment will be recommended by the coordination meeting of the Provincial government.
The developed system will be piloted and tested in 5 locations including provincial governments.
Establish the system.
The system will primarily provide climate data to the Environment Protection and Climate Change Council (EPCCC) and Provincial ministries for Agriculture, Cooperatives and Natural Resources.

#### 3.3.2.2 Developing and testing of apps
In collaboration with local governments, one model app will be identified and developed that can be used by provincial and local governments.
One App model will be identified and developed to address climate vulnerability to climate change and using that for decision making.
The project will capitalize the PCU/CFI 4.0 for the development of apps.
Developed Apps will be piloted in 15 sites and tested through regular monitoring.
The tested model will be mainstreamed in provincial government's regular programme.

#### 3.3.2.3 Generating baseline data
In collaboration with local governments, the project will generate baseline data and map of vulnerability, hazard sites, and services and facilities in communities based on risk profiles.
The generated data will be used to identify vulnerable areas and local governments will be capacitated to maintain such data.
For sustainability, the data generation and maintenance will be integrated into provincial government's disaster plans.

#### 3.3.2.4 Establish climate change adaptation knowledge sharing and learning structure within key clusters to facilitate planning and management
In collaboration with other knowledge clusters in the GRB, the project will establish a climate change adaptation learning structure (1 federal, 3 provincial and 151 local).

122. The project will be executed by the PMU under the guidance of the PSC. The composition of PMU will be as follows:

* **Member:** Chief Water Management Centre Province 4
* **Member:** Executive Director, NTNC
* **Member:** Programme Coordinator, IUCN Nepal
* **Member:** Project Officer, IUCN Nepal
* **Member Secretary:** Chief of Planning and Watershed Management Section, Department of Forests and Soil Conservation

* Be responsible for all financial matters including financial management, procurement and project disbursements.
* Oversee the disbursement to provincial and local government for the implementation of approved project activities (conditional grants).
* Ensure coordinated delivery of the agreed projects outputs and activities. It will coordinate all partners, stakeholders, and other relevant entities involved in the project. The PMU will also oversee, monitor, coordinate and consolidate monitoring, learning and knowledge products with the support of relevant experts involved in project implementation.
* Prepare annual plans and budget

135. DRM representative will be invited in the project steering committee (PSC) meeting. PSC will be a mechanism that coordinates all stakeholders involved in the project. The DRM coordination and monitoring mechanism is a continuation process of the government.

136. A high-level coordination committee known as National Action Committee (NADC) chaired by the Prime Minister formed a high level committee for managing all of Nepal's watersheds based on a river basin approach. This committee has been renamed as the National River Basin Authority (NRBA). The NRBA has now formed another coordination committee to execute the NADC agenda under the coordination of a National Project Steering Committee (NPSC) chaired by the Prime Minister. In addition, the government of Nepal has also established four major river basin offices in 2019 to manage the Koshi, Gandaki, Karnali, and Mahakali basins. The project will use this high level committee for coordination.

137. In order to ensure that the project results are sustainable in the long run, project has planned to mainstream and operationalize the river basin approach in the GRB with Gandaki River Basin Office recently established by the government of Nepal. This office is responsible for coordinating all activities related to well managed climate change information and adaptation technology centers.

138. DRM will be represented on the NRBA high level coordination committee and post project. DRM will also be represented in the Gandaki River Basin high level coordination committee and future committees upscaling river basin approach in other major river basins of Nepal. The project will also support the development of water resources production systems. Given the high level membership and federal jurisdiction of the committee and Gandaki river management office, it is expected that the project will have a significant role in mainstreaming and upscaling of the practices of the Gandaki Basin to other large river basins in Nepal even after the project.

148. Regarding other relevant experience, IUCN has extensive experience in deploying nature-based solutions to global climate change challenges. For example, IUCN has been involved with several projects of NepalMOFE. Peru and Uganda implemented a Flagship Ecosystem based Adaptation (EbA) project from 2013 to 2016. The project aimed at reducing the vulnerability of communities in the Andean region to the impacts of climate change using sustainable management, conservation and restoration of ecosystems, as part of overall local development strategies. ## B.5 Justification for GCF funding (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Need for GCF funding

167. The country’s budget has remained at a very low level in the last ten years with an average growth rate of 3.8 per cent and a loan of 0.77 per cent in 2015/16. Although agriculture is considered the backbone of the economy, it contributes only about 2.9 per cent per annum. The per capita GDP was USD 751 with an annual inflation rate of 7 per cent in 2016.

### Table 9: Nepal’s Economic Growth Rate 2006 to 2016

| Fiscal Year | 2006/07 | 2007/08 | 2008/09 | 2009/10 | 2010/11 | 2011/12 | 2012/13 | 2013/14 | 2014/15 | 2015/16 | Average |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Agriculture | -7.9 | -8.0 | -13.0 | -10.0 | -4.0 | 2.5 | 6.0 | 2.9 | 2.9 | 2.9 | 2.9 |
| Non Agriculture | 4.4 | 5.9 | 4.3 | 5.0 | 3.6 | 4.5 | 5.0 | 6.4 | 3.1 | 0.8 | -0.7 |
| Industry | 3.9 | 1.7 | -0.6 | 4.0 | 4.3 | 3.0 | 2.7 | 7.1 | 1.5 | -6.3 | 2.1 |
| Service | 8.0 | 10.0 | 9.0 | 10.0 | 10.0 | 10.0 | 10.0 | 10.0 | 10.0 | 10.0 | - |
| Economic Growth | 2.75 | 5.8 | 3.9 | 4.26 | 3.85 | 4.61 | 3.76 | 5.72 | 2.32 | 0.77 | 3.8 |

### The economy is characterized by high inequality (0.328), unemployment, weak infrastructure and domestic production, low domestic savings and investment, weak investment environment, low capital expenditure, high trade deficit, and a large share of the population living below poverty line. The poverty rate has remained very low in Nepal. The baseline status for the year 2014/15 (the 14th Plan period) to 2017/18 (for people below poverty line) is as follows: total population was 26,935,000; population aged above 15 years was 22,856,000; population aged between 15 and 64 years was 228,000; infant mortality rate (per 1,000 births per year) was 23; human development index was 0.54%. In the overall development indicators, Nepal ranks 179th out of 188 countries.

### The financial status of the country has remained weak for the last several years. In 2015/16, the budget deficit per capita (USD 3.8 billion) was 14% of GDP and the debt service ratio per capita (USD 22.9 billion) was 17%. The MOF has reported that the trade deficit rose to about USD 3.8 billion which is 15.4% of GDP.

### Low economic growth rate, persistent energy shortages, labour problems (shutdowns, unions), politicalization, uncontrolled public expenditure, and lack of transparency in budgeting are major constraints on the economy. Uncontrolled tax leakages, stagnant development with high inflation rate, rising trade deficit due to low level of export competitiveness, and weak infrastructure are also major challenges. Weak institutions, poor governance quality, and intellectual capacity are major problems of the economy. These problems are exacerbated by climate change.

### Increasing domestic production of goods and services that have competitive comparative advantage and productivity growth is a key strategy for poverty reduction in Nepal. The country has great potential to create human employment by creating employment opportunities at home and repelling demographic dividend by developing human capital through education, training, and skills development.

### For these reasons, without external support the Government of Nepal (GoN) cannot afford the necessary financial resources to implement the proposed projects. However, the GoN is committed to mobilize domestic resources with potential to improve resilience of climate vulnerable communities and ecosystems. Given the financial constraints and capacity limitations of the GoN, it is envisaged that the resources available to the GoN through the national budget for conventional infrastructure-based approaches to climate adaptation are insufficient. The private sector and local capital markets are

B

### Market failure and adaptation finance

The most important case of market failure in this type of adaptation project is the provision of public goods: construction of small-nature-based structures (bamboo check dams, plantations of grass and trees), construction and maintenance of water infrastructure (ponds, wells, canals, irrigation systems, ponds, water reservoirs); construction and maintenance of water holes in community grasslands; establishment of green belts around villages; and the provision of seeds and seedlings for tree planting. These are all public goods that cannot be easily internalized and thus relies more on government. It requires government to bear the bulk of the costs*. As government need to play a key role in providing citizens with security and certain public services, it is important that adaptation finance should be provided by the GCF so that governments can respond to such need.

Another important case of market failure in this project is the intervention in creating access to climate information and adaptation technology and capacity building on adaptation. The lack of access to these resources will reduce farmers' productivity as well as production thereby negatively affecting on the competitiveness of the farmers.

Financial barriers and for request GCF grant (choosing grant instrument)

No country has a national policy or strategy with limited budget for climate change adaptation. There are insufficient financial resources within government to implement large-scale adaptation projects. The lack of institutional capacity in the government is another reason why there are inadequate resources and technical expertise to replicate nature-based adaptation approaches. The GCF seeks maximum co-financing from other sources, including national governments, development partners, and private sector actors. For these reasons, a grant financing mechanism is thus sought to support the urgent adaptation interventions of the project. This will be done by requesting a grant instrument for the project from the GCF. The GCF grant cover to costs of adaptation activities

A. Additional information on the application of the GCF principles in "GCF Handbook: Decisions, Policies and Frameworks as agreed by the Board of the Green Climate Fund from its 8.01 to B.3.2 September 2019", pp.35) which includes the following:

i. The GCF will not finance projects that are primarily aimed at generating financial or other economic benefits.

ii. The economic internal rate of return assesses the cost effectiveness of projects that do not generate financial returns and is used to determine whether a project has sufficient potential for co-financing from non-financial benefits the project will generate, although the financial analysis does show financial benefits in the order of millions of dollars. In this case, the GCF grant will cover costs of adaptation activities.

The GCF grant will also cover technical assistance and may also cover technological interventions including the development of micros, small and medium enterprises for livelihoods.

These funds are used to cover the costs associated with the project implementation, including the cost of the project activities and are used to fund part of the project activities, and thus these costs need not be borne by the GCF. These activities are:

- establishment of ecological, social, and economic baselines for the selected site in Panchase,
- implementation of selected ecosystem-based adaptation measures under the principles of adaptive management,
- implementation of a knowledge management strategy that will collate, analyze, and share,
- incorporation of climate change considerations into existing land use planning processes, including development of Local Adaptation Plans, policy engagement with the government,
- application of best practices in agroforestry and other sustainable livelihood practices,
- technical input for the construction of small nature-based structures (bamboo check dams, plantations of grass and shrubs),
- application of bio-engineering techniques to provide structural support for erosion prone rural forest roads,
- restoration of degraded and vulnerable forests and grassland ecosystems through the removal and (productive) reuse of invasive species,
- environmental monitoring.

Community participation in various activities of the project such as reforestation, enrichment planting and maintenance of planted trees can be a source of income for the communities. The project will provide 10% of the planted trees in their own community or private forests. At the market wage rate of Nrs 600 per day (USD 5/day) the communities can earn Rs 3,600 per tree (USD 6). This means that for every 100 trees planted by them they will involve 198,100 project covered households. The project will support several nature-based tourism activities including bird watching, trekking and mountain climbing in the project area.
