### B.6 Exit strategy and sustainability (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

To ensure that the project is sustainable after its completion, the project has planned an exit strategy in adapting to climate even without external support. The project has planned an exit strategy in seven broad areas as shown below:

#### River basin approach mainstreamed and operationalised

River basins are a natural unit of management suited to assess and analyse ecological dynamics, and to bring stakeholders together at multiple scales to manage shared resources more effectively, ultimately contributing towards climate change adaptation[21]. The project will adopt the river basin approach in the Gorkha River Basin as a paradigm shift in enhancing climate resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems. Whilst the government has already initiated the process of mainstreaming the river basin approach into its policies at this national level the GoN has also formed a high-level committee, chaired by the Prime Minister, on integrated water resources management[22]. The Ministry of Forests and Soil Conservation (MoFSC) has developed an Integrated River Basin Approach paper to implement. By the time the project finishes, the MoFSC will have established at least one river basin centre in each of the 14 sub-district level Water Management Centres at the provincial level. As the project fits within the government's approach, its learning will be shared with other provinces.

#### Operational procedures in place

Operational procedures for project delivery mechanisms that engage both government agencies and civil society organisations will be in place. This approach will build federal, provincial, and local capacity and enable the establishment of a long-term institutional framework to ensure sustainability. The project will involve key partners, project stakeholders such as ICIMOD, WWF, JICA, FAO, etc. will benefit from experience and lessons learned.

#### Well-managed climate change information and adaptation technology knowledge centre established and operational

The project will develop a communication and knowledge management strategy that aims to ensure the project's findings have maximum update to contribute to national, provincial and local policy development. Towards this aim, the project will establish a well-managed climate change information and adaptation technology knowledge centre with immediate network of partners (local government, civil society, and private sector). 2) Policy and practice at local level: The project will work closely with local governments, civil society organisations, and other civil societies, particularly but not only in Nepal (who may have an interest in the climate change adaptation)

### Sustainable project results

B

It includes a resilient ecosystem that has started functioning well with improved flows of ecosystem services that result from nature-based adaptation to climate change: 2) Resilient communities with high adaptive capacity; and 3) Hydrometeorological monitoring systems to access climate information and adaptation technology.

## Policy mainstreaming of project learnings

It includes 1) climate change adaptation measures integrated into the governance structure of the newly developed federal government institutions, including the new provincial and local governments at three tiers of government; 2) support for the GoN policy of allocating at least 80 per cent of the total budget from Climate Change Fund to climate change adaptation activities; and 3) institutional arrangements that will enable planning and delivery mechanisms. While there is a wide range of climate-related policies at national level, implementation has been weak due to lack of coordination between different government agencies and authorities. The recent implementation of a new federal parliamentary structure throughout Nepal offers an unprecedented opportunity for mainstreaming climate change adaptation into the policy framework accordingly. The project will help develop new institutional and planning arrangements at the new provincial and local levels that are consistent with national policies, and strengthen existing ones to ensure effective mechanisms of government, civil society, and the private sector.

## Ownership and participation

It includes a participatory approach for continuation of adaptation measures

As this project is designed through consultation with government agencies, local communities, NGOs and CBOs, the project will be implemented in close collaboration with these stakeholders at all levels of government and communities to foster ownership and empower local communities in implementing project interventions. This will promote long-term sustainability by ensuring that the lessons learned from the project are embedded into best practices in the long term. Participatory approaches and co-management of the project involving local governments and communities contribute to long-term sustainability of project interventions.

## Financial exit strategy

The project will work with the River Basin Office to identify the financing arrangements to leverage significant investments for climate change adaptation projects at the provincial level. The financial exit strategy banking will not just benefit MoEF but also other Sectoral Ministries with their own going programmes that can be joined up with this project.

Some of the options in terms of the framing of the financing arrangement could include establishing a Trust Fund that includes contributions from traditional and private sector donors, contribution by various stakeholders such as communities, banks, the RBIs and income raised by the RBIs providing various services and a suite of financial instruments.

The project will also explore various types of financing instruments for leveraging funding for the Trust Fund or equivalent arrangements:

- Grant financing from bilateral and private sector donors given Nepal being a LDC are an acute result significant recipient of ODA.
- Concessional loans from development banks and commercial banks for ECA measures that blend hard infrastructure elements with natural infrastructure such as check dams, forest restoration etc.
- Debt financing where debt terms and conditions are pegged to the ECA measures results achievement such as water supply projects in Kailali District.
- Green bond financing where credit enhancement is provided by extending credit guarantee to cover a portion of the project cost. This has been successfully used for the Gandaki River Basin Project at the Kali Gandaki for example:
  - Payment for environmental services with the indigenous peoples and other hill and mountain people providing both financial and non-financial transfer payments for their stewardship functions by development banks.
- Tradable quotas/establishment of quotas for extraction of NTFFs, fish harvested from the Gandaki River and its various tributaries.
- Revenue generation schemes for pollution of water bodies, degradation of natural resources, etc. including polluting various water bodies and wetlands in the Gandaki River Basin such as Phewa Lake.

*areas will be managed through the following activities:*

**183. Invasive plant species, including some alien, have been found in the GRB, with the changed pattern of climate. The invasive plants are mainly Ageratina adenophora and Ageratum concolor. These two species have already invaded wetland and water hyacinth in wetlands has already threatened biodiversity and has been a conservation challenge in Chitwan National Park. Similarly, these invasive species have also invaded the forests of the GRB. The forest areas are being invaded by Ageratina adenophora, and Ageratum concolor. Likewise, the understory of forests was reported as being invaded by Ageratina adenophora and Ageratum concolor in the forests of Chitwan National Park, Anga, Katre kanda, and Bhaluwa.*

**184. In order to address this problem, these invasive species will be managed by uprooting and reducing their production in community forest lands and community grasslands. Reusing the removed biomass of invasive species for biochar production is also planned as a part of the project activities. The project team has conceptualized an EU supported biogas project where CFUG members produced biochar from the invasive species and sold to biochar producers in Kathmandu Valley, which will be used as soil amendment in agriculture and horticulture. This is well explained in Activity 2.1.3 - Restore the biodiversity of vulnerable forests and grassland ecosystems through the removal and (prospective) reuse of invasive species. The allocated budget for this is US$ 10,500.*

### D

#### 18. The 19 districts in the GRB are inhabited by 1,722,558 households (5,131,932 people). The GRBs covers 320,969 km² including 140,522 ha of forest, 196,069 ha of grassland, and 13,351 ha of water bodies.**The total area of the GRB is approximately 8% of the country’s land area. The GRBs are characterized by a high proportion (47%) of agricultural lands that do not have irrigation facilities and depend on rain-fed irrigation (MADIS, 2013).**

- The GRBs are also characterized by low levels of access to modern energy services, limited climate-resilient farming practices and ensuring improved market access**and capacity to use the services and products.**

- There will be improved management of about 285,000 ha of natural ecosystems and 113,000 ha of agricultural lands through the implementation of forest user group (FUG) interventions.

- Out of 6,000 Community Forest User Groups (FUGs) in the GRB, with average area of 94 ha per FUG and a community size of about 250 people. The FUGs will be supported to implement climate-resilient farming practices and improve management of 600 FUGs covering approximately 56,400 ha of forest.

- Additional support is provided for the implementation of 180 FUGs in the GRB which will benefit from direct and indirect management interventions, such as restoration through plantations, removal of invasive species, etc., by the year 2030.

#### The fund also aims to promote the paradigm shift towards climate-resilient adaptation pathways by taking into account climate change impacts on forest ecosystems and their services in the planning and implementation of forest conservation activities. This will be achieved through the shift in process of planning and implementation of climate change adaptation measures, which will be integrated with other land use plans and policies at all levels. The GRB as a whole landscape along the basin becomes one unit of planning and implementation of climate change adaptation measures. At the national level, the fund will support the development of relevant plans and policies at local, provincial and federal levels will include measures for climate adaptation, and in particular forest conservation activities. At the sub-national level, the GRB will serve as a model for the rest of the river basins of the country and also as a reference nationality for areas facing similar challenges.

#### This will also contribute to achieving the adoption goal of Paris Agreement, which aims to focus on enhancing national adaptation capabilities through the development of climate change adaptation strategies and practices as appropriate, to engage in adaptation planning and implementation through national adaptation plans, vulnerability assessments, and risk management. The fund will support the development of relevant plans and policies at all levels. At the national level, the fund will support the development of climate monitoring centres and inclusion of climate issues in the integrated development plans. At the sub-national level, the GRB will serve as a model for other river basins.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 45 OF 80

#### Potential for paradigm shift

192. The project aims to shift the planning and adaptation approach from prior boundary-based approach to river basin approaches, which will be more effective in addressing climate change impacts on water resources and ecosystem services at the landscape level (see Section B.5 for details). The provincial-level governments (three provinces 3, 4, and 5) will develop a provincial-level plan, while the municipal-level government (five in Province 2, eleven in Province 4, and three in Province 5) will develop a municipal-level plan. This project has high potential to shift to a river basin approach in climate change adaptation decision making in the plans and policies of Nepal's new political/administrative structures. Paradigm shift in planning and policy development for climate change adaptation will have a high potential for improving land-use, water availability, ecosystem services in the entire GRB.

#### Potential for scaling up and replication

193. The project has been recently introduced comprising 75 local government (previously 3,493), provincial, and federal levels. In the new governance rule, environment protection is included in the concept of local government. This project will be scaled up to all 75 local governments as a result of the changes; local regulations that were formed for a certain location earlier are no longer valid and the newly established rules will be applied throughout the entire country. The project has high potential to be replicated at provincial and local levels, and the plans, policies, regulations and institutions at the level are very similar. This project can also be scaled up in other countries that have similar climate change models demonstrated through a pilot project is required. Hence, this project has a high potential to shift to a river basin approach in climate change adaptation decision making in the plans and policies of Nepal and related ministries through a policy feedback mechanism to be developed within the framework of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process for scaling up and replication.

#### Potential for communication and knowledge management

194. The project includes an output entitled knowledge management established for climate resilient river basin planning and adaptation in Nepal. This will address the lack of trust and vulnerability between local, provincial and federal governments. The project will develop a communication and knowledge management strategy to ensure that all stakeholders are informed about the project findings and its outputs. The communication and knowledge management strategy will aim to maximize the use of project findings by national, provincial and local climate change policy makers and other relevant stakeholders in Nepal. To this end, the project will establish an information sharing platform for the exchange of knowledge products on building the resilience of ecosystems and communities through nature-based solutions, including ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA). The project will also develop a range of knowledge products that are accessible to the public through a website, radio, television, popular media, project publications, workshops and seminars.

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

105. The cumulative effect of the project’s outcomes will enhance capacity at community and institutional levels. This includes strengthening the capacity for climate change adaptation and resilience in communities and institutions that are sustaining. This is the first step in encouraging all key sectors, including local government, civil society, and the private sector to participate in the development of a national adaptation plan (NAP). The project will be developed through community and stakeholder consultation. Local bodies have given their written opinion that they would like to see the NAP process as an opportunity for them to engage with the public sector. The project will support local government in climate change adaptation in full participation of all private sectors, building confidence among the private sector for its engagement in adaptation.

106. The project will encourage farmers to participate in insurance schemes for major enterprises that will be supported by the project. This is a key element of the project’s strategy to ensure that the NAP is not just a document as presented in Section D (1), this project has envisaged that there will be private sector engagement value of the development of the NAP. The project will also support the implementation of climate change adaptation and EEA into their Integrated Development Plans. In addition to such direct regulatory and policy support, the project will provide technical assistance for the establishment of a Project Steering Committee (PSC) led by the Secretary of the Ministry. The PSC will consist Joint Secretariat level members from all the key sectors involved in climate change adaptation and EEA. These include the Department of Foreign Affairs and General Administration, Ministry of Land Management and Cooperatives, National Planning Commission, National Environment Agency, National Council for Indigenous Peoples’ Organization, Municipality Coordination Committee, Forest User Groups, etc.

### Contributing to a sustainable development pathway

108. In addition to maintaining and operationalisation of river basin approach, other transformations will include: upscaling the project’s climate change adaptation and EEA interventions in the pilot communities; promoting agro-based farming; substituting farmers towards commercial farming; complementing existing built infrastructure with renewable energy sources; and promoting sustainable land management practices such as nature-based pest management.

### D.3 Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 10 sentences)

#### Environmental co-benefits

193. The project will contribute to the conservation of ecosystems by conserving and sustainably managing them provides natural protection against extreme weather events and reduces disaster risk, as well as producing multiple co-benefits. The project will generate environmental co-benefits through:

- Increased forest and vegetation cover through new plantations and reforestation of forests; restoration of degraded lands, and improved forest management
- Improved water quality in rivers and lakes
- Improved management of wetland and water systems
- Reduced soil erosion and sedimentation
- Reduced agricultural expansion into natural areas
- Carbon sequestration: Mitigation through reducing greenhouse gas emissions and increased carbon sequestration.

200. The project will also share learning on climate adaptation will strengthen and increase social capital, empower communities, improve social inclusion and promote cooperation within communities.

### 4.D. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Economic indicators: The economy is dominated by agriculture, which accounts for about one-third of GDP and employs two-thirds of the labour force. Although agriculture is considered the backbone of the economy, agricultural productivity has been stagnant in recent years. The per capita income growth rate was at a low level in the last ten years, with an average annual growth rate of 3.8 percent and a low of 0.77 per cent annual growth during the period 2014/15 to 2016/17. Other economic indicators include: high levels of poverty and domestic and productive savings; inadequate investment environment, low capital expenditure; high debt burden; deficit financing; and high inflation.

The social sector has also remained at a low level in Nepal. Nepal was ranked 149 on the HDI (0.5274) in 2016/17. The literacy rate is 63 per cent, with only 48 per cent of women and men being literate; and 21 per cent of people living below poverty line. Other indicators are: Gender Development Index value of 0.56.

## Alignment with existing policies such as NDCs, NAMAs, and NAPs

The planned interventions are closely aligned with the SDGs' national priorities for enhancing resilience of communities to climate change impacts in line with relevant national policies laws, including the Constitution, the National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA), the National Framework on Local Adaptation Plans and Programmes (LAPPs), and the National Climate Change Strategy. Further information on the use of project in supporting these instruments can be found in the feasibility report (Annex 2).

### Nepal

#### 1. The NAP process

The NAP process is currently underway, with a draft NAP document expected to be communicated by UNFCCC in Nepal. Nepal has initiated, inter alia, Nepal's adaptation needs in the post-2020 context will be incorporated into the NDCs and NAPs. This will ensure that the project aligns with the overall development process; strengthen the implementation of Environment-Friendly Local Governments (EFGLGs) Frameworks; promote the adoption of climate-resilient technologies, and water conservation and greenery development; develop and implement adaptation strategies for climate change impacts in line with national priorities and policies; and enhance capacity building among academic institutions.

#### 2. NAMAs

Nepal is yet to request to UNFCCC a request for support in the development or implementation of NAMAs. Regarding NAP, in Nepal has initiated the process for the formulation but not yet finalized. The project will be aligned with the NAP and NDCs as per the depth adaptation needs for the future, and in the context of post 2020.

### Capacity Development

#### 1. Accredited Entity (AE) - IUCN

The proposed interventions in this project are focused on nature-based solutions. IUCN has a long history of working with governments to support climate change adaptation. In addition to several other projects globally, IUCN has very successfully implemented three projects in Nepal a flagship project in the Himalayas called “Ecosystems Protecting Infrastructure and Livelihoods (EPIL) for Climate Change Adaptation: Strengthening the evidence and informing policy” project from 2015-19. "Ecosystems Protecting Infrastructure and Livelihoods" (EPIL) is a flagship project of IUCN in Nepal, which aims to build capacity at the local level to address climate change impacts on infrastructure and livelihoods.

The Mountain Institute (TM) is implementing the “Scaling up mountain EDA” project in the Himalayas (Nepal), Mount Egon (Uganda), and the Andes (Colombia). The project has been implemented with IUCN Nepal, TM Uganda, and Colombia. These experiences have enabled IUCN to build significant capacity to implement climate change adaptation projects at scale.

#### 2. MOFE - Ministry of Forests and Environment

The DOFSE under the MOFE will be the lead in executing projects related to forest management and biodiversity conservation. The MOFE is an authorized body for planning, implementing and monitoring soil conservation activities and has a strong institutional capacity on forest management and biodiversity conservation. It has multidisciplinary teams at the district and national level to work on participatory approach. DOFSE has been involved in several projects related to climate change adaptation such as floods, landslides and droughts. It has already implemented projects supported by UNFCCC, GEF, LDCF, and AF. The MOFE is also responsible for developing the National Forest Policy, the National Biodiversity Framework and regular government planning and implementation processes.

#### 3. NEA - Nepal Environment Agency

The NEA is a national environmental regulatory institution that has been supported by the GCF in this project to align with the country's strategic national policies and priorities on climate change. GEF engaged IUCN Nepal in early stages of the project development process, and the NEA was involved in the preparation of the project requirements. The NEA engaged various national stakeholders to ensure compliance with the scope mandated by GCF and more importantly its creation ownership among the actors for the successful implementation of this

## Overall ownership

221. The project proposal is based on an idea submitted by the NDA to respond to their call for ideas (3rd January 2016). A consortium of IUCN, DFID (then DCSM) and NTNC proposed the concept note in response to this call. This consortium was formed as a result of the implementation of this project and ensures that the project result delivery is within the scope of the project and is being implemented by the NDA. The idea submitted by IUCN was accepted, and the consortium prepared a concept note, to be followed by a full-funding proposal to be submitted to the GCF for consideration.

223. IUCN prepared the concept note and it is the MWE who reviewed and approved it for submission to the NDA. The project design was developed in collaboration with the Ministry of Forests and Environment (MFE) and the NDA MWE, and adjustments to the project design were subsequently undertaken by the project design team following consultations at national level (see section 230 on stakeholder engagement). 

## Ownership of the Local Government

224. The project preparation phase included extensive consultations at national and sub-national levels with the local government. These consultations took place in 2016-2017 (see section 230 on stakeholder engagement). The consultations have included workshops, face to face meetings and information sessions, and focus group discussions. The project proposal was reviewed by the NDA MWE and approved for submission to GCF on 25 May 2019.

## Ownership of the Local Government

226. Overall, the project has been conceptualised together with the Ministry of Forests and Environment (MFE) and NTNC. The project proposal was developed in collaboration with the MWE and NTNC, incorporating their inputs and to ensure that it is aligned with national priorities. The project design addressed the climate change adaptation issues identified by government policies and strategies. The project design team included representatives from the NDA, MWE, NTNC, and IUCN. The project proposal involved the opportunity to include government needs and interest in the proposal. The final funding proposal was approved for submission to GCF on 25 May 2019 by the Deputy Secretary of the Planning Division and the Chair of the Technical Committee of MoF on 22 May 2019 and was approved for submission to GCF on 3rd June 2019. The project proposal was presented at a technical consultation conference call with ITAP held in January 2020.

## Ownership of the Local Government

228. In addition, the NDA organised several meetings and guided the conduct on the process for developing the project proposal. The NDA also provided inputs to the project design team with the proposal preparation financing from UNEP's Readiness Fund. The NDA representative participated in the theory of change workshop held at IUCN office in Kathmandu on 13-14 December, and in a technical consultation from 13-14 December; working meetings from 12-15 February 2018, a technical consultation workshop on 19 March 2018.

## Overall ownership

229. In addition, the NDA organised review meetings on 15 September and 5 October 2017; 30 November 2017 and 18 January 2018. Guidance from NDA helped shape the proposal. The funding proposal was reviewed by the NDA MWE and approved for submission to GCF on 25 May 2019 and was approved by the NDA for submission to GCF on 3rd June 2019.

## Overall ownership

230. As explained earlier, the Ganeshkot Basin project serves as the vehicle that the Government is using to improve the management of water resources in Nepal. The basin has been identified as a priority area by the High Level Committee to manage all watersheds in Nepal, following its establishment by the Prime Minister. To date, the Government has not been able to implement any projects on this scale in a fragmented manner, without significant evidence of progress at a river basin scale, let alone nationally.

## Overall ownership

230. This project is also supportive of the Government's Agriculture Development Strategy 2015-2035 as it will complement the investments needed to make the Basin more resilient to climate change and therefore contribute towards achieving the national development goals. However, lack of investments in this sector has led Nepal to become a net food importer since 1990.

#### Engagement with civil society organisations and other relevant stakeholders, including indigenous peoples, women and other vulnerable groups

**23. The project will be preceded by extensive consultation with representatives of government, non-governmental organizations and bilateral development agencies. **24. At the site level, 1,421 people were consulted during the feasibility study. 54.5 per cent of people consulted were women and 38.9 per cent were men; 6.6 per cent were indigenous peoples. The project will be implemented in 70 per cent men; and 31 per cent indigenous peoples. Efforts were made to include people from all spheres of life in the consultations.**

**25. At the national level individual consultations were conducted with climate change experts, academicians, development partners, government officials, representatives of civil society organizations and other stakeholders identified with the 'climate change elite group'. #### 5.8 Efficiency and effectiveness (max: 100 points; approximate page length: 3 pages)

**Financial viability and cost-effectiveness (max: 25 points)**

**24. It is estimated that the total budget of the proposed project will be US$ 32,715 million. Out of this, the GCF contribution to the project will be US$ 20,896 million and the co-financing by the partner will be US$ 11,819 million (see Annex 4 for detail on budget estimation).**

**25. A socio-economic impact assessment was conducted with contributions from various stakeholders through their participation in various activities of the project in general and more specifically in reforestation, enrichment planting and agroforestry plantation. The project expects to obtain land community concession of the equivalent of US$ 3-3.5 million.**

**26. There are several government offices including the Enterprise Development Office, the Women Development Office, the Forestry Department, the Environment Protection Department, the Water Resources Department and the Local government. The project will leverage management and technical support for the project from these organizations too.**

**27. The private sector entrepreneurs who are investing through their own equity or through borrowing from financial institutions such as banks and microfinance institutions (MFIs) have been identified in the project area. They participate private sector investment for livelihood improvement in the project area when it is clear that such enterprise development will contribute to the overall objective of the project.**

**28. Those enterprises that are involved in directly supporting adaptation activities such as micro-irrigation, agroforestry and reforestation have been identified through a participatory process with stakeholders and considered as enterprises that contribute to enhancing the adaptive capacity of the communities. The provisional criteria for determining these enterprises include:**

* Support sustainable income generation connected to biodiversity conservation
* Ability to deliver consistent results in terms of technical and financial capacity to manage projects
* Assurance of community participation in project design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation;
* Demonstrates sound financial management experience;
* Has access to ongoing grants or loans;
* Well-established accounting and financial reporting systems; auditing requirements

**Cost-effectiveness and efficiency**

**29. The project will directly address the strategic impact areas of the GCF's adaptation (ecosystem and ecosystem services), livelihoods of people, communities and regions, food and water security. Though it has not been possible to quantify these impacts in monetary terms, the project is expected to contribute significantly towards management of forest, reforestation and enrichment planting.**

**30. The project will be cost-effective as it will provide a range of ecosystem-based solutions such as EEA, EPIC, and IVMI with concrete feasibility analysis of the alternative options, drawing on use of local capacity of ongoing projects in the area. It is expected that these actions will contribute significantly to enhancing resilience in making the community climate resilient; including resilient natural ecosystems and agroecosystems, the cost per unit benefit ratio for each action is estimated at US$ 10-25. The project has been found to be cost-effective (Feasibility Study, Section 11.5). It shows that the project is cost effective in terms of building resilience as well. See Annex Ia for narrative and Ib for detail results in Excel Sheet.**

### Investment efficiency: Investment efficiency was analysed by undertaking a thorough financial and economic analysis with and without project (see Annex 3a for the results and 3b for detaild analysis in Excel). Based on various assumptions, it is concluded that the project will be cost-effective. The project team and senior officials of the MOFE, It was concluded that the average life of trees planted in the mid-hills can be considered to be years. This is based on the fact that the trees are planted at a very young age and are expected to take 10 years to reach costs and benefits at normal values. Based on the opportunity cost of borrowing capital from the commercial banks, a nominal 10 per cent discount rate has been chosen. The financial and economic rate of return is as follows.

#### External financial rate of return with and without project

243. With GCF financing, the NPV of the project will be USD 17 million. The benefit cost ratio (BCR) will be 1.85 and the project will come to break-even point in years. The financial internal rate of return is 10 per cent.

244. Without the GCF financing, the NPV of the project will be USD -23.8 million. The BCR will be 1.04 and the project will not reach a break-even point within years.

#### Economic internal rate of return

245. With GCF financing, the NPV of the project will be USD 22.07 million. The benefit cost ratio (BCR) will be 1.92. Break-even point is around 11 years. The economic internal rate of return (EIR) is 23.43 per cent.

246. Though there is a regular programme of the government, the scale is very small. Hence, without the external financial support from GCF, it will be difficult to achieve the desired impact and also to withstand significant changes in the GCF to minimize disaster events. All the costs to manage disasters such as floods, fires, landslides, etc., are borne by the government. The project will provide a cost-effective solution for water resources management, cost of rehabilitating physical infrastructures such as roads, irrigation channels, etc.

247. With the project, the economy will be USD 10 million and the community and ecosystems will be USD 53.5 million and community contributions to the equivalent of USD 5.3 million. These changes would not be achieved without GCF support.

As mentioned earlier, the Gandaki River Basin project is focused primarily on generating non-financial benefits for the communities in the basin through improved livelihoods, access to services, and investment in land and water management to reduce climate risks. The project therefore targets grant financing from the GCF to achieve these objectives. However, it should be noted that such financing is not possible at onset in a basin where poverty persists, and where the economy is largely rural and linked with agriculture. The overall objective of the project is to use the momentum gained from improving the resilience of the people to climate change impacts to create a stronger enabling environment for private sector investment.

### Considerations

249. The Feasibility Study (Section 5) draws upon best practices and lessons learnt from past and on-going initiatives in Nepal, as well as from other countries. It also draws on the experience of various levels of government departments from the target locations and local communities provided insight into past and on-going initiatives that have been successful or unsuccessful.

In order for EIA to be successfully implemented, there is a need to foster integrated, cross-sectoral approaches in the following areas:

- Clarifying and addressing stakeholders' different views, interests and knowledge levels are key to building a strong consensus.
- It is important to identify and collaborate with champions or leaders who can motivate, mobilize and guide their peers.
- There is a need to establish public-private partnerships to sustain the interventions to be piloted by the project

**Page 57**

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 53 OF 80

- Aspects of traditional nature-based practices seem to offer more appropriate and accepted solutions and are likely to be more successful than approaches that are brought from the external environment with no local context.
- Biodiversity and social impacts can be achieved together and also increase the overall success of EIA.
- Incorporation of indigenous knowledge is essential for the success of any project.
- Use of local species and materials for restoration of the ecosystems and control of erosion and floods gives better results.
- Water availability can be increased through wetland restoration, water source protection, community pond projects, etc.
- Use of bioengineering gives sustainable results for rural road construction and slope stabilisation.
- This will also help in reducing soil erosion and sedimentation in rivers and other water bodies.
- Agroforestry, ecotourism and value-chain development should be integral component of climate resilient farming.
- Leadership of women and socially excluded groups need to be promoted in natural resource management
- Livelihoods of the local communities need to be considered.

250. The feasibility of these best practices was verified with the communities and stakeholders at various levels. These practices are also adaptable for different agro-climatic zones, respect species location and sub-watershed.
This will be further verified during annual planning at the time of project implementation.

1. Nepal Demographic and Health Survey 2011 of the Ministry of Health and Population has classified food insecurity into four categories and has used the Hunger Scale (HS) to measure the severity of food insecurity. The HS is a simple tool that measures the severity of food insecurity on a scale from 1 to 3. Food secure: These households do not experience any food security (current concern) and rarely worry about this condition; 2. Mildly food insecure: These households occasionally worry about food, but they are able to meet their dietary needs most days of the week; 3. Moderately food insecure: These households sometimes worry out of food, going to bed hungry, or going a day without eating even if it is rarely in other words, any household that scores between 1 and 2 on the Hunger Scale (HS) is considered as food secure.
2. The current baseline could be established with an 83% budget allocated for climate change action. See for detail: Nepal’s Climate Change Budget Report 2015. This report has identified 46 municipalities in Nepal that have been categorized into four groups in allocating development budget in relation to climate change adaptation (as follows): Level 1 - Number of municipalities allocating less than 30% of their development budget in relation to climate change adaptation; Level 2 - Number of municipalities allocating between 30-59.99% of their development budget in relation to climate change adaptation, and so on.

#### 254. Table 14 presents the description of activities with their sub-activities and deliverables. These deliverables are reflected in the implementation plan (Annex S).

##### Sub-Activities

**E.E.3. Establish climate resilient agroforestry practices**

* **Description:** Increasing productivity of rained agriculture due to reduced water stress, improved soil fertility, and climate change: youths from rural areas are migrating to urban centers, affecting negatively to food security.
* **Deliverables:**
    * One climate resilient feasibility study and identify appropriate sites
    * Two climate resilient agroforestry-based value chain developed
    * Development training manual and training of the selected farmers
    * Demonstration of climate resilient agroforestry practices
    * Plantation and maintenance of the agroforestry trees (5000 Households in 2 replicant sites)
    * Selection of the selected agroforestry option and establishment of appropriate agroforestry enterprise

**E.E.4. Construct small nature-based structures (grass check dams, bamboo check dams, plantations of grass and trees)**

* **Description:** The project is built on the learning of the Ecosystem based Adaptation Approach (of Ethiopia) and the Eco DRIP project (ecosystem approach - "EPIC"), restoration of agricultural lands degraded by soil erosion done using biosilvopasturing approach. These structures will be constructed at 15 highly and very vulnerable locations identified during consultation.
* **Deliverables:**
    * Nature-based structures constructed at 60 sites
    * Bamboo protection dams constructed at 30 sites
    * Check-dams constructed in 15 locations
    * Bond payment by bamboo farmers
    * Diversion channels

**E.E.5. Promote drought and flood tolerant rice varieties (one drought tolerant variety identified and one flood tolerant variety identified) and plan areas in the Chure and lower Terai**

* **Description:** The project will identify, introduce and farmers' adoption of drought and flood tolerant rice varieties enhanced through training and extension services to farmers in Chhatarpur and Chitrakot districts and some plain areas of Bhojpur district.
* **Deliverables:**
    * 148,665 ha under flood tolerant variety
    * 20,379 ha under drought tolerant variety
    * Agriculture Research Council will confirm the maintenance and conservation site
    * In collaboration with the local farmers' organization, confirm the maintenance and conservation of the selected sites.
    * Farmer food and drought tolerant farming technology through training and extension services to all potential farmers

## 2.1.2 Apply bio-engineering techniques to provide structural protection and reduce erosion in prone rural forest areas.

As most of the project sites are either in high mountains or in hot hills, there are many problems related to soil erosion. Some engineering work (check dams) could be done on the degraded lands such as planting grasses, bamboo in gullies will be done to prevent soil erosion and protect property during the monsoon and halt further degradation of the land combined with NBS in the gullies at 700 sites.

In order to prevent mudslides and soil erosion caused by heavy rains during the monsoon, plantations will be done along the slopes. This is important to maintain rural accessibility and reduce soil erosion due to heavy rain. Plantation along 70 km road will be done in phases and the required structures will be designed at finer scale during implementation.

## 2.1.3 Restore the biodiversity of vulnerable forests and protect against invasion by invasive species

It has been experienced that having plant species, including agroforestry, are coming up on the GRSB with the changed climate. The following tree species such as Teak, Parthenium in the low land terrestrial forest and Eucalyptus, Acacia, and Chirban National Park (low land of GRSB) are already dominating the area. These as well as public lands in hill areas are heavily invaded by invasive species such as Agarista adscendens, Lantana camara, and Lyonia ovalifolia (Agerat), Hedyotis, Kalmia latifolia, etc.

In order to restore biodiversity, these invasive species will be removed and uprooting and reusing in value added products. The total area of 1000 ha has an average 20 ha per CFJ, and 10 community forests have been identified.

The sector acting vital in the control of invasive species removed from the community forest is the quality of their raw material for charcoal and briquette production. This will help to get rid of the control invasive species in the forests.

## 2.1.4 Create new SOPs that support future interventions on agroforestry, forestry

A standard operating procedure (SOP) for all interventions will be created and compiled by the project to help the communities and

- Identification of species for restoration along the 70 km road
- Biodiversity restored from the impact of invasive species in vulnerable forests (1000 ha), and degraded grasslands (100 ha)

## 3.1.3 Training and supporting communities in climate change adaptation for restoration and conservation of ecosystems in target areas

The projected future scenarios of climate change have reflected that climatic changes will lead to more frequent extreme events occurring and increasing the vulnerability of communities. Studies have shown, however, farmers' capacity will be enhanced through training and learning centres as 19 district. It will be important for farmers to adapt to the new measures in order to reduce their vulnerability to climate change and thus making the livelihoods of these communities more sustainable.

There will be 50 such field schools run by the project in collaboration with local government in other municipalities.

In addition, communities will also have access to community-based water refuges and human wildlife conflicts in the target areas.

## 3.1.4 Link upstream and downstream vulnerable communities through informed management of spring-fed water source protection

Flooding, inundation and sedimentation due to climate change and low flow or water scarcity can be reduced through improved land use practices that make the upstream area of such vulnerable communities in the downstream more sustainable. This will be done by linking upstream through management of spring-fed water sources and flood protection in upstream. This support will be linked with the development of specific sites for finer detail will be developed after receiving the support from the local government and community.

## 3.2.1 Prepare River Basin Management Plan (RBMP) framework with integrated watershed and water resource management plans for the GRB that includes grasslands, fisheries, wetlands and ecosystems

In view of the recently changed climate change scenario to federal system, the project has taken an initiative to prepare sub-watershed level plans, which will be integrated into the basin level plans and federal governments with the support from the local government. The plans will be integrating forest, grassland, fisheries, wetlands and ecosystems.

### 2.2.2 Develop a methodology for ecosystem services valuation to support planning

For the sustainability of the project results, provincial and local government will need to mainstream the restored ecosystems and continued ecosystem services into their policies. They must persuade the finance people to allocate funds for the restoration and maintenance of the value of ecosystem services in return.

The following are some of the acceptable method of valuing ecosystem services: Ecosystem Services Valuation (ESV) and used by local, provincial as well as Federal governments in Canada and globally. The valuation method via EIA project will be developed and tested to make it generic for the GRB as ultimately for all provinces.

### 2.2.3 Policy Development for local governments to incorporate climate change adaptation and SEA into their Integrated Development Plan

Integrated Development Plan (IDP) is a provincial level plan that is prepared by the province as they go along. As the local government will be preparing IDPs, incorporating climate change adaptation and SEA will be overlooked. They will need to have adequate capacity at the local level to do so.

The policy document will guide the government to incorporate climate change adaptation and SEA into their IDP. It will support 50 local governments and generate institutional capacity for them to do so that it will be replicated by other local governments of the GRB and then gradually by other local governments in Canada.

### 3.1.1 Establish a provincial level system for collecting lessons learned and evidence based scientific information on climate change adaptation best practices.

A system for collating data and information from the field and scientific literature on ecosystem- and community-based approaches to adaptation.

The system will include M5 on climate change adaptation, EIA, SEA, and climate change adaptation programs in progress. It will also include climate change adaptation technology and tools.

# E.2 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, 1 page)

## Monitoring and reporting channels

255. As IUCN is the accredited entity for the project, the project will comply with the IUCN Monitoring and Evaluation Policy (April 2015). IUCN recognizes that the purposes of monitoring and evaluation (M&E) include: Learning; and accountability.

256. The IUCN Monitoring and Evaluation Policy requires monitoring to comply with the so-called SMART criteria: Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant, Time-bound. It also states that evaluations should be conducted in a way that evaluations explore the five major criteria: namely relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, impact, and sustainability.

257. Performance measurement will focus on two aspects of intervention: 1) inputs delivering leading to tangible results; and 2) outputs delivering leading to outcomes and impacts. The project will use both quantitative and qualitative data at scaled and scaled-out. Achievement on input delivery and output results will be monitored regularly through semi-annual and annual reporting, while the outcome and impact level results will be evaluated in the 4th and 7th years of the project.

## Performance Monitoring Framework (PMF)

258. For monitoring, reporting and evaluation, a PMF has been specially designed for this project (see Annex 11 for details). The PMF consists of seven distinct mechanisms (see Figure 4), each with distinct operational features and time frames.

### Activity level

1) Monitoring of beneficiary selection and community participation by communities
2) Monitoring implementation and input delivery to the Field Execution Office
3) Monitoring implementation and input delivery to government in implementation by Provincial Coordination Units (PCUs) of the Provincial Government
4) Monitoring Project Outputs by the Project management unit (PMU)
5) Monitoring Project Level Outcomes by IUCN/UNCCD as an executing agency
6) Monitoring Financial Outcomes by IUCN/UNCCD as an executing agency

### Monitoring Fund Level Impacts by Green Climate Fund Stakeholder involvement in monitoring and evaluation

259. The project will ensure that all relevant stakeholders and bodies will need to play key roles in executing elements of the PMF for which they have direct control and input.

260. Additionally, the project will need to strengthen the development of M&E capacities of stakeholders and local beneficiaries. Stakeholders and communities are encouraged to actively participate with the Project Management Committee (PMC) including a) attending periodic joint meeting of project management committee and stakeholder engagement meetings; b) participating in project planning workshops; c) and d) Working closely with the PMC in developing Annual Work Plan.

261. Monitoring will be conducted through regular reporting, monitoring meetings and workshops, and by collecting information, monitoring and reporting on project performance indicators. For details on stakeholder engagement in monitoring, see Annex 7b.

Monitoring implementation challenges and risks

262. The implementation challenges and risks will be monitored by the PMU and the project stakeholders through on-going monitoring of progress against the approved work plan and planning and reported on in regular basis. Measures for addressing the reported challenges and risks will be discussed at the next TSC meeting with mitigating measures for approval and inclusion in the annual programme of the following year.

Financial monitoring

263. Financial monitoring and reporting will be as per Figure 3 in Section B.4. The PMU will be responsible for the on-going financial monitoring and reporting.

F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

**F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)**

264. Even though the AE, IUCN has not undertaken due diligence assessments of activities against the specific risks of money laundering and fraud, it is important to note that IUCN has a number of policies in place to address the risks of money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism and other prohibitive acts.

265. Filing of financial statements: The AE, IUCN requires all implementing partners (other than sovereign governments) as well as pre-contracting (other than sovereign governments that are exempted since they are signatories to the UNODC Memorandum of Understanding) to file their financial statements with IUCN prior to signing a contract. This enables IUCN to have a comprehensive understanding of the ownership of the organisations and its main funding sources. The AE, IUCN also requires all implementing partners to provide information on any history of fraud and money-laundering. If otherwise, IUCN can decide to not engage with the concerned implementing partner.

266. Secondly, relevant check is at the stage of contracting where for each and every contract with an implementing partner, IUCN needs a sign-off form to be completed and duly signed off. The sign-off form in this specific section on procurement will need to include confirmation that the following assessments have been carried out which include relevant assessment:

- **Financial Risk Capacity Assessment**
- **Financial background checks**
- **Business Integrity Screening**
- **UN Security Council Sanctions List**
- **EU Sanction List**
- **US Office of Foreign Assets Control Sanctions List**

267. Thirdly, the AE, IUCN’s 2019 UNFCCC UNEP SFI/GCF executing partners contain a number of requirements designed to minimize risk of money laundering and fraud. Specifically, by requiring that the implementing partner provide information on their financial statements, including balance sheet, income statement, bank account, and reported on with regards to very specific activities/achievable and budget lines.

268. The procedures mentioned above are applicable to all recipient organisations such as grantees, implementing partners, etc., and are in line with the AE’s policies and guidelines for procurement of goods, works or services.

269. Thirdly, any costs submittal by recipient organisations need to be in compliance with relevant IUCN policies, procedures and guidelines and will be reviewed by the AE prior to approval.

270. The AE, IUCN has a number of other policies and procedures in place for minimizing uncertainties but more so for prevention of unlawful activities such as money laundering, terrorist financing and/or other prohibitive acts.

The AE, IUCN has rigorous policies, procedures and guidelines determining contracting of both government and non-government entities. These are based on the risk assessment of the recipient organisation. There are specifically developed templates and procedures to assess various aspects including general and background checks, financial statements, etc., which are required for each entity that is proposed to be contracted by party/individual.

The AE, IUCN also includes analysis of the administrative and structural processes of the contractor. This includes review of governance and management structure of the recipient organisation, review of internal controls (e.g., procurement, accounting, financial reporting, human resources, security clearances), review of internal control systems, etc. In addition, in terms of financial processes there is review of the sources of income and expenditure, including revenue reports, capital structure, verification of source of income and utilization of the expenditure, audit process etc.

273. Besides, entities are bound to follow the rules and regulations of the government along with the AE’s policies. In particular, IUCN has a number of policies in place for ensuring that implementing partners, which lessen the chance of financial fraud as well as secure the utilization of funds by the activity. For reference, AE’s 3019 UNFCCC UNEP SFI/GCF executing partners contain a number of requirements.

274. This project builds on the successes of ecosystem-based adaptation initiatives taken by the government and other implementing agencies in Nepal such as the National Trust for Nature Conservation (NTNC), ESD and EcoDrR (ESDR). PCPC and SPCR that promote climate change adaptation and/or protection and restoration of ecosystems, have been working with IUCN since 2013. The AE has a long-standing relationship with the executing entities (PCU, NTNC and IUCN Nepal), and the due diligence already in place for these, which has ensured sound financial and programme monitoring systems as well as strong technical oversight.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 72 OF 80

## Selected Risk Factor: Description and Mitigation Measure

### Selected Risk Factor 1:
**Category:** Technical and operational  
**Probability:** Medium  
**Impact:** High  

* **Description:** Constraints to implementing reforms necessary for addressing climate change risks - there are certain acts and regulations to be amended under the new constitution, which will affect the authorities and responsibilities between the three tiers of government in the new federal system. It might take some time to get such regulations and operational guidelines in place.

### Mitigation Measure(s):  
* Climate adaptation will be strengthened through the project supporting policy development and awareness raising.
* The project will identify and support champions at all levels, from the public and private sectors.


### Selected Risk Factor 2: 
**Category:** Technical and operational  
**Probability:** Medium  
**Impact:** Low  

* **Description:** Insufficient capacity to support emerging implementation challenges - the newly formed provincial and local governments are not fully equipped with required resources or sufficient technical capacity.

### Mitigation Measure(s):  
* Capacity building to enable a range of institutions at different scales to effectively coordinate and engage.
* Continuous monitoring and evaluation.
* The provision of continuous support and monitoring by the project team will provide rapid response support to emerging implementation challenges.


### Selected Risk Factor 3: 
**Category:** Medium  
**Impact:** Low  

* **Description:** Securing government and private sector investments in ESMF (Environmental Sector budget allocation) more on direct impact infrastructure construction with short-term plans, and the level of awareness in the private sector on climate change adaptation is at a rudimentary stage due to which it has not seen scope in investing in climate change adaptation.

### Mitigation Measure(s):  
* The involvement of partners will assist in addressing this risk.
* Financing tools and risk mitigation instruments that demonstrate the potential for climate adaptation to yield a high return on investment will be developed and deployed.

## G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

278. The project aims to improve climate resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems in the Gondwana River Basin. Given that the project is small scale and will not have a significant impact on the environment or society, it is considered unlikely that the activities carried out under this project will have major adverse environmental and/or social risks and impacts. The project will be subject to an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) as described in section 280.

279. The proposal has completed the screening processes of the ESMF and has been classified as moderate risk project (category B). The full ESM Screening Report including the discussion on potential environmental and social risks is attached to this document.

280. Annex 5B presents the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) that has been developed as part of the screening process. The ESMF describes how the project will be implemented, including the planned field interventions (sub-projects). The ESMF describes how sub-projects will be screened on environmental and social risks and impacts.

281. The ESMF is based on a risk assessment of potential environmental and social risks based on the proposed activities for the interventions (Table 13). While noting that the existing state of the field interventions and their expected outcomes are not fully known, the ESMF identifies those risks which have been determined with a reasonable degree of certainty, will be limited in scale and none of any them are expected to be irreversible. The ESMF also sets out how these risks will be managed or mitigated. Specifically, the impacts are expected to be addressed either by applying standard best practice or readily-available mitigation measures as described below.

### Capacity of the EEs to implement the ESMF

282. As presented in Table 13, the ESMF is a living document. The MOFE is mandated as a focal point for the government regarding environmental issues. The MOFE has two separate divisions looking after the environment and climate change. The Environment Division looks at all matters relating to the environment including biodiversity conservation. The Climate Change Management Division looks after the climate related matters. These Divisions are led by Joint Secretary level officials who have been designated as focal points for the ESMF. The MOFE has a standard monitoring and supervision schedules for each division and follows a checklist.


---
**Page 79**
---

G

**Stakeholder consultation on gender assessment**

187. The Gender Assessment was conducted during the preparation of the proposal (see Annex 7a - Institutional and Stakeholder Analysis). Based on their roles, responsibilities, strengths, and capacity building needs, the following stakeholders were identified as key for this proposal: government institutions and organizations involved in EIA. Such stakeholders were consulted at all 19 districts of the GRB in a group of multi-stakeholder workshops (see Annex 7b - Stakeholder Consultation). The workshop participants included representatives from government agencies, civil society organizations, private sector entities, and other relevant stakeholders.

188. Detailed consultation process and outcomes are presented in Annex 7b. There were 1,421 people consulted during the feasibility study phase of the project. Of these, 50% were women and 50% men. During the detailed design phase, members consulted, 65% were women and 35% men. Efforts were made to include people from all spheres of life in the consultations, including those who are often excluded such as women, youth, persons with disabilities, and other marginalized groups. The consultation process was guided by the following principles: gender equality and inclusion in the socio-economic sector; c) political and institutional environment for gender equality and inclusion; d) social norms and cultural practices that hinder or promote gender equality/inequality in climate change adaptation, and a capacity development needs for gender mainstreaming.

Summary of key findings

189. Vulnerability in GRB continues to be highly correlated with gender, caste, ethnicity, region, identity, and geographical location. Women are more vulnerable than men to the adverse impacts of climate change and natural disasters because of gender based discrimination, ingrained patriarchal socio-economic and political systems, and other social norms and cultural practices that hinder or promote gender equality/inequality in climate adaptation.

190. The majority of women who were consulted for this proposal come from a Dalit group; they usually settle near the landslide prone river banks, and are thus more vulnerable.

## G

### Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

**G3. Financial management and procurement**

The project will ensure gender equality in all relevant activities. There are 38 activities designed over seven output areas and USD 165,914 allocated to implement the Climate Change and Gender Action Plan (CCGAP). The CCGAP is a joint initiative of the Ministry of Environment, Forests and Climate Change and Gender Officer and Gender Officer of NTNC. IUCN Nepal and NTNC have proven success in meaningful engagement with women’s groups, women’s organizations, and other stakeholders.

Their implementation approach will be extensively used to safeguard gender and social issues in the project. Mobile phone-based financial management systems for farmers, farmer groups, saving and credit groups, and enterprises will be prioritized to ensure benefits for women.

# Financial Management

## Financial Monitoring
- As a GCF accredited entity (AE), financial management and procurement of this project will be guided by IUCN’s internal audit, evaluation, and risk management policies.
- These include financial reporting templates and forms that are sent internationally for reporting purposes. This includes bank policy and procedures, operational policy, record management, monitoring, and internal controls.

## Financial Accounting and Auditing
- USD will be used in Project accounts. Oanda.com website’s exchange rate will be used to convert the foreign currency into Nepalese Rupees (NPR) at the end of each month.
- The project will be audited in accordance with IUCN policies and procedures according to the current audit policies, as well as the International Organization for Standardization (ISO) 9001:2015 standard. For Nepal (ACAN), in-situ scheduled audits are performed during the project cycle as per IUCN assurance plans, based on the risk assessment of the project and its activities. Audits will be conducted to ensure that they are appropriate purpose and in accordance with the work plan. A scheduled audit can consist of a financial audit or an internal audit.

## Project Monitoring
- IUCN provides a variety of assurance activities which will (but not limited to) Periodic site reviews of project progress, including financial status, implementation of project activities (by external auditors and qualified accountants), and Programmatic monitoring of activities, which provides evidence regarding the state of project implementation.

## Project Accounts
- Project accounts will be audited annually by an external Chartered Accountants, registered in the Association of Chartered Certified Accountants (ACCA) or a Chartered Accountants, registered in ACAN or by qualified IUCN Staff, to monitor executing partner activities.
- Under Section 10 of the Act on Public Procurement, the AE will sign a contract with the Executing Entity (MOFERP). MOFERP will contract out the work to its own suppliers. A detailed description is given in section 2.3.

## Financial Planning
- The project will provide at least the following types of financial documents: Project Sources and Uses of Funds, Use of Funds by Project Activity, Project Balance Sheet, Project Cash Forecast, Annual Budget, and Final Financial Report (as per IUCN’s standard inventory record). An updated report will be maintained about the status and whereabouts of the purchased goods. The inventory will be verified on a yearly basis and a report prepared.

## Procurement
- Under the current implementation arrangement, As an AE, UICN will use its own procurement policy. IUCN has well-designed procurement policy namely - Policy and Procedures on Procurement of Goods and Services February 2018.
- The procurement process is divided into two phases: (a) Prequalification phase; (b) Bid evaluation phase. Only the criteria and their relative importance as stated in the RFP will be used to evaluate Proposals.

## Procurement Process
- IUCN seeks to minimise environmental impacts in all its procurement decisions. Suppliers and Proposals are evaluated on both environmental and commercial factors, including: quality of goods or services; technical capability; financial health of the supplier; previous experience and reputation. Purchases are made from suppliers who have been prequalified by IUCN.
- The following criteria will be used to evaluate proposals: (i) Technical proposal; (ii) Financial proposal; (iii) Environmental Proposal; (iv) Other considerations as stated in the RFP.

## Procurement Contracts
- Procurement contracts shall be entered only with responsible suppliers who are reputable, well established and have a good reputation for quality of goods or services. The contract will be signed by IUCN and the supplier.
- Some parts of the project area are relatively remote and require transport on sealed and unsealed roads that are often in poor condition. Therefore, it is important to ensure that the supplier has experience with this type of terrain.

## Project Budget
- The project budget includes funds for: stationery and consumables, furniture, common equipment and renovation, security and vehicle running costs, vehicle hire (for periods before vehicles can be purchased) and for occasions when vehicles are not available. Other items include: office supplies, maintenance materials, construction and up-scaling materials, teaching materials, training materials, utilities and miscellaneous items.

## Monitoring and Reporting
- All procurement activities will be monitored by IUCN staff. The inventory of all purchases shall be registered in IUCN’s standard inventory record. An updated record will be maintained about the status and whereabouts of the purchased goods.

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, projects must be disclosed as soon as possible after submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information.
- ☐ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, does not contain any specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without breaching confidentiality.
- ☐ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF as certain information is being provided in confidence. According to the Information Disclosure Policy, the GCF proposes to disclose the following copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
    - A redacted copy of the funding proposal which the confidential portions are replaced accordingly together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality as per the information disclosure policy; and
    - Redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.
