# Secretariat’s assessment of FP131

## Proposal name: Improving Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Communities and Ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin, Nepal

### Accredited entity: International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN)

### Country/ies: Nepal

### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Builds upon previous interventions (Global Environment Facility, United Nations Development Programme, World Bank, Asian Development Bank, etc) to bridge particular existing gaps in climate change adaptation and reduce vulnerability to climate change. | Some environmental risks, in particular glacier lake outburst floods are not fully incorporated/considered in the project. |
| Close alignment with existing national policies, strategies, and plans, including a targeted contribution, nationally appropriate mitigation actions (NAMAs), low emission development plans, and national adaptation programme of action, in addition to sector strategies, including water resources conservation, environmental and energy. | Governance capacity at the provincial and local levels are not fully developed. While the project will address this issue, this may impact long-term sustainability. |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XVIII, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations,” respectively.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

1. The "Improving Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Communities and Ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin in Nepal" project aims to sustainably mainstream and operationalize a river-basin approach for watershed management in order to make climate vulnerable.


---
**Page 91**
---

# Communities and ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin (GRB) more resilient: The interventions focused on

## Enhancing the resilience of the livelihoods of vulnerable communities in the GRB region through climate-resilient agroforestry and livelihoods improvement actions so they can adapt to changing climatic conditions, including water source management to increase water availability and water use efficiency.
- Strengthening the climate resilience of ecosystems through community-based conservation practices such as tree planting, soil and water conservation techniques, in addition to invasive species management, and, furthermore, providing technical training for local communities.

## Providing institutional support to develop appropriate governance mechanisms, baseline data and information systems at the national and GRB level, and knowledge sharing among stakeholders for effective implementation of interventions.
- The project area covers the GRB, which contains 6 protected areas (out of 20 in Nepal), one UNESCO World Heritage Site (Kathmandu Valley), 14 Ramsar sites (out of 35 in Nepal), and 3 Ramsar sites (out of 10 in Nepal). Due to a larger number of habitats created by the extensive altitudinal variation, the GRB has high biological diversity, with 14 plants listed in the appendix I of CITES.

## Increases in temperature and shifts in the intensity of seasonal rainfall are observed to accelerate soil erosion and increase the frequency and severity of floods and droughts that are erosive. A decrease in snowfall and overall drier winters in upper elevations leads to decreased water flow in the GRB and its tributaries by increasing frequency, duration and intensity of dry spells, which results in reduced livestock production.
- More favourable conditions for invasive species can be created, adversely affecting local biodiversity.

### The GRB region has a population of 5,131,932 people (9.3% of the total population of Nepal). The region has more than 40 ethnic groups. Most people in the region live in poverty and are dependent on natural resources for their livelihoods.
- Women have the primary responsibility for maintaining households and also provide most of the agricultural labor; this has led to women and their dependents (children and the elderly) becoming more vulnerable.

### Following the adoption of a new Constitution in 2015, the Government of Nepal established the Gandaki River Basin Management Authority (GRBMA), which is both national and local. The GRB includes 151 local government bodies located within three provinces.
- Many environmental laws and policies are not yet fully implemented or enforced; however, climate-relevant policies and plans are either not in place or out of date to align with the new government mandate.

## The GCF has committed USD 32.7 million, with a request to GEF for grant finance of USD 27.4 million (81% cost). The Government of Nepal, through the Ministry of Forests and Environment, is contributing USD 0.56 million in the form of grants (in-kind), respectively.
- USD 3.45 million and USD 3.6 million in the form of grants (in-kind), respectively. The International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN) Nepal also is contributing USD 0.565 million in the form of grants (in-kind).


---
**Page 92**
---

Component 1: Community resilience - enhanced resilience of livelihoods of the vulnerable communities through adapting to climate change sustainably (Total cost: USD 134.1 million; GCF cost: USD 12.7 million)

This component aims to improve the capacity of communities to effectively manage natural resources and improve their agricultural productivity for their livelihoods.

The component has two key outputs:

(a) Increasing the productivity and climate resilience of communities: The interventions to achieve this output include promoting agroforestry systems in the vulnerable area, applying ecosystem-based adaptation in constructing small nature-based water harvesting structures such as check dams, terraces, trenches, and trees; and promoting drought and flood tolerant varieties for hill districts and plain areas.

(b) Increasing water availability and water efficiency: This will be achieved through reconciling a water model for the entire GBR region; constructing and operating 300 micro-irrigation systems in the vulnerable area; promoting rainwater harvesting systems, including conservation ponds and water reservoirs; in addition to promoting water-efficient agricultural practices.

The proposed interventions under this component take ecosystem-based adaptation approach to enhance livelihood options for the vulnerable communities.


Component 2: Ecosystem resilience - strengthened climate resilience at ecosystems (Total cost: USD 15.15 million; GCF cost: USD 16.8 million)

This component focuses on restoring ecosystems to reduce the impacts of natural disasters and improve ecosystem services, thereby increasing the capacity of the communities to maintain and support climate resilient ecosystems.

The proposed interventions under this component include constructing climate resilient green infrastructure such as check dams, terraces, trenches, and trees; maintaining accessibility to rural areas; and biodiversity conservation activities, such as reforesting degraded lands. The GCF will provide technical assistance in creating a standard operating procedure and providing technical training to help the communities and the local government to continue interventions and maintain climate resilient ecosystems.


Component 3: Climate governance - strengthened climate governance and institutional framework (Total cost: USD 10 million; GCF cost: USD 2.85 million)

This component aims to address the existing constraints in the governance structure as well as the hydrological boundaries. Interventions will promote community-based mechanisms for planning, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting of components 1 and 2; create a knowledge management system that provides baseline data and information on ecosystem services and their values; and develop institutional frameworks for assessment (quantification, valuation and attribution) of ecosystem services and national accounting.

In addition to this component, the project aims to promote community-based planning through multi-stakeholder consultation. Baseline data and information for 50 basins will be acquired and utilized in developing national, provincial and local plans and regulatory framework development and operation.


Gender: environmental and social management framework (ESMF) and monitoring and evaluation components (Total cost: USD 18.6 million; GCF cost: USD 2.93 million)

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium-high
The GRB is subject to several climate change-related impacts. Changes in rainfall patterns are impacting the ecosystem services that the natural environment brings to the local population, including water availability and food security. The GRB also faces glacier lake outburst floods throughout the GRB region. The project addresses these environmental issues in an integrated manner from a community perspective of vulnerability of the local population.

The proposed project is an ecosystem-based adaptation measure for sustainable management of natural resources in the ORBL where the large majority of the population is dependent on small-scale agriculture to support their livelihoods. Climate change is affecting rainfall patterns and increasing the frequency of extreme weather events, making it more probable that inclement weather or weather-based natural disasters may occur. For the direct beneficiaries of this project, who depend upon the basin upon which their livelihoods are based and the impact potential for them is high.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium-high
The recent devolution of powers from the central to the provincial and local levels throughout Nepal necessitates institutional and human capacity-building to ensure that adequate governance is provided. The project will integrate climate change assessment in regional planning, development, and implementation processes at all levels. Appropriate medium- to long-term plans that are reflective of the changing conditions in the area.

The project will also provide a platform for dialogue between the government and the local population, with attention to the changing climate and its impacts, is only recently emerging in Nepal. The project will hold capacity to make consideration of climate change pervasiveness through the governance structures.

#### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High
The interventions of the project that will impact are all based on local ecological conditions as well as the skills and capacity of governmental institutions and those of the local communities. Due regard is given to the position of women and indigenous peoples in the area. When properly funded, the approach of the project can easily be extended to other comparable areas of Nepal.

The interventions of the project are expected to align with the fulfillment of the needs of the local population. The project ensures that the ecosystem services provided by specific activities strengthen the ecosystems such that the delivery of ecosystem services is ensured even under scenarios of climate change.


---
**Page 94**
---

3.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

25. Nepal is a least developed country placed in a challenging geography, with high risk of natural disasters. The earthquake that occurred major earthquake of April 2015 killed over 9,000 people and caused economic losses of approximately USD $5 billion. As such, Nepal has very limited fiscal space to build resilience against climate change impacts.

3.5 Country ownership

Scale: High

26. The funding proposal aligns with all the national policies on climate change and the nationally determined contributions submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change.

27. The funding proposal was developed with full consultation of all relevant stakeholders, including representatives of indigenous peoples.


3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: Medium-high

30. Interventions on ecosystem-based adaptation tend to be relatively highly efficient, but their efficiency is likely to remain very dependent on local conditions. It would not be possible to estimate ex ante. The economic analysis that provided by the accredited entity (AE) gives an economic internal rate of return (IRR) of 16 per cent. Therefore, it can be concluded that the project is likely to be cost effective.

30. The financial resources required for the implementation of the project are all provided by GCF in the form of a reimbursable proposal; 81 per cent of the total project costs re-financing of the remaining 19 per cent is in the form of in-kind contributions from the Government of Nepal, a local non-governmental organization and the AE.

While the project is generally aimed at improving the climate resilience of Nepal’s agricultural sector through the use of improved GGR, there is a possibility of some minor to moderate environmental and social risks or impacts associated with interventions as well as on the socioeconomic dynamics of various social groups.

The ESMP includes the screening process, procedural framework and guidelines for project implementation (other than) the identification of potential environmental and social risks and impacts; (ii) measures to avoid such risks and impacts are incorporated into the project design; (iii) mitigation measures that can reduce or eliminate possible impacts can be implemented. The ESMP likewise provides for the applicable policy and

# Gender policy

## 4.2 Gender policy

The proposal contains a gender assessment and a gender action plan, and therefore, it complies with the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism. The document indicates that Nepal has been improving the legal and institutional mechanisms to promote


---
**Page 96**
---

The assessment is compiled based on a desk review and stakeholder consultation with women’s self-help groups and community-based organizations and non-governmental organizations (NGOs). The gender analysis of the project area indicates that the division of labour between women and men is skewed, based on socially prescribed roles. Women are primarily responsible for household chores and unpaid care work, which often involve household and family responsibilities, yet their ability to equally participate in decision-making within those households and outside in the community is greatly limited.

The assessment shows that women make a significant contribution within their households by representing 80% of all farmers in agriculture; though this work lacks recognition and compensation. Even when their contribution is remunerated, there is a significant pay gap for the same work done by men. The majority (95%) of the total farms of Nepal are owned by women or are jointly owned. Male migration results in feminization of households, which further increases the burden on women to manage and maintain the farm.

The inability to manage and maintain the land leads to a reduced income from agriculture, leading to food insecurity, lack of access to clean water, sanitation, and energy, and livelihoods. The fact that many households depend solely on forests for their household energy needs also means that women are primarily responsible for the collection of firewood and fodder to fulfill their energy needs and for their livestock feed.

The assessments acknowledged the disproportionate effect of climate change on women in Nepal. The assessment acknowledges that in the GEF vulnerability is highly correlated with gender, caste, ethnicity, regional location, age, and education level. Women are more vulnerable due to an ingrained patriarchal socio-economic and political system combined with women’s lower socio-economic status, lack of access to resources, and their greater exposure to climate change and natural disasters.

The assessment also indicates that women-headed households and families face significant challenges in accessing land rights, financial resources, training and technology, and access to decision-making. The challenges illustrated above, in addition to negatively impacting women themselves, have a direct impact on the well-being of their children, household members, and other environmental challenges.


In addressing these challenges, the assessment also shows that women have the knowledge and understanding of what is needed to adapt to changing environmental conditions and to devise practical solutions. Women’s closeness to all use of natural resources also present significant opportunities for them to contribute significantly to their own, their family's and community's well-being and development.


The Gender Action Plan (GAP) is a key component of the project that addresses gender in a gender policy. The GAP provides a number of activities aimed at addressing the various challenges illustrated in the gender assessment. It contains indicators, targets, timelines, budget lines, and responsible persons for each activity to ensure the successful implementation of the GAP implementation. The GAP will be conducting a rapid social analysis in each site to establish the social context and identify key stakeholders who can contribute to the project. Women’s groups will be engaged in the project. The activities included in the GAP are geared towards targeting and ensuring representation of groups of vulnerable women, such as Dalit and women-headed

## 4.4 Fiduciary

1. The AL for the project is BCCN which will assume the implementation function in addition to oversight function of the project. As an AL, BCCN will be overall responsible for the project, including project preparation and implementation, financial management and procurement, as well as supervision and monitoring.
2. The technical quality assurance of the project while the IFCN Asia Regional Office (ARO) will be responsible for the project’s technical guidance and oversight in accordance with the project document.
3. As an AL, BCCN oversees project implementation in accordance with the project document and relevant laws and regulations. BCCN will ensure that all project activities are carried out by qualified personnel and through appropriate technical guidance to ensure that the appropriate technical quality is applied to all project activities. In addition, BCCN will be responsible for the overall supervision of the project through its ARO and global headquarters. BCCN will review the draft disbursement plans and distribute funds in accordance with the approved disbursement plan. The disbursement plan must be detailed and in line with a disbursement plan which it will approve and review annually. Supervision missions will be carried out on an annual basis to which oversight of project implementation and the budget will be reviewed at greater depth.
4. BCCN will establish a Project Management Unit (PMU) to ensure delivery of results. Funds will be transferred from GCF to MoF, according to the disbursement plan approved by the AL, in accordance with the financial agreement related to the project. In turn, BCCN will disburs e funds to MoF.
5. The AL for the project is MoF. An implementing agreement will be signed between BCCN and MoF to implement the project on behalf of GCF. An implementing partner agreement will also be signed between the IFCN Headquarters & ARo with MoF for outlining their respective roles in the project implementation. A Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established under the guidance of the PSC, whose main role is to provide overall direction to the project hosted by the Department of Forests and Soil Conservation, the Ministry of Forestry and Environment. The PMU will be responsible for all financial management activities undertaken by the EEs. It will be accountable for all fiduciary matters, including financial management, procurement, staff recruitment and coordination with BCCN on conditional disbursement to province and local government for the implementation of approved project activities (conditional grants), prepare annual plans and budgets, and coordinate between project entities.

### Results monitoring and reporting

As a cross-cutting project, the adaptation and forestry/land use interventions of the investment expects to benefit 835,647 direct and 1,052,950 indirect beneficiaries. Through coverage of the entire value chain, the project will also contribute to reducing deforestation by an estimated 847,250 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent reductions over a 20-year time horizon.

The funding proposal and logical framework appropriately apply GCF fund level (impact and outcome) results management framework and performance measurement frameworks. The proposed indicators are well-defined and have been triangulated with information from progress reporting on expected results in addition to integrating project performance monitoring measures looking at behavioral changes and policy/realizing environment transformations.

Regarding section E.1 of the funding proposal, overall the logical framework complies with GCF requirements.

The funding proposal theory of change could benefit from further details reflecting on the causal pathways at the project level in relation to the rationale (which are ideally tested for robustness) and the expected results. The theory should also include impact/data evidence generated to attribute changes to GCF investment and demonstrate reasons why the outcomes selected are the best and most cost-effective option(s) to resolve the barriers or problems, with evidence on how the project intends to deliver such gains.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP131

## Proposal name: Improving Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Communities and Ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin, Nepal

### Accredited entity: International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN)

#### Country/ies: Nepal

#### Project/programme size: Small

## Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: Moderately-High

1. **Nepal** is a mountainous land-locked country containing the high Himalayas. Nepal has a marked altitude gradient, with its summit of Mount Everest at 8,848 m and the Terai floodplain at 60 m above sea level. The country can be divided into four broad physiographic units: (a) the high Himalayan (mountain) region; (b) the middle hills; (c) the low-lying Terai plains; and (d) the coastal plain.

2. During winter, precipitation in the form of snow is stored primarily in the glaciers, which start to thaw when temperatures increase during spring and throughout summer. The glacial meltwater flows into rivers that provide water for irrigation, drinking, and other uses. The fresh water from springs and headwaters in the rivers supplies villages and towns located at lower altitudes as well as those in the Terai region.

3. The climate of Nepal varies widely both temporally and spatially. The average annual rainfall is 1,500 mm to 2,800 mm, with a range from as much as 3,345 mm across the country. The monsoon causes over 80% of annual rainfall to occur within a span of four to five months (June–September). This results in high runoff and sediment loads during the rainy season in the rivers. Simultaneously, altitude largely determines the geographic distribution of rainfall. Based on this, Nepal can be divided into three major regions: the high Himalayas; the middle hills; and the low-lying Terai plains.

4. Historically, Nepal’s economy has remained dependent on subsistence agriculture and forestry. Over the past few decades, tourism has started to contribute to the economy. The country is rich in biodiversity with a wide range of flora and fauna. However, it mainly produces agricultural products, as against low levels of export, which in turn has put significant pressure


---
**Page 103**
---

on foreign markets. It is claimed that insufficient production of key crops (such as rice and maize) to meet domestic demand may be attributed to the high import rates of staple crops in the country. During the past decade, agriculture exhibited a modest growth of 2.9 per cent, most of which was due to expansion of cropped area. The agricultural sector is highly dependent on rainfall including forests. Available literature indicates that the slow growth of the agriculture sector in recent years may be attributed to (a) climate change and its associated extreme weather conditions; (b) insufficient irrigation facilities; (c) unavailability of critical agricultural inputs such as fertilizers, seeds, etc.; and (d) lack of access to markets. It is also claimed that the early signs of a changing climate in Nepal has reduced the productivity of common crops.

1. An analysis using long-term meteorological data obtained from the national repository suggest that climate change has been occurring in Nepal. On average, temperatures in the country have risen by 2.5°C since 1977. Not only is there a strong interannual variability in the rate of change but also a significant increase in extreme weather events. The high-altitude regions exhibit the highest rate of increase in average surface temperature, which is perhaps why glaciers have been retreating at an alarming rate.

6. An analysis using long-term meteorological data (for the period 1971–2014) indicates that both the mean and the maximum temperatures are found to be increasing across the country. The duration of cold spells has decreased, while the duration of warm spells is increasing in Nepal. The diurnal temperature range is also found to have increased over time.

1. Nepal's rainfall significantly varies by climatic zone. For example, the Mustang district receives an average annual precipitation of 205 mm, whereas the Terai plains receive a rainfall of 3,435 mm in Pokhara. Nepal is highly influenced by monsoon rainfall, which generally arrives in June and continues until September. The duration of the monsoon season has also increased over time. Monsoon rainfall also exhibits spatial variability. While the north-western mountainous region receives 100% to 200% more precipitation than the Terai plains during the monsoon season, it receives less than 50% in the month during the monsoon season. The average Terai rainfall is estimated to vary between 2,000 and 3,400 mm.

6. Overall, with an exception in the high mountains, annual precipitation has been generally increasing in Nepal (during 1977–2014). However, a closer examination into the rain-influencing factors reveals that the rainfall pattern is changing over time. A closer analysis of units suggests that (a) winter precipitation is increasing in the high hills, while decreasing elsewhere; (b) the monsoon season has become shorter and less intense in the Terai plains (e.g., Kathmandu Valley, Terai); (c) the monsoon is becoming increasingly wetter; and (d) throughout all the physiographic units, the post-monsoon rainfall is decreasing. While declining rainfall in combination with increasing temperatures may lead to a decrease in water availability, the greatly increased monsoon rainfall is not only causing floods, but also creating conditions in the steep terrain that are conducive to landslides and soil erosion. The increase in extreme weather hazards such as seasonal droughts, monsoon floods and landslides are found to be having occurred over time.

6. A model-based analysis suggests that the mean annual temperature will continue to increase by 0.92°C by the 2030s and 1.3°C by the 2060s with respect to current averages. The


---
**Page 104**
---

state monitoring efforts have resulted in a forecast increase in annual precipitation of £1 and 7.9 per cent for the 2030s and 2060s, respectively. The annual rainfall for the high mountains is found to be greater, with an increase of to 2.6 and 9.5 per cent for the 2030s and 2060s, respectively. This forecast is in line with the findings from the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change Pathway R.5 scenario results in more dramatic increases in temperatures and levels of annual rainfall for the region.

The funding proposal therefore deals with a number of climate change related phenomena such as floods, landslides and droughts, with the focus on two such phenomena: rainfall-induced landslides and flash floods, specifically those of the latter which is related to lack of adequate moisture on topsoil.


1. In particular, the funding proposal considers the preparation and implementation of climate risk management strategies across all the interrelated catchments upstream and downstream of GBH at the landscape level. The final outcome of the funding proposal will be a set of recommendations for the development of a framework (political/administrative boundary) of land use to management at the river basin wide level underpinned by an understanding of the vulnerability of the region to climate change, sustainability of ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) strongly, or even fully, depends on a deep understanding of threats and risks regardless of whether these are climate change related or other type of risk. The proposed framework will be based on the vulnerability for each localities risk should be well analysed based on the climate change patterns of this particular region. In addition to the above, the specific characteristics of different climatic zones and sub-ecosystems take into consideration.


10. The rainfall in Nepal is found to exhibit high variability. There is a general understanding that the annual rainfall has been increasing slightly (increasing by less than 4 per cent) across the GBH districts. There has been a slight attention effect on this modelled increase of rainfall, which is attributed to the fact that there exists a wide range of rainfall variability across the GBH district. The same analysis finds a clear decline in the number of dry days and an increase in the number of wet days. This decline in the number of dry days clearly indicates that the episodes with sharp rainfall bursts have been on the rise, which is likely to be associated with increased frequency of heavy rain events casting on terrace fields, resulting in loss of production, and destruction of infrastructure, including road networks. Widespread landslides also lead to lots of losses in the high mountainous areas.


13. The analysis of consecutive wet days on the basis of observed data clearly indicates that in the high mountains and middle hills, the number of consecutive wet days has been increasing, while the same has been decreasing in the hills and the Terai area. From scientific literature, it is found that landslides are triggered by antecedent rainfall (also reported in the feasibility study). The increase in the number of consecutive wet days can be an inference that in the high mountains and middle hills, increased antecedent rainfall might have been triggering landslides.


16. The same analysis carried out on the basis of observed data sets also revealed that high-intensity rainfall episodes above a 99 percentile level have been on the rise, irrespective of altitude. This is consistent with the findings from the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change and the seasonal data suggest a rise in monsoon rainfall, a cause-and-effect relationship is found between the increase in the number of consecutive wet days (which is more pronounced in the high mountains and the upper parts of the middle hills) and also a chance of flooding during the monsoon. The same analysis also reveals that the number of dry days has been declining, and consecutive dry days to be on the rise across the GBH districts. This explains hydrological activity over the years.


---
**Page 105**
---

1. The funding proposal relies heavily on projection datasets and subsequent analyses. As these data are derived from computational model-based analysis, having a common boundary assumption that the climate forcing will increase with time (with an increase in atmospheric CO2 concentration) is commonly obtained (irrespective of model). The model-based outputs under the scenario of increased atmospheric CO2 concentrations and temperature increases (signifying the 2030s) are used for the estimation of evapotranspiration, which is found to increase by ~7 per cent. This will lead to a decline in the productivity of natural grasslands, which will in turn adversely affect livestock production.
16. The funding proposal also presents a projection on crop-specific evapotranspiration. Projected future crop evapotranspiration is found to be higher for all the crops compared with respective historical data. The projected increase in evapotranspiration and declining available water content will tend to adversely affect crop yields. The analysis of the project shows that the farmers are likely to experience such phenomena as experienced by the farmers in different physiographic regions.
17. References are made to a number of past projects. The International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN) has been implementing three climate change adaptation-related projects in Nepal, where EBA has been tested. The funding proposal presents an analysis on the impact potential of the project by comparing it with other projects identifying the efficacy of the projects as adaptation measures. A few recent projects using EBA have indicated that the government of Nepal is consider EBA as part of its climate change adaptation strategy.
18. The project will directly benefit 1,963,045 vulnerable households out of a total 12,715,586 households in the target districts. Out of these beneficiaries, approximately 65 per cent (i.e., 65 per cent of the beneficiaries are women). The beneficiaries represent 1,000 villages and 394 wards. The project will directly benefit agricultural output, involving 6,133 million hectares of land and forest out of 8,285 million hectares of forest land. The project will directly improve the management of $4,800 hectares of forest through community-based forest management (CBFM) activities in the target context beneficiary households. The project will be implemented in seven years from inception, at a total cost of approximately $137.2 million.
19. The impact of a project is often determined by the adequacy of the selection of target area(s) and target beneficiary groups. The number of beneficiaries in different vulnerable districts varies significantly (some 40 per cent representing districts with high vulnerability). The population (some 84 per cent) representing districts with medium vulnerability) is targeted, it is claimed that this ensures a fair distribution of benefits among the most vulnerable communities and the direct beneficiaries. In the selection process, fairness may be ensured by cross validation in the field and through community consultation.
20. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) Hinds merit in the funding proposal. In view of the above analyses, the independent TAP is of the opinion that the impact potential of the project is moderately high.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Moderately-High

2. In recent times, Nepal introduced its new constitution and the provisions therein clearly emphasize greater exercise of power through the empowerment of local area-specific


---
**Page 106**
---

### GEF-UNDP/PAM/03

Page 198

institutions at the grass-root level, shying away from its century-old monarchy based centralized power exercise. In managing ecosystems and provisioning disaster risk reduction related services, local area-specific instructions are given to rules in the governance process. The proposed river basin approach is expected to be a paradigm shift in political boundary approach to a river basin approach, which is expected to be guided by coordination and cooperation among various stakeholders at all levels. This is considered to be a paradigm shift in the governance process. If the legal and institutional barriers are addressed properly, such a shift will have to be regarded as inevitable.

The new governance framework will address accountability and the established governance processes are not accustomed to dealing with a completely new governance mechanism. No piloting of the river basin approach is envisaged at this stage. The government of Nepal parliamentary government is eager to pilot the new governance mechanism to give EBA a test run towards a holistic development approach involving a river basin. It is premature to say whether or not the proposed river basin approach will be successful in Nepal, but it is clear that EBA governance will integrate holistic and systems understanding of the complexity of issues governing ecosystems and their services.

The project will develop a comprehensive GRI management framework underpinned by EBA that will spell out governance arrangements and financing mechanisms and address legal gaps, including those related to land use planning. The project will also support the preparation of sub-watershed-level plans with relevant local government authorities and water users associations. These plans will be based on an ecosystem-based approach which will correct the prevailing political and/or administrative boundary-based design and implementation of development projects. This will lead to a more integrated view of the upstream and downstream parts of a common and shared river basin. Moreover, the issues of climate change and its impacts are also addressed in the project through the promotion of sustainable ecosystem-based goods and services, keeping the conservation concerns at the core.

This project is aimed at initiating this change in paradigm. There is political will to integrate EBA into the existing governance framework. The government has already adopted EBA as a response to climate change, since it has been successfully tested and adopted elsewhere. The project will support the development of an integrated river basin approach and considers EBA as a viable model to deliver holistic adaptation. In the micro-scale testing of EBA in Nepal, several elements of EBA have shown early "no regret" successes regarding water resources management and climate change adaptation through adaptive actions using EBA which indicates replication potential. Financing arrangements to trigger replication will be developed for the project. However, it is important to note that country-specific institutions remain to be worked out and are subject to endorsement of the draft national policy on EBA. The level of institutional capacity in Nepal is considered high.

For Nepal, EBA with adequate institutional back-up is fairly innovative and if the approach succeeds by involving beneficiary communities and other stakeholders, it will gradually become a part of the existing governance framework. This is important since it is a prerequisite for a shift in the existing policy regime. One of the early actions of the project, if approved, would be to develop an enabling national policy on EBA. It is expected that necessary political will may be mobilized through advocacy and a RBD-friendly policy may be endorsed through the National Assembly.

The project has given adequate attention to learning and knowledge-sharing. Not only will the farmers be provided with training on EBA and adaptive capacity-building on climate-

1.3 Sustainable development potential

**Scale: Moderately-High**

Any development project, with or without clear linkage with climate change, is supposed to contribute towards achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The exception: it is expected to contribute to a number of SDGs. For example, the project will contribute to SDG 1 (No Poverty), SDG 2 (Zero Hunger), and SDG 3 (Good Health and Well-being). The focus on women’s participation suggests that the project will directly contribute to achieving gender equality and hence SDG 5. The project is likely to contribute to reducing poverty among small farmers, which in turn will provide a major contribution in terms of achieving good health and well-being of communities, which falls under SDG 3.

The project is expected to deliver economic co-benefits by strengthening farmers’ capacity to address environmental concerns in farming practices. The establishment of EBA is expected to contribute towards the reduction of food importation at the national level, thereby saving valuable foreign currency for the country. Moreover, by contributing to economic sub-sectors such as agriculture and agro-processing industries, the project will provide employment opportunities, which in turn will provide livelihood opportunities for the beneficiary communities. Additional benefits may be arrived at a retail level through the sale of agricultural produce from small farmers, which is likely to be a major contributor to Nepal’s economy. By design, the project will create greater employment and income opportunities for women, who are expected to play an increased role in small enterprises.

It is expected that by enhancing household-level food security, the project will contribute towards the reduction of food importation at the national level, thereby saving valuable foreign currency for the country. Moreover, by contributing to economic sub-sectors such as agriculture and agro-processing industries, the project will provide employment opportunities, which in turn will provide livelihood opportunities for the beneficiary communities. Additional benefits may be arrived at a retail level through the sale of agricultural produce from small farmers, which is likely to generate an increased amount of revenues for the government (in the order of USD 4 million per year).

The core of an EBA is the conservation, strengthening and reconstitution of ecosystems through improved management. Therefore, any EBA-type project directly helps in strengthening the capacity of communities to manage their natural resources.

(a) Increased forest and vegetation cover will result from new plantations and the regeneration of forests, restoration of degraded lands and improved forest management practices.
(b) Forest biodiversity is expected to directly benefit from improved forest cover;
(c) Soil conservation, especially in hilly terrain, will be brought about by direct measures to reduce soil erosion; 
(d) The project is expected to contribute to the management of wetlands and water systems, which might also trigger enhanced retention of groundwater;
(e) Assuming that agricultural productivity will be enhanced by the various interventions, the project may also contribute towards reducing illegal wildlife activities in natural areas;


---
**Page 108**
---

## (Through forest conservation, augmentation and regeneration, the project will contribute directly to the sequestration of carbon in forests.)

32\. A range of social benefits is also likely to be accrued by the project. By contributing to household food security, the project is likely to reduce malnutrition and enhance social well-being, especially for women and children. The project will provide new opportunities for income generation in agro-forestry, ecotourism and the establishment of small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in the target areas. This will contribute significantly towards reducing poverty in the target areas. In this process, it is also expected that the overall socioeconomic status of women and children will be enhanced.

33\. The project will provide a platform for the revival of out-migrated male farmers by providing them with land tenure security and access to credit facilities. This will enable them to come back to their ancestral villages, thereby contributing to social harmony. In the absence of males in impoverished households, the women face various types of social discrimination including domestic violence. The project will provide a platform for the revival of these men who will bring social harmony back to the target village.

34\. The project will contribute towards the empowerment of marginalized groups by involving women and poor and marginalized communities, including different castes and ethnic groups. By offering various institutional services and training, the project will help to build capacity among these groups for their better participation in forest management activities.

35\. The project promotes to integrate gender concerns into the design and implementation of conservation and development activities through a gender action plan (GAP) as part of its activities. There is a gender and social inclusion action plan which highlights as many as 35 actions to address gender and ethnic issues. As indicated above, the project will directly contribute towards reducing poverty in the target areas by providing women with better land productivity and employment will provide women with greater opportunities. Similarly, it is expected that the project will also contribute significantly towards improving forest ecosystem management processes, which will empower women in the target areas.

36\. The project has established direct linkages with the delivery of enhanced climate actions towards addressing the impacts of climate change on forests and related issues while these issues will be addressed, while efforts will be made to avoid exacerbating floods occurring during high-intensity rainfall events in the target areas.

37\. The emphasis of the project is on policy uptake and strong commitment towards bringing (a rather decisive) change to governance practices from a business as usual arena-specifically, the establishment of Reforestation and Rehabilitation Investment Funds. The scale of change provides an explanation regarding modalities on how this will be achieved and how the existing institutional arrangements will be changed. It is expected that the project will establish RRFs beyond the lifetime of the project still remain hypothetical. The policymaking process will provide a platform for gaps on an urgent basis so that the sustainability of the proposed RRFs will be beyond doubt.

37\. In view of the above discussions on sustainable development, the potential to contribute to it appears moderately high.


### 1.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

Nepal and its poor population are highly vulnerable to climate change. Nepal's economy is largely dependent upon agriculture, which provides for one-third of the gross domestic product and employment of two-thirds of the labour force of the country. In recent years, agricultural


---
**Page 109**
---

production has occurred steadily, at a rate of 2.9 per cent per year. Such a growth rate is even lower than the already low 3.8 per cent growth rate in the economy reached during the past decade. Since a large majority of the Nepalese population, especially in the mountains, hills and the Terai region, depends on agriculture for their livelihoods, this has had significant implications for inequality, leaving smallholder farmers in a marginal economic state. It may be argued that rising food vulnerability has been partly responsible for the poor performance in agricultural production in Nepal.

The country's mountains have experienced considerable changes in temperature over the past decade. However, the relationship between these changes in temperature indices and crop production at different altitudes is not fully understood. The impact of climate change on the cropping pattern of smallholder farmers with respect to the crop system also is not available (and/or presented). Moreover, there has been a gradual limitation of agricultural production due to the large-scale outmigration of rural population labour force from agriculture. Therefore, it becomes clear understanding of the cause and effect relationship of climate-related factors and parameters with crop production in Nepal will be helpful for formulating appropriate policies and avoid issues related to agricultural production cannot be discounted.

There is no denying that smallholders of Nepal, irrespective of their location within GGR or elsewhere, are facing a number of challenges, including pressing issues related to improvement in agricultural productivity. Women producers, despite receiving occasional remittance, do not have access to credit facilities and other inputs for improving their livelihoods. They also do not have advantage of growing value chains. They also lack technical knowledge regarding most types of irrigation systems and water management practices. However, it is argued that the accredited entity will seek technical and management support with regard to these issues.

The entire country, on the other hand, is going through an institutional transformation process. The emphasis of the new constitution is towards gradual devolution of power from the centre to the local level institutions. This has led to a strengthening of the federal structure in governance processes. However, there is a dearth of institutional capacity, which is often reflected by the lack of effective implementation of policies and programmes at the local level institutions. This is why many of the provisions under the current policy regime cannot be implemented, despite all the political will that has been already transparent through political decision-making. The same applies to the financial sector as well. There are significant challenges in financial aspects. Nepal's need for institutional strengthening and the capacity to implement development projects is very high.

Since the creation of common goods is primarily the responsibility of the Government of Nepal and there is no direct private sector large scale financing for this, international financing appears to be a necessity. However, there have been significant delays due to the significant financial barriers of an LCR in the creation of public goods at the river basin level and the area where the project will be implemented. This has led to a delay in starting adaptation in an area which is particularly vulnerable to climate change.

The project offers significant opportunities to strengthen institutions operating at different levels, including the local government, and to build capacity for inclusion of EBA. The need for such institutional strengthening is recognized and the project embraces activities to address these capacity building needs.

In view of the above discussions, the independent TAP recognizes that the needs of the recipient are high.


---
**Page 110**
---

1. **Country ownership**
   - Scale: High

2. Quite a few national policies and strategies in Nepal link climate change and related responses. However, it is identified as a sector likely to be impacted adversely by climate change and related phenomena.
3. Nepal is the first country in the world to advocate the concept of developing a local adaptation framework of action (LAFA) at the national level. It involves local-level implementation of LAFA which highlights the importance of the global level as the way forward, delivering participatory community-based adaptation. However, the concept of offering holistic adaptation solutions at the local level is still in its infancy and requires further development.
4. The policy framework for climate change adaptation depends on the mobilization of political will around setting up RBO and its functioning following the integrated approach to climate change adaptation.

5. Following the emergence of parliamentary democracy in Nepal, the country formed a new ministry to look after affairs regarding environment and climate change. The institutional arrangements were further strengthened by the Environment Protection Act (2014), the Climate Change (2009) and the Climate Change Policy (2011). The Strategic Programme on Climate Resilience (SPCR) was formulated in 2013, which is a comprehensive framework for climate-resilient agricultural development. There are various acts and policies that are operational at different levels of governance. These include the Forest Act (2074), the GBM level, which include the Environment Protection Act (1996), the Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Act (2058) and the National Strategic Framework (2015). The project is found to be in harmony with the existing legal and policy framework of Nepal.

6. - The proposed programme closely aligned with the Government of Nepal's national priorities for enhancing the resilience of communities and ecosystems.
7. The system is aligned with relevant national policies, such as the National Adaptation Programme (NAPA), LAPA and the Climate Change Policy. Management of climate change induced extreme weather events such as droughts, floods and landslides is included in the national policy framework. However, there are no specific provisions for ecosystem enhancement and restoration.
8. Despite the mentions of ecosystem enhancement and restoration in various policies for the optimization of the use of renewable natural resources, EBA has not been considered in the policy regime as the primary and/or preferred delivery mechanism. There is therefore a need to develop an enabling environment that will facilitate the development of potential primary delivery modality.
9. The local-level institutions are not yet fully prepared to deliver adaptation through ecosystem-based approaches. This is due to the lack of a dedicated programme and potential involvement of the local government units in the proposed participatory approach.

10. - The alternative governance mechanism is yet to be developed and subsequently endorsed by the policy makers.
11. EBA is not yet among the industry best practices for mainstream climate change adaptation, as it is still at an experimental stage.
12. The knowledge management efforts must be enhanced through the project to build confidence on EBA to deliver adaptation in Nepal. This will ensure that there are no barriers and a viable modality to deliver adaptation. Following the endorsement of the draft policy, the policy disconnect will not be eliminated.

13. - While climate change is being in Nepal for many decades, its role in ecosystem management, biodiversity restoration and enhancement and natural resources management in Nepal is well recognized.
14. The country has a long history of using EBA to deliver adaptation solutions focused on nature-based solutions, despite having a slight policy disconnect regarding EBA and


---
**Page 111**
---

adaptation to the community level. Past and ongoing IUCN projects trialing HCA have enabled it to build significant capacity to implement climate change adaptation projects in Nepal. However, IUCN has not excelled in providing irrigation support to communities to compensate for their water scarcity problems. The project will be able to address this gap by drawing on the local level expertise will be useful in addressing this apparent weakness. The coordination bodies at the delivery level might be able to fill any deficiency.

In addition to engaging local-level actors and enhancing their capacities, the project relies heavily on participatory processes to plan and deliver local-level adaptation. In the process of planning and implementation, it is expected that a wide range of stakeholders, including government- and local-level institutions, local civil society actors and agencies, will be involved in the project. The project has received a letter of support from the national designated authority. Moreover, it is claimed that the national designated authority has been involved in the development phases of the project. The project has gone through several rounds of review by the national designated authority. These consultations will be key during the implementation of the project, especially at the formative stages.

In view of the above analysis, it appears that the general country ownership is high.


### 1.6 Efficiency and Effectiveness

#### Scale: Medium-to-high

53. The project's budget is estimated at USD 32.715 million, of which the GCF contribution is pledged to be USD 4.089 million (12 per cent of the total). The remaining (USD 28.626 million) will be in kind contributions from the national designated authority as well as other partners (16 per cent). The co-financing ratio is at 1:0.19. The GCF contribution is sought as grant. As Nepal is a member of the GEF, it is eligible for co-financing.

54. The implementation of nature-based adaptation solutions will cost USD 18.0 or 28.6 per beneficiary. The cost for making the natural ecosystem and agro-ecosystem resilient will be in the order of USD 37.0 per beneficiary, which is a very high cost.

55. The investment efficiency is calculated on the basis of considering the average life of trees planted at 20 years to determine the lifespan of the project and taking into account a discount rate of 10 per cent, which is typical for an LAC. With the project, the net present value will be USD -835 million (USD -497 million in case of co-financing). The investment project is financially stable. The project is expected to come to the break-even point in 11 years.

The financial internal rate of return for this scenario is estimated at 20 per cent, and the present value will be USD 835 million. The financial internal rate of return in such a scenario is reduced by co-financing from 497 per cent to 26 per cent. However, it should be noted that the overall project is financially viable. With the project, the economic net present value if estimated at 10.8 GEF units per beneficiary and USD -35 million in case of co-financing. The internal rate of return will be 21.361 per cent. All these estimations lead to the conclusion that the project is financially and economically on a strong footing.

56. Ecosystem-based adaptation has succeeded successfully in many parts of the world. It has also been pilot tested in Nepalese conditions. However, it remains questionable whether EBA is among the industry standard solutions for addressing water scarcity problems, especially in mountainous terrains such as GBR. The limited number of initiatives considered in the recent past by IUCN indicates that, by virtue of technical simplicity, lower unit cost per person and gender-friendliness, the EBA options fare well in comparison with other alternative adaptation strategies.

57. Community-based adaptation experimentations are ongoing. It is anticipated that the project, if


---
**Page 112**
---

approved, will foster confidence among practitioners to invest more on soft EBA-based adaptations under various Nepalese conditions.

57. The independent TAP finds efficiency and effectiveness as medium to high.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

58. The independent TAP recommends that the Board approve the project.

# Gender documentation for FP131

Improving climate resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin, Nepal


## Annex B: Gender Analysis and Gender Action Plan

### Part I: Gender Analysis/Assessment: Guide (Project/Program Level)

As an AE, IUCN will follow its gender policy named "Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment" to mainstream gender-responsiveness within the IUCN programme of work* available [here](#).

**Gender issues to be addressed**

- **Maternal mortality:** In Nepal, the maternal mortality ratio is 258 deaths per 100,000 live births, which is one of the highest in Asia. The government's Second Long-Term Health Plan (1993–2017) gives high priority to improving neonatal and maternal health outcomes and includes many interventions to improve access for the poorest and most excluded, including a safer motherhood program, a community-based health volunteer system, which is quite successful.
- **Major health problems specific to women:** are prepubertal uterine atrophy, under nutrition and anemia due to heavy workloads, combined with inadequate access to nutritious food during menstrual cycles and post-delivery. This situation is prevalent in the far western areas, as well as in some parts of the Terai region where the majority of the population lives below the poverty line or even the overall poverty level is very high. One study estimated that more than 600,000 women in Nepal are suffering from anemia.
- **Child mortality:** The child mortality rate is broken into three different stages i.e., neonatal, infant and under five mortalities. Whereas the neonatal mortality means the probability of a child dying within its first week of life; infant mortality refers to the probability of a child dying during the first year of life and under five mortality means the probability of a child dying within the first five years of life. The rate of neonatal mortality is 21 deaths per 1000 live births, infant mortality is 53 deaths per 1000 live births and under-five mortality is 74 deaths per 1000 live births in the five year period from 2011-2016.
- **Neonatal and infant mortality:** In ORB, neonatal mortality is 18 deaths per 1000 live births, infant mortality is 35 deaths per 1000 live births and under-five mortalities is 27 deaths per 1000 live births for the ten-year period (2007 – 2017) (MoH 2017). The under five mortality rates for both boy and girl children in the same - 34 %, which shows a better survival status and non-discrimination for girl child in terms of survival in this area (Table 1).

### Population growth
Out of GRBS total human population of about 5.13 million 46% are male and 54 % female. The male to female ratio is 0.95:1. The population increased at an average annual rate of 1.87 per cent during the period 2001-2011, which is higher than national average of 1.41 which is less than national average of 4.8. Two mountain districts, Manang and Mustang, have the lowest number of households and female population.

### Education
The share of total enrolment for girls is 50.4 % at primary level, 90.8 % at lower secondary education and 62.7% at upper secondary education. This shows that the gender parity has been achieved in basic education, including primary and lower secondary education (1:02 at primary school). However, there are still significant differences between boys and girls with a huge disparity among boys and girls in terms of dropout rates from secondary school education. The reasons for this include poor infrastructure, lack of access to quality economic condition and poverty is the main reason boys drop out. Both boys and girls from the well-off families tend to go to school but in very few cases of school enrolment are found among the poor families.

### Literacy
However, in terms of adult literacy rate, there is wide gender gap where 75 % of males and only 57 % of females are literate. As per 14+ plan "the literacy of 15-24 age groups of 86.6 %. This shows that the literacy level among young people has improved significantly for both men and women. Mostly girls and women only completed the secondary level of school and after that they will not continue their education further at higher level. The male literacy rate is remarkably higher (75%) than female literacy rate which is 57%. The difference in adult literacy, however, lower than national average difference which is 75 % for male and 57% for female.

# Improving climate resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin, Nepal

## Poverty According to the latest 2018 Multidimensional poverty report of the GoN National Planning Commission (NPC), 25.6% of Nepal's population is multidimensionally poor. This means that their income has halved its Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) from 59 % in 2006–2007 to 31% in 2018, based on ten indicators which contributed to the improvement. For the first time this report enables the MPI to be disaggregated by province and district. The provinces of Nepal formed seven provinces with the highest rate of multidimensional poverty – with every second person being multi dimensionally poor (54 %) followed by Provinces 5 and 7 (approximately 30 % being poor) [NPC, 2018]. Some of the GRB areas are in Province 6 and Province 7.

### Figure 1: Map of Nepal showing location of provinces

#### Income level
The overall per capita income on the basis is NRs.49,362 per annum, which is lower than the national average of NRs.58,780. The average human poverty index (HPI) of the GRB in this study was 0.18 with a standard deviation of 0.05. Province 7 has the highest HPI for all ethnic groups in the basin was 0.482, compared to national average of 0.490 The HPI of Province 6 is 0.398 and Province 5 is 0.314. The provinces are ranked as follows: Districts - Dhangadhi, Rasuwa, Nuwakot, Mustang, and Gorkha (is very high in Dhangadhi). Kaski is the district with lowest HPI of 0.258 followed by Province 7 (0.314), Province 6 (0.398), Rasuwa is poorest amongst all districts with the highest HPI of 0.422, followed by Nuwakot with a HPI of 0.356, Gorkha with an HPI of 0.338 and Mustang 31.16 [NPC & UNDP, 2014].

#### Nutritional status
The situation of under nutrition and malnutrition of girls is not as severe as for females in general. Girls are more likely to be malnourished than boys because they have less access to food or roads and markets and have better food security. Nevertheless, there are pockets of poverty and certain groups who are at risk of being left behind. These include women and children, especially those who are generally poor, not educated and live in remote areas. Girls get lower less priority in terms of education and health care compared to boys. They marry early, have fewer marriage, and are not given any priority for education below primary level, or health care.

In terms of undernutrition/malnutrition, a 2017 article in the daily newspaper Kantipur reported that in recent years, there has been an increase in the number of deaths among children aged five and even dying due to gender-based discrimination in food distribution and lack of timely health care for girl children. Very heavy workloads of rural and mountain women are also a major cause due to male migration. Women have no time or money to buy food, pay for education, or take care of their children. Women have no time to look after their children and themselves. This situation is contributing to the poverty cycle in which women are trapped.

#### Gender Gap Index (GGI): The GGI for Nepal is 0.661, and Nepal ranks 110 out of 144 countries measured. Nepal women are progressing in terms of political representation they rank 88 out of 151; in education, they rank 92 out of 137; in health, they rank 101 out of 160; and in economic and educational attainment (123) they are ranking far behind their global women counterparts.

Political participation: Traditionally, Nepali women have played a limited role in political leadership. In recent years the rate of women's political engagement is gradually increasing as the government has issued a guide for women's representation but it is still not sufficient. Nepal held its first parliamentary elections in 2013 and second parliamentary elections in the year 2017, which is a historic milestone for the country as it was held after almost three decades of political transition from monarchy to federal democratic republic with national leadership. But still representation of women in political parties is low, especially at the higher levels of party leadership. According to the election commission (EC) guidelines, 30% of candidates should be women and 50% of the total positions should be filled by women. The EC has also laid down a rule that at least one-third of the elected representatives in each local unit must be women. However, the EC has not made any specific provision for equal representation of men and women in party nominations. As per this rule, 91% of the deputy positions (deputy mayors in municipalities and vice chairpersons in rural municipalities) were won by women, but men won 96% of chief positions (mayor in municipalities and chairperson in rural municipalities). Women won only 20% of the total positions. Furthermore, there were no quota for women for local level positions i.e.

## Legal Status of Women in Nepal

Internationally, Nepal has made strong commitment to human rights and ratified almost all major international human rights conventions, including CEDAW in 1991. Nepal signed the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (CRPD) at the World Summit Conference on Environment and Development (UNCED) in Rio de Janeiro in June 1992. Nepal ratified the CRPD on March 30, 2007. The National Human Rights Commission Act was promulgated on the 31st of July 1994. Therefore, it is a duty of the Government of Nepal to implement the provisions of the Convention and other international human rights instruments by incorporating them in national protection mechanisms as per the article 9(1) of the Treaty Act 1990.

Nepal has been making legal and institutional improvements for the promotion of women's rights, by establishing protection mechanisms such as National Human Rights Commission and National Women Commission. However, despite these efforts, women continue to be discriminated due to weak enforcement of laws, policies and plan of action, and poor performance of state machinery. The main reasons are lack of political will, inadequate resources for effective governance, but mostly because of state's unwillingness to invest enough resources for the protection and promotion of human rights.

The Gender Inequality Index of the 2018 UNDP indicates that the inequality between men and women achievements are known as reproductive health, measured by maternal mortality ration and adolescent birth rate. Nepal ranks 135th out of 149 countries in terms of reproductive health status occupied by females and proportion of adult females and males aged 25 years and older with at least some secondary education; and economic status expressed as labour market participation rates for women and men, female headed households, and the proportion of people aged 15 years and older in which Nepal ranks in 118th position out of 100 countries in global scenario. The Human Development Index (HDI) and Gender Inequality Index (GII) shows that Nepal falls under the category of medium human development ranking 149th position out of 189 countries in the world with lowest GDI among South Asian countries.

Still, the status of Nepalese women lags far behind that of men- low levels of access to education and health services, lack of economic opportunities, gender-based violence, among others, discriminatory social institutions, social norms, and practices are the main hindering factors in restricting women's access to opportunities, resources and power (Banerji, 2013). Gender discrimination is a major barrier for women’s empowerment. It manifests itself across the life cycle in different forms (Pukhrel, 2008) and entrenched in the political, economic and social spheres of society.

Improving climate resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin, Nepal

The population census conducted in 2011 has shown that 21% of the households in Nepal are women-headed as compared with the total households living in poverty (25.16%). The percentage of female-headed households is highest in the Eastern Region (37%) with approximately 41% of these households deriving their income from agricultural wages.

Despite this central role in agriculture, women face structural constraints in relation to land tenure rights. CEDAW[1] reports that women's access to land in Nepal is consequently limited. Women are often denied inheritance and ownership of land by the male members of the family. In addition, female-headed households average only 0.5 ha of farmland, compared with an average of 2.3 ha for male-headed households. The Eastern Region has the lowest percentage of women-headed households in Nepal: 21% of households in the Eastern Region, 25.5 % in the Tarai's Mid-West Region and only 40% in the Western Region. In addition, the number of female-headed households in registration files when land is recorded in the name of a woman has increased the number of such households.

For instance, in all communities of Nepal land is inherited from the father to the son. In the 2001 Census, only about 11% of households reported any land in female legal ownership. Only seven % recorded homeownership of livestock. Overall, women own less than one-third of the total agricultural land and livestock (WB and DFID, 2006)[2]. Thus, women are discriminated against when it comes to inheritance as well as property rights in general.

Recent amendments to the Country Code of Nepal have also improved women's access to property other than land. Unmarried daughters now have the right to ancestral property (other than land) until they marry and then only if they do not receive a dowry or are below the age of 35. However, there are ongoing restrictions in relation to women's independent use of their property and their ability to require to received permission from a male relative before disposing of any immovable property[3].

The Gender Equity Act (2006) advanced the property rights of women even further and gives equal property right on ancestral property to son and daughters. It removed and amended discriminatory laws that denied women's access to land, housing and other assets. 17% of households, land or house or both in the name of female member of the household (CBS, 2012)[4].

Most households in the GRB have landholdings of more than 3 ha. Moreover, only 5 % of households have landholdings of more than 2 ha. This shows that the land highly fragmented. Furthermore, the data also reveals that in some districts have landless households with Syangja is having the highest landless households.

Beliefs, Perceptions and Stereotypes related to Gender

In the project area across mountains, hills and Terai (plains), beliefs, perception and stereotypes related to gender are similar. However, there are location-specific differences. In the mountain areas they are more aware of climate change risk and adaptation compared to the plains where agriculture-related activities, hotel business, livestock rearing and agriculture. Men share household activities of cooking and child care.

This pattern is similar in hills areas. Women in hilly areas are more literate and due to relatively easy access to radios, mobiles, electricity, phone radio, television, to some extent internet, and proximity to urban cities and exposure to the outside world hence they are much aware about climate change risk and adaptation compared to women in the hills there are remote areas and poverty pockets where women are discriminated against.

In the Terai certain caste groups (e.g. Chepangas, Tharus, Mahaj, Mushars, and Madhesia) practice gender equality and women have more decision-making power within their households than in the GURB that women are caretakers and nurturers and their role is more appropriate within the household. Women in these communities are more aware of climate change risk and adaptation due to radio, FM TV, and mobile phone, even literate women are becoming more aware. Their engagement in various community groups helps them to understand about climate change risk and adaptation.

Division of Labour

In Nepal, the division of labour is based on socially prescribed gender roles. Men do more of the outside work, political work, trading and marketing, and paid jobs, whereas women are more engaged in domestic work, child care, and unpaid agricultural activities.

The historical gender roles, beliefs and stereotypes of the 'public' male breadwinner (provider) and 'private' female care-giver are taken in the current changing situations. A patriarchal culture dominates in Nepal where men have more decision-making power within their households which is given little status while the ability of men to earn money brings respect, and their traditional role of provider gives them higher economic and social status. Women's decision-making role and control over resources is low in most communities. The division of labour between men and women, expenditure and education are men's business and women can exert little or no influence over the cultural norms that govern gender relations.

Women in Nepal are increasingly engaged in paid work, but they are engaged or spend most of the time in the household and agricultural activities and is often unpaid. Women's major sphere of operation is within households. Women's role in society is mainly in care sector, child-rearing, food preparation, cooking, cleaning, laundry, childcare, education and household maintenance. Women have important roles as primary land, water and natural resources users. The responsibility for the use and management of forest and other natural resources is the responsibility of women and control over the resources is the responsibility of men. The large portion of unpaid work is assigned to women and girls, especially collecting
