# Improving climate resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin, Nepal

## Firewood, water, fodder, and caring for livestock, crops, and households (child care, cooking food)

Due to migration of mostly male members in search of better employment abroad, rural labour force is getting feminized. It has further increased burden of household chores on women's shoulders. Women are now responsible for managing the entire household including agricultural lands are abandoned. As women are taking care of the households and agriculture, women and their dependents (children and elderly people) living in rural area deriving their income from agriculture and livestock.

In some ethnic groups and social classes, women's roles in the economy goes beyond the domestic sphere as when Thakali women are involved in the hotel and catering business, or when Dangi women are engaged in the construction of houses. Women also participate in work from labouring households and from the so called occupational castes work as field labourers or porters of others.

In the poor households, ploughing, roofing, climbing the trees to top the fodder, threshing rice, sowing, making bamboo baskets and bamboo mattress, manufacturing agricultural implements etc. are the major tasks that the women have to perform. Threshing miller and grader, grinding maize and millet, cutting and winnowing of crops, cooking food for family members and other people's works. Digging, wedding, harvesting and carrying load are common for both sexes. Mothers usually take care of children and elderly people while the men go out for work using money earned by grandmother, sister or elder brother (retrieved from https://www.humanrightswatch.org/node/10238).

## Participation in Formal and Informal Economy

The Gross Domestic Product (GDP) of Nepal is USD 20.88 billion and GDP per capita USD 2.313 (NBS -2011)**. The average annual economic growth rate from 2012-2016 was 1.40 %. The Gini Coefficient which measures income inequality was 32.8 in 2014 (ADB, 2017).

In Nepal's 14th Year period plan (2015-2019), an average annual growth rate of GDP of 7.2 % was set as the target for the next five years. The plan also sets out a number of goals such as gender equality, an inclusive society, environmental protection and capacity development of different groups.

Agriculture and tourism are two major sectors of the economy. Remittances are significantly contributing to the economy in the GRL, as number of migrant household from this area is highest among all districts in Nepal.

Nepali girls and women work for more than boys and men, spending 25 % to 50 % more time on households' tasks, economic and agricultural activities. Yet, due to the nature of women's work which are often unpaid, on the one hand, and the flawed development indicators, on the other hand, women's participation is not specifically included. Women are the more orthodox Hindu communities who are largely confined to domestic and subsistence production in agriculture and livestock rearing. However, there are some exceptions that exist in the Tibet-Burman communities where women participate actively in the market economy.

The role played by women in the care sector, predominantly their reproductive work, bearing, nursing, child-rearing, feeding, education and health care is not recognized as a part of the national accounting systems. While these activities are crucial for household members' well-being.

Migration is also increasing, with official figures reaching 11,937 in 2008 and 45,600 in 2013. The majority of these are young women who migrate for work as given the numbers migrating without official permission (ADB, 2015). It has been estimated that 1 million women have migrated since 1970 as migrants. The Foreign Employment Act, 2007 aims to protect the rights of workers and professionals.

## Situation of Women and Men in the Specific Sector of Intervention

In Nepal, agriculture accounts for nearly 70 per cent of total employment and contributes nearly one-third of Nepal’s gross domestic product (GDP). Women in Nepal constitute 72.8 per cent of the agricultural workforce. However, women are not equally represented across all sectors and are mostly limited through groups. According to UNIFPA1 women participation in farmer groups had reached 30% by 2009. The proportion of women in the total number of farmers is estimated at only 5%. The benefits of agricultural extension and training have still largely accorded to men. For example, women are less likely than men to be involved in decision making about farming, and gendered norms make it difficult for women farmers to seek out male extension agents.

As high as 80 per cent of women are involved in agriculture and livestock sector in the GRB, and a trend of ‘feminization of the agricultural sector’ is emerging as an outcome of high male outmigration. Women’s participation in this sector is critical for food security. This issue needs to be taken into account seriously by the project when designing its activities. However, women are not equally represented across all sectors and their engagement in the sector, they are not treated as farmers and they don’t have the same access to land, credit, extension services and other inputs. Only 10 % of the total farms of Nepal are owned by women or jointly owned (MOAD,2015).

Nepal is one of the countries worldwide in which women’s participation in community forest management has been increasing. Women’s involvement in forest management and participation of women in community forestry is admirable. Women are considered as primary users of forests in Nepal. They have a traditional institutional knowledge in relation to the use of forest resources for fuel, building materials, food, best medicinal plants, timber, quality of leaf litter, medicinal value of herbs, nutritional value of fruits and vegetables, amongst others. A large number of women also collect and process forest products such as honey, wild fruits, and other forest products. Supporting their families through the use of such products has become increasingly difficult in the present scenario of a rapidly degrading environment.

Improving climate resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin, Nepal

Out of total 13061 Community Forest (CF), about 1072 CFs are managed by women (5.53 %) (DoFCF), however, the community forests handed over to women are usually small in area and either difficult to manage or not economically viable. The remaining 90% of CFs are managed by men (54.8%) with a share of 80-85 %. This is due to the fact that the head of the household is usually registered as the member at the CFUG.

People in the GRB are highly dependent on forest resources for their food, household energy, and livelihoods. Women’s access to water resources has decreased, leading to a decrease in agricultural productivity (MARC, 2010). The proportion of women who depend on firewood as their primary source of fuel is 77.4 per cent of households solely depend on forest to fulfil their household energy, the pressure on forest is increasing [MARC; 2016] and women who are responsible for collecting firewood have to travel long distances in search of firewood to collect fire wood as well as fodder for their livestock. This gender specific role and need of women are not being addressed.

A study conducted by Asian Development Bank (ADB) in Nepal revealed that each female water carrier must remain under 1.3 hours per day during the monsoon season and an average of 2-3 hours a day at other times of year. The study also found that the lack of women’s participation in water sector is mainly project related and due to intervene of donors. A study by the Thapathali Water and Sanitation Centre (RCO) of community water supply and sanitation projects in GRB conducted between 2015-2016 revealed that only 15 per cent of women participate in the full cycle of planning, implementation and monitoring of these projects. The full participation of women are more sustainable and effective than those that do not involve women in the project.

Anticipated differences in Men’s and Women’s Vulnerability and Adaptive Capacity to Climate Change

The impacts of climate change are not gender neutral – women’s high dependence on agriculture and climate impacts on the sector have a disproportionate effect on women. A recently published, first of its kind, national household survey on impact of climate change in Nepal (2016) by the Ministry of Federal Affairs and Local Development (MoFALD) observed increased creeper species in agricultural land and that this has contributed to a decrease in their income. This is an area for consideration for the project - to enhance income and employment opportunities for women through diversification, value addition and training, in addition women responsive adaptive interventions.

The NAPA 2010, states that women’s access to water resources would decrease leading on increased competition among households, which could lead to conflict and reproductive health. An alarming health issue for rural women in Nepal is uterine prolapse due to heavy workloads and inequalities in the distribution of household responsibilities. Women are also more vulnerable to violence against women, anxiety and depression in women. Which ultimately increases the vulnerability of women.

Vulnerability in GRB is highly correlated with gender, caste, ethnicity, regional identity, and geographic location and poverty. Women, because of gender based discrimination and ingrained patriarchal socio-economic and political system and their lower socio-economic status in comparison to men are more vulnerable to climate change impacts than men. ### Existing Gender Inequalities that may be Exacerbated by Climate Change Impacts

Women have the knowledge and understanding of what is needed to adapt to changing environmental conditions and to come up with practical solutions. But they are still a largely untapped resource because of their limited access to education, training, credit, and technology, and limited access to political decision-making spheres often prevent them from playing a full role in tackling climate change and other environmental issues.

Trafficking of children and women in Nepal is a growing problem. Around 15,000 Nepali citizens, mostly unmarried women and children, were trafficked in 2014-16, according to a national report released by the National Human Rights Commission (NHRC) [2016](cited in e-aasrpt). The Nepal government has been working with international partners to rescue victims of trafficking. In a three-month period after the Gorkha earthquake, 85% of rescued trafficking victims had never been to school and 97% were illiterate. Women and children are particularly vulnerable to this case natural disaster and climate hazard is also a key trigger. The Human Trafficking and Transportation (Control) Act, 2007, provides for the protection and rehabilitation of trafficking victims.

This basin is the biggest of Nepal's four river basins in terms of total glacier cover area. It contains the largest number of glaciers in the country, including the Gangapurna Glacier (GLOF’s [First Report Disserta Part II]). There has been increased frequency and intensity of rain, flood and landslides (Srivastha, 2003)). The basin is particularly vulnerable to water-induced hazards such as floods, landslides and glacial lake outburst floods. Glaciers have shrunk by 15% in the last three decades and there has been a significant increase in the number of rockfalls, landslides and landslides in Nepal between 2000 and 2014, 939 (35 %) occurred in the GRB, which has only 23% of total land area.

Events tend to be more common in the mid hills, especially the districts of Parbat, Syangja, Gorkha, Dhading, and Nuwakot. – Dhading had the highest incidence of floods and landslides with 125 events per year (Srivastha, 2003). The district of Nuwakot has the highest number of affected families (8,187 out of 22,637) (Ministry of

# Inequalities between Different Social Groups

## Gender and Economic Class

Inequalities in the basis of gender or the economic class is greatly hampered the climate change adaptation strategies. The women are more vulnerable on account to their climate change adaptation strategies. Women in Nepal do, in general, have less economic assets, lower wages, poorer education, higher rates of illiteracy, little knowledge of their legal rights, less confidence in public and are bound to their household responsibilities at home; however, there are sharp differences in the empowerment and inclusion of women across different social groups based on age and gender. Furthermore, patriarchal values lead to suppression of women not only by men but also by women themselves, discriminating against daughters-in-law or higher cast women discriminating against lower cast women.

## The Gorkha Rural Block Subdivision (GRBS)

The GRBS is inhabited by more than 40 ethnic groups and indigenous communities and in the Terai there are 15 ethnic groups. The GRBS has a Human Development Index (HDI) of 0.482 compared to the national average HDI of 0.637. Similarly, the life expectancy at birth for the GRBS is lower than the national average (69 years). Likewise, the literacy rate and life expectancy values of these districts are also lower than the national average. Average HDI of 19 tribes in the GRB is 0.510. Kaski has highest HDI of 0.576 followed by Dhading with an HDI of 0.548. The lowest HDI values for these districts means they are lowest in the rank among the 19 GRBS followed by Nuwakot with HDI of 0.466. The overall HDI value for ethnic groups is 0.372 compared to a national average of 0.581 (NPC & UNDP, 2014). The average human poverty index (HPI) of the GRB is 0.327, compared to the national average of 31% (NPC & UNDP, 2014). The HPI of ethnic communities in the GRB is higher than the GRB average and the highest HPI among these districts are Dhading with an HPI value being very high (42.24 in Dhading), Kaski is the richest district with the lowest HPI of 16.50, followed by Chitwan with a HPI of 4.80. Rasuwa is poorest amongst 19 districts with the highest HPI of 42.32, followed by Nuwakot and Kaski with an HPI value being 31.16 (NPC & UNDP, 2014).

## Vulnerability to Climate Change

Some specific groups within the Gandaki River Basin such as small farm holders, cattle herders, poor agriculturalists, pastoralist and Dalit communities, elderly women, children and women are particularly vulnerable due to climate change and are impacted mostly. Both gender equity and social inclusion of these groups is hampered by their lack of access to different government sectors as they have to comply with their donor's requirement. However, the level of social

inclusive participation, decision-making, leadership roles played by poor women, marginalized ethnic groups and disabled people is more important than that in power.

Due to the inequalities that exist between different social groups the impact of climate change is also differs. The poor and disadvantage group are more affected by the impact of climate change. Women are more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change because they are often located by climate hazards and have low ability to cope with the climate change impacts. Evidence shows that due to the inequality in terms of the financial resources, disadvantages group are completely excluded from the decision making process.

Nepal has a high incidence of Gender based Violence. And which everyone, regardless of their sex can be affected, women remain by large the main victims. In 2017, 169 people were killed as a result of domestic violence in Nepal. The majority of these deaths are women and children killed because of domestic violence (WB, 2018). This situation also affect the women's capacity to adapt the climate change impacts. Therefore, it is important to raise awareness and control for not exclude the gender base violence by providing the opportunities to engage in the project activities and raising awareness on this.

### Women’s and Men’s Anticipated Roles in the Project

Incorporating a gender perspective successfully and effectively requires that men and women understand their roles and responsibilities. This is because information on countering its negative impacts on an equal basis to both women and men. So the project mainly focused on the empowerment of women and girls, which will help them to be more active in decision making process. Women have equal access to knowledge, awareness, capacity building, resources and technology, which are essential for adaptation to climate change. The project also focuses on the participation more active in decision-making and policy development at all levels. So in every stage of planning, implementation and evaluation, women’s voices should be taken into consideration or as taken a key important stakeholder. The project also focuses on the benefit sharing mechanism. In addition, in this project, men are expected to contribute to identify the sex specific climate change problems and share with them their traditional practices being followed to cope with such extreme weather events. Men are expected to support women in household chores and free women for adopting adaptation measures.

### Access to Resources

The distributions of the resources in Nepal are still not well managed. The major two factors economic and social identities have great impact in the distribution of the resources in Nepal basically at the distribution of land (ADB, 2015). The current land tenure practices happen in the Nepal is based on the traditional system which is patriarchal in nature. This results in the inequity of the resources so there still remains huge inequality in the basis of the distribution of land. In addition, women are not allowed to own or inherit property and land in many districts. Advasis, Janajatis and women. Glancing at the data around 95% of Dalits living in terai region of Nepal do not possess their own land and termed as landless while in the case of hilly region 40% of the population is landless.

Women in Nepal do, in general, have less economic assets, lower wages, poorer education, higher rates of illiteracy, little knowledge of their legal rights, less confidence in public, and are bound by social norms. These factors make women more vulnerable to climate change and limit the empowerment and inclusion levels of women depending on class, caste, ethnicity, religion and age. Furthermore, patriarchal values lead to subjugation of women not only by men but also by

### Improving climate resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin, Nepal

women e.g. mother-in-law discriminating against daughter-in-law or higher cast women discriminating against lower cast women.

Due to a patriarchal and patrilocal system, women in Nepal had been denied full access and control over family property. The legal framework maintained this discrimination until the 11th Amendment of the Constitution of Nepal (2015), which repealed several discriminatory provisions of the Civil Code along with other acts and entitled women to significant rights. The amendment provided equal inheritance rights to unmarried daughters, widows, and their children; it also allowed for divorce in cases where a woman was over 35 years of age) and removed discriminatory conditions that prevented women from having custody of their children after divorce or separation. It also abolished the practice of divorce and the provision of receiving monthly or yearly support in lieu of property. Widows were given the right to inherit property, including land, which they previously did not have; they were previously required to return property to the deceased husband's household upon remarriage.

Women in Nepal had legal access to bank loans and other forms of financial credit. However, women’s access to institutional credit from banks and financial institutions in 2004 was only 1.7%, and gender access remains marginal compared to men as the land is the most important source of collateral.

In the Gandaki Basin, large sections of the rural and urban population lack minimal access to water for drinking, sanitation and irrigation; their livelihoods depend on agriculture, wage earning, and small-scale trade. Women in this region are particularly vulnerable because they have weak social support networks. These people are most likely to be affected by climate change.

To address these challenges, the project aims to improve gender equality through integrated and holistic management of natural, human, cultural, physical and financial capitals.


## Access to Information

In Nepal, from the service provider side there is equal access to information and opportunities provided but due to low level of education, literacy women may not have good knowledge and understanding. The level of awareness among women in the Gandaki Basin regarding climate change may be high tech to illustrate women so the vulnerable communities’ men and women have not equally benefited from the services provided by the government and non-governmental organizations from the anticipated outcomes of the project. The GRB is comparatively advanced in terms of gender equality compared with other regions in Nepal, particularly in the western part of that region (province 2) where gender based discrimination is widespread and many harmful practices are still prevalent such as female infanticide, early marriage, dowry, child marriage (chaupadi) and child delivery is very much alive in the far west, and pahari (hill) system and women’s participation in decision making is low. The communities of the Tharu ethnic group that live in Tharus which are dominant in this region are comparatively more advanced in terms of treating women equally and women are to some extent engaged in enterprise and market-oriented trading activities.

In GRB there is strong presence of women led community organizations like mother’s group (aama Samaha), natural resource management and conservation groups e.g. CFUGs, buffer zone committees, etc., which are very active in the Gandaki Basin. These groups have formed health community volunteers group which will be instrumental to reach and deliver project specific information and services to women and vulnerable communities. The groups provide vital information and opportunities to the vulnerable communities through these networks and institutions. However, the level of awareness among women regarding climate change and adaptation practices is weak among the various ethnic groups. Dalits and disabled groups and communities.

# Access to Education

No, due to the social cultural norms and traditions the access to education, technical knowledge and skill upgrading with respect to social groups are also difficult for poor and marginalized women to access education, technical knowledge and skill upgradation. Girls specially the disadvantaged ones are not able to get education in school because of lack of teachers, school teachers, social workers and the like. Because of reading and writing skills of the well-off families, the local level government and semi- government jobs also fall in their hands, so they have been given priority over girls.

In GRB, there are the Women development officers who are implementing most of the gender issues lack technical skills to implement all the relevant development programs. At the local level skill training is not being imparted for women in the field of agriculture and horticulture. When district forest offices or other relevant agencies have to address gender issues, they set aside the women's group and give priority to men's groups because most times the women do not sufficient knowledge. The training programs in the field level are not targeting the women and deprived groups and sometimes they are not communicated for skill development opportunities.

Due to extreme variation in topography and existence of micro-climates in short distances, the geographic domain of one specific technology is small in general in Nepal and have limited knowledge about the impact of climate change on agriculture, horticulture, livestock and mountains. Due to this reason the researchers are not well-versed with the impact of climate change on these sectors. The project will also train women in GRB area to increase their access to technical knowledge and skill required to women in the GRB area.

## Provision for Women and Men to Access Project supported Services and Technologies

In the Gandaki River Basin various government, non-government and local CBOs are active in implementing projects that aim at increasing the income of poor households, improving sustainable livelihoods, conservation of resources and to tackle climate change impacts to reduce vulnerabilities. So far, all sectors of programs and policies have considered social inclusion as a priority for women and men equally. However, there is still a long way to go and mainstream the policies to increase human, social, financial, natural and physical assets. However, in GRB area, the project will provide training on climate change adaptation services and technologies to both men and women equal due to the level of education, participation and socio-cultural norms and traditions. The project will also train women and men in GRB area with resources and technologies to both men and women in the GRB area by applying the Ecosystem based Adaptation (EbA) approach for climate change adaptation services and technologies.

Furthermore, the project will value and utilise the traditional knowledge of women and indigenous people on climate change adaptation. This will increase their capacity to reduce vulnerability and increases resilience to climate change which will provide by this project. Likewise, the project will also have trained or build the capacity of men and women on the handling of climate change services and technologies provided by the project.

## Participation in Decision Making

After the promulgation of the constitution in Nepal the participation of the women and men from the vulnerable communities have gradually increased in the decision making processes but still there are some barriers that prevent them to participate effectively at household, community level decision. Although women's involvement in households and community level has been increasing over time, their participation is still not enough for effective participation in decision making process is still unalbeit of the limit expected due to domination of male members in the society. There have been some initiatives taken by the government to citizens to participate in political and decision making about climate adaptation. However, women, economically indigenous people, Dalits, disabled and others often have limited access to decision making making as the caste


---
**Page 131**
---

In the Nepali context, the empowerment and development of women is inextricably bound to the traditional Hindu social structure, which influences all aspects of social, cultural, and economic life. This system has been designed by men for men and does not empower household and family responsibilities. Nepali women have internalized this system and makes it difficult for them to envision themselves in roles outside the home. Women participate in the community level groups and forums but their voices are rarely heard or taken into consideration in community management including, drinking water management, natural resource management and conservation, disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation. The role of women is not counter balanced by income, meaningful representation in decision making forums, or an equal share in land ownership. Women do not try to apply the norm of equal pay to both men and women and also raise the awareness.

The socio-cultural norms and traditions make difficult for vulnerable communities specially women in their participation at community level. The lack of access to education discourages women from participating in decision making forums. Furthermore, there is a lack of representation of women in the community level groups and forums due to high male members in the community level major groups and forums and most of the decision making positions are held by men. Women have limited access to land ownership rights, marginalized peoples and Dalit households do not usually hold influential positions in community level groups and forums. Women also face challenges in accessing government line measures. They also have less access to the services provided by government line agencies.

The GRB is listed within the top 10 districts with migrant women/population reflecting a high rate of migration. The highest number of migrants are from the Terai region, followed by total households in Guna and Arghakhanchi districts, the highest in Nepal (CBS, 2012). Migration has a significant impact on the socio-economic status and responsibilities for women who become the de facto household heads in the absence of men. This brings about new challenges as well as challenges for women to adapt to climate risk and vulnerabilities.

The armed conflict and upper escalating rate of out migration have increased the responsibility burden on women, who are still regarded as independent and autonomous farmer by the social practices of the country. They are directly involved in the agricultural production and land use decisions. Women also face a lot of challenges at the decision making power regarding the use of land and agriculture production of the country (AIRE, 2017).

Barriers to the introduction and implementation of gender responsive climate resilient solutions

There are several key barriers to the introduction and implementation of gender responsive climate resilient solutions as presented below:

- Lack of sex-disaggregated data, information, monitoring and evaluation
    - Lack of understanding of climate vulnerability and risk resulting in poor understanding of gender-differentiated risk of climate change and climate induced disasters.
    - Non-inclusion of gender specific indicators in the entire monitoring and evaluation cycle of climate resilient projects.
    - Lack of synergy and capacity across sector/ministries/agencies to address gender-environment data gaps and gender-responsive M&E.

This project aims to address this to some extent.

# Improving climate resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin, Nepal

## Women’s negligible representation in climate change policy, leadership and decision making

- Lack of effort to increase representation of women in climate leadership and decision-making.
- Lack of opportunities for women and their representative organizations’ views being represented in formal climate change policy, leadership and decision-making.
- Lack of planned and quality training on gender and climate change to women and men participating in the climate change adaptation process.
- Lack of planned, regular training and awareness raising activities on gender equity, climate change and appropriate adaptation measures, focused on rural women and disadvantaged people.

## Heavy unpaid workload especially of rural women and inequitable division of labor

- Women contribute their substantial labour (unpaid) in agriculture and natural resource management which does not correspond increasing income, and meaningful representation in decision-making.
- Women reported during field visit difficulties in adapting agricultural practices to climate change. This is because they are the ones who bear the brunt when production yields decline amidst changing climate patterns as it takes its toll on women, who are major producers of food and other household needs.
- Due to social norms and inequitable gender division of labour; unpaid work is assigned primarily to women. Women have to manage their time between work, care farm, and they are highly impacted by climate change. In addition to this, women are responsible for water collection, cooking and cleaning, which is the cause of “gendered” vulnerability to climate change.

## Low level of education (illiteracy) climate risk awareness and access to information

- Women’s limited access to climate risk information, early warning systems and training is making them more vulnerable and restricting their capacity to adapt.
- Women are less aware about climate risks and are not able to take precautionary measures in time.

## Gender discrimination, cycle of poverty, and physical vulnerability of girls and women

- Early and child marriage of girls; no opportunity for economic and social advancement, repetitive life-cycle of poverty and disempowerment.
- Children and youth are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts. In terms of safety and security, increasing risk of human trafficking and exploitative work in entertainment sector as seen after earthquake and floods.
- Women and girls who migrate or are forced to migrate are particularly vulnerable to climate induced disasters with the loss of family support networks and lack of access to livelihoods and social services such as health care out.

## Limited livelihood options and access to productive assets

- Low access to finance, family property, productive assets and restricted access to markets. This limits women’s capacity to livelihood diversification which is a key activity for adaptation.

## Migration: Increase in fallow land and land degradation

- High out-migration of youth men after climate induced increased poverty and weather leads to a lack of labor force in the community, resulting in fallow land and land degradation, mostly in rural areas.
- Implications on food security, health and nutrition of women and children.

Due to their high influence in decision making, participation of men and wealthier people in economic development interventions in the project site is high. In contrast, due to low influence in decision-making processes, women and poorer, shrine groups in income generation is moderate, and participation of poor households, marginal groups and Dalit households in economic development is low.

## Opportunities to Promote the Leadership of Women in Local Government and Institutions

Nepal is going through political transformation and this could be an advantage to mainstream and integrate gender inclusion and poverty reduction issues for better livelihoods through management of natural resources. The new constitution provides a framework for implementing policies outlined in the new constitution to ensure the voices, and access and benefit sharing of women, deprived groups and Dalit households in decision making processes. This is also a great opportunity for the political leaders to show their commitment to uplift women, deprived groups and indigenous people and empower them through education and skill acquisition. They can play a decisive role to develop programs to empower, and enable women and deprived groups securing their livelihoods.

After the promulgation of Constitution of Nepal there are lots of opportunities to promote the leadership of women in local government/local institutions and formal/informal institutions. In the local election of Nepal 2017 women also got leadership position at local level due to mandating of quota system for women representation. This is a great opportunity to strengthen the government policies and law for the women leadership position in different sectors. An increase in mainstreaming gender issues, inclusion of women in decision making processes will ensure the efficiency of the programs being implemented. Gender mainstreaming and inclusive legal policies will also help women to access resources and services that are required to change social-cultural norms and enable them to fight for their rights in resource management and decision making at local level. One major input will be to eliminate discrimination from resource management and development activities.

## Capacity building, empowerment, awareness raising, sustainable development and accountability programs will not only raise the human resource capacity of the central or district level project staff but also empower women and deprived groups. By mainstreaming gender equality and inclusion programs these programs will support the deprived communities to have their share in participation and encourage them fully participate in all the development and conservation activities.

The project aims to enhance women's participation in formulation of policies, strategies and plans aimed at providing equal opportunity to articulate their needs and priorities and ensure that the policy tools are gender friendly.

## Differential Needs/Priorities of Women and Men and Project Plan to Address

Growing evidence suggests that men and women experience climate change differently and have different priorities and ability to respond to negative climate change impacts. In order to enable women and deprived groups to participate in decision making processes, their needs and contributions, we must pay close attention to gender-based differences and embed them into the design of project activities and policies.

There are several areas where there is disparity in the impact of climate change in Nepal. The main disparities are: differential perceptions of climate change due to low level of education of women and deprived groups; lack of access to information, limited access to communication tools to climate information, approach in managing climate risks, etc. Disparities that makes women more vulnerable in Nepal also include high level of poverty and more impact on women due to the desire to have children.

# Project Plan to Take Into Account the Needs of Specific (Vulnerable) Sub-Groups

The project will take account of specific vulnerable sub groups needs and participation in all stages of the project. It is assumed that women have a greater capacity for adaptation, skills and innovation in the use of local resources for climate change adaptation. It assumes that if women are empowered to participate in decision making they will not only generate desired ecosystem goods and services, diversify livelihood options and increases gender equality but also ensure that vulnerable communities and ecosystems will be more resilient to impacts of climate change.

The project will focus on gender-responsive implementation activities and promote gender-sensitive development impacts by integrating gender equality into all activities. There are varying levels of access to resources, education, skills, and finance which will shape how women and men adapt to climate impacts and they may share the benefits of climate finance differently. Actively engaging women as "agents of change" in climate solutions yields multiple other benefits, including improved livelihoods for both women and men.

The project will focus on women playing a central role in planning and implementing activities and will seek to ensure that their needs are met while ensuring that the overall development outcomes and livelihoods are not adversely affected. From the project interventions the livelihood condition will be improved.

It is anticipated that because of increased income and other benefits, communities will have increased access to better education, health, and community services. They will also have increased participation in community decision making thereby enhancing good governance practices and increasing their capacity for climate change adaptation. As a result people will earn more income and also save their time which could be utilised for the education and health of children.

## Proposed Specific Response Strategy for Each Target Group

The project will give due priority to gender and social inclusion in all activities wherever applicable. Firstly, the project will do vulnerability assessment of the gender and based on the results of this assessment it will identify specific needs and gaps. As a result of increased income and other benefits, communities will have increased access to better education, health, and community services. They will have increased participation in community decision making thereby enhancing good governance practices.

## Plan to Utilize Specific Knowledge and Skills of Women and Men to Contribute to Project Outcomes

The project will utilise the specific knowledge and skills of women and men, especially from vulnerable groups through the development of indigenous (and local) business opportunities, promotion of indigenous and tradition knowledge and also undertake the Free Price Information System (FPIS). The project will also promote the use of traditional knowledge in time regarding resource conservation and enterprise development. The project will recognize that local communities have a wealth of knowledge about their environment and ecosystems based on the use of local resources. Such innovation will be identified and used by the project, subject to agreement of local people. The traditional, local and indigenous knowledge and skill of

## Project Identified Opportunities to Challenge Gender Stereotypes

By involving local people from different segments of populations such as women, poor and marginalized groups in all aspects of planning, implementation and monitoring of the project, not only are the involved people being empowered; the intervention will also help to break down kinds of discrimination and stereotypes. The involvement of women in decision-making processes, empowerment and shared learning on resilience building will strengthen and increase social capital and behavioral change with community empowerment and social inclusion and build solidarity within the communities.

  - Research on climate-resilient agriculture production practices and technology suitable for women in light of feminization of agriculture

## Arrangement for the Implementation of Gender Action Plan

### Preparation of Gender Action Plan Based on the gender assessment and areas identified for consideration in the project design phase. The plan was developed in consultation with women's organizations - particularly women's self-help groups and CBOs and NGOs working in climate change adaptation and mitigation, as well as with government officials. Women are directly involved in project implementation and are beneficiaries of on-ground activities. The interventions have been designed to be culturally and socially sensitive.

All the project outputs and outcomes will also be reinforced by gender-specific activities to ensure that gender issues are duly addressed. The GAP targets women-headed households to increase their income through direct employment opportunities, which will be created for women directly engaged by project facilitators - both to take advantage of livelihood opportunities and to strengthen their capacity as decision-makers in the household. Women will also be employed on a daily wage basis; 65% women as direct beneficiaries and 80% women as indirect beneficiaries.

The Action Plan is based on project's gender-responsive approach which is in alignment with national policies and polices including climate change, forest, agriculture and water, all of which promote increased participation of women. The project will ensure equal pay for the same work; for example: equal pay between men and women for the same activities is not evident in the GRB. In the GRB, 50% of the total budget has been allocated to women's self-help groups (SHGs) and 20% were paid NRS700. When hiring staff or contracting workers, the project will ensure equal pay for the same work irrespective of gender. The project will also provide a daily wage of NRS150 per day for pregnancy leave, and childcare will be ensured for project staff and women involved in training courses during the project.

In order to ensure that the mainstreamed gender issues are properly addressed, there are 38 activities designed and USD 100,000 dedicated to implement the Climate Change and Gender Action Plan. The GESI-disaggregated climate data base management centre will be established by Senior Climate Change and Gender Officer of NTNC. IUCN Nepal and NTNC have proven success in establishing such centres in other projects. These centres will provide a platform for gender and community development. Their implementation approach will be extensively used to safeguard gender equality, equity and inclusion. The project will also ensure that women are prioritized by Panchayat, farmer groups, saving and credit groups, and enterprises will be prioritized to ensure benefits for women.

# Introduction

Nepal is facing a wide range of impacts from climate change including changes in rainfall patterns, longer dry seasons, frequent landslides and soil erosion, food shortages, increased risk of glacial outbursts and rising temperatures. These changes are having negative impacts on agriculture and food security, water resources, forests and biodiversity, health, tourism and infrastructure. This leads to reduced adaptive capacity and resilience of communities and ecosystems.

The Government of Nepal (GoN) has given strong emphasis to the river basin approach through different sectorial policies, plans and strategies. It has also started using Ecosystem based Adaptation (EbA) and Ecosystem-based disaster risk reduction (EbDRR) through various projects and has recently recommended the development of EbA in all 14 river basins and other vulnerable ecosystems and areas.

IUCN, as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the Green Climate Fund (GCF), with the Department of Soil Conservation and Watershed Management (DoSCWM) Ministry of Forest and Soil Conservation, and the National Trust for Nature Conservation (NTNC) have collaborated on the development of the proposed project "Enhancing Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Communities and Ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin".

## 1.1 Socio Economic Background

**Population:** According to the 2016 update of the 2011 census, Nepal's population is 28.33 million. The average annual population growth rate (APGR) has been 1.4 per cent (ADB, 2017). The population density is 193 persons per km². The sex ratio is 0.6 males for every female.

Nepal's population comprises 48.5 per cent male and 51.8 per cent female. The Census data of 2011 shows that the literacy rate in Nepal has increased from 79 to 83 percent, with an increase of 11 percentage points from 78 per cent in 2001 to 75.27 per cent in 2011.

**Ethnicity:** There are two caste/cultural groups reported in the 2011 census. Other is the largest caste/cultural group (46,398,053) of the total population followed by Brahmin-Hill (12.2 per cent; 3,226,903) and Kirat (5 per cent).

**Languages:** There were 123 languages spoken as people's mother tongue in the 2011 census. Nepal is spoken by 48.7 percent of total population followed by Maithili (11.7 per cent; 3,092,530) and Bhojpuri (6.0 per cent; 1,584,958).

**Religion:** Of the ten categories of religion reported in the census, Hinduism is followed by Islam (3.1 per cent [711,552]), Buddhism (2.8 per cent [640,000]), Sikhism (1.5 per cent [339,000]) and Christianity (1.3 per cent [299,000]). Poverty: The Gross Domestic Product (GDP) of Nepal is USD 20.8 billion and GDP per capita USD 2,313 (NIS-2015). The average annual economic growth rate from 2012–2016 was 1.46 per cent. The Gini Coefficient for the country in 2015 is estimated at 48.

In Nepal's 14-year period plan (2016-2030), an average economic growth rate of 7.2 per cent has been projected. Among other objectives in this plan, a focus on cross-cutting issues such as gender equality, inclusive society, environmental protection and capacity development of different institutions.

## Objectives of the Study

This gender assessment aims to provide an overview of the situation of Gender Equity/Fairness in Nepal, identifying key gender issues and other categories of discrimination and vulnerabilities especially caste.

# Improving Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Communities and Ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin, Nepal

## Gender Vulnerabilities Relevant to the Project

The assessment identifies gender-mainstreaming opportunities within the project components.

### The Gender Equity/Equality Assessment

This Gender Equity/Equity assessment provides grounded justification for preparing a Climate Change Gender Action Plan (CCGAP) with indicators, targets and activities identifying women as beneficiaries, leaders and decision makers with tentative cost estimates.

## Methods

The assessment is based on a desk review of available literature and data as well as consultation with a wide range of stakeholders from the national level, provincial level, and local level down to the level of vulnerable communities.

- Relevant data and information were collected from published secondary data and publications from the Central Bureau of Statistics of Nepal Census Data of 2011, the World Human Development Report 2013, the National Planning Commission of Nepal (NPC), the World Bank, ADB, the Ministry of Population and Environment, the Ministry of Forest and Social Conservation, and various other reports related to gender equality, climate change adaptation, natural resource management (NRM) and disaster risk reduction (DRR) sectors in Nepal.

- The units of analysis used for the gender equity assessment are the household-level for quantitative data collection and the community level for focus group discussions, with key informant interviews at the national and provincial level. Data and information are disaggregated by sex and age to identify gender-based vulnerabilities and opportunities.

- All 19 districts within the Gandaki River Basin (GRB) were visited by a group of multi sector experts including a gender expert and field facilitators for site specific firsthand information collection and consultation with key stakeholders and vulnerable communities. Information collection was done through direct observation, interviews and focus group discussions guided by a pre-prepared checklist.

## A Review of National Gender Policies, Legislation and Strategies

### 2.1 Gender Equity/Equality in Constitution and Law

#### The Constitution of Nepal 2015

The Constitution of Nepal is a significant milestone for gender equity and social inclusion (GES). It enshrines the rights of women, the poor, the vulnerable and people from different social groups.

Positive provisions include affirmative action to address historical disadvantage and a ban on sex or caste-based discrimination in all areas of life. The article on Rights of Women establishes the right to equal lineage; right to safe motherhood and reproductive health; right to participate in all bodies of the State; right to property and family affairs; and positive discrimination for women in public service. It also provides that any act of sexual harassment or act of violence against women punishable by law. The Right to Equality further elaborates the special provisions by law for the protection, empowerment or development of citizens. The Rights to Social Justice ensures equal opportunities to participate in state bodies based on the principles of inclusion and proportional representation.

#### Property Rights (Economic Rights)

Due to a patriarchal social and legal system, women in Nepal had been denied full access to and control over family property. The legal framework maintained this discrimination until the 11th Amendment of the Civil Code (commonly referred to as the Women’s Bill, 2001), which repealed several discriminatory provisions of the Civil Code along with other acts and entitled women to significant rights.

The Annex III: Gender Assessment Report


---
**Page 151**
---

amendment provided equal inheritance rights to unmarried daughters and sons (where previously an unmarried daughter had inheritance rights only if she was over 12 years of age) and removed discriminatory conditions that prevented women from having full access to property. Women were granted the right on their husband's property upon divorce and the provision of receiving monthly or year lump-sum payments for a period not exceeding five years after marriage. Women also have the right to sue even if they remarry (where previously they were required to return property to the deceased husband).

The Gender Equality Act (2006) advanced the property rights of women even further and gives equal property right on ancestral property to son and daughters. It removed and amended discriminatory language in the Hindu Marriage Act, 1953, which prohibited women from inheriting a portion of household, land or house or both as being the name female member of the household (CBS, 2012)

### Gender Equality and Inclusion Indicators in the Socio-Economic Sector

#### Multidimensional Poverty Index - 2018

The latest 2018 Multidimensional poverty report of the Govt./National Planning Commission (NPC) notes that 36.4 per cent of Nepal's population is multidimensionally poor. However, Nepal has fallen in Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) from 59 per cent in 2006 to 29 per cent in 2014. Under-nutrition and completion of up to five years of schooling are two key indicators which contributed to the improvement in MPI score.

The recently formed seven provinces of Nepal, whereby Province 6 and 2 have the highest rate of multidimensional poverty (approximately 53 percent) followed by Province 7 (approximately 49 per cent) - followed by Provinces 5 and 7 (approximately 30 per cent being poor) (NPC, 2018). Some of the GRB is in province 5. See Figure 1 for the location of the Provinces

Figure 1: Map of Nepal showing Location of provinces


In terms of under-nutrition among children, a 2017 article in the daily newspaper *Kantipur* reported that in some areas of province 6, girls are highly malnourished compared to boys and even dying due to gender-based discrimination in food distribution and lack of timely health care for girl children. Very heavy workloads of rural and urban based women due to male out-migration, gender inequality on the household level, gender bias, and different social norms and practices have a negative impact on their children and themselves. This situation is contributing to the poor health outcome for women and girls (Economic Times, 2017; Dec-23 Saturday).


---
**Page 152**
---

In the GARB the situation of undernutrition and malnutrition of girls is as severe as in far western districts in province VI. Comparatively, the districts of GARB are more accessible. Nevertheless, there are pockets of poverty and certain caste groups including Chepang (in Dhading and Chitwan), Magar, Bote, and Tharu who are generally poor, not educated and live in remote areas. Girls get lower priority in terms of education and health care services. They marry early, often at 14 years or younger. Early marriage, and are not given any priority for education beyond primary level, or health care.

Human development index (HDI) and Human Poverty Index (HPI)

The Human Development Index (HDI) for Nepal is 0.582, the lowest ranking among countries of the South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation (SAARC), aside from Afghanistan (NPC & UNDP, 2014). Among the ecological regions, the Hills have the highest HDI value at 0.530, compared to 0.497 in the Terai and 0.486 in the Mountain region. The Human Poverty Index (HPI) value for Nepal in 2011 is 31.32. Urban-rural differences are considerable, with rural poverty nearly 1.8 times higher than urban.

Global Gender Gap Index (GGI), World Economic Forum

The Global Gender Gap Index (GGI) measures gender parity between males and females. The GGI for Nepal is 0.661, and Nepal ranks 110 out of 144 countries measured. Nepali women are progressing in terms of political empowerment, education attainment, health status and economic opportunity. However, in terms of economic opportunity (115) and educational attainment (123) they are ranking far behind their global women counterparts.

The Gender Inequality Index (GII), UNDP

The Gender Development Index (GDI), score for Nepal for 2011 was 0:534. Among the ecological regions, the GDI value based on the geometric mean is the highest for the hills at 0.615, followed by the Terai at 0.587 and Mountain region at 0.559. The gender gap in education is the largest among major challenges across Nepal. Nationally, the average income of women is 57 per cent lower than the average for men; whereas 83.1 per cent of women are economically active which is a paradox that reflects their low economic status.

The Gender Empowerment Measure (GEM), UNDP

The Gender Empowerment Measure (GEM) for Nepal in 2011 was 0:546. Among ecological regions, the Mountain region has the lowest GEM at 0.378 and the Terai is the highest at 0.572. This is due to the low share of Women in Parliament at 18.8 per cent, compared to 28.9 per cent for the Hills and 32.9 per cent for the Terai; as well as low combined income values. Among development regions, the urban region has a GEM value of 0.574 which is higher than the rural region at 0.516. The share of women in administrative and professional positions. The Eastern region has the highest GEM at 0:578, followed by the Terai and Western regions.

Employment Status and Labour Force

The proportion of economically active women in Nepal is quite high compared to other South Asian Countries. According to the National Labor Force Survey, 80.1 per cent of women are economically active compared with only 42.5 per cent for men. The majority of economically active women live in rural areas and work as agricultural labourers (63.7 per cent). Women also have a high level of economic activity in the informal sector, particularly in the service industry compared to 70 per cent of men. However, the wages of women lag well behind those of men. In non-agricultural wage employment, women are concentrated in low-paying and less productive jobs at low capital intensity (e.g., domestic work). The number of women working as agricultural labourers was a total of 266,668 (against that year), although this probably underestimates the volume given the numbers migrating without official permission (AICD, 2010). It has been estimated that 11 per cent


---
**Page 153**
---

total remittances are from women migrants. The Foreign Employment Act, 2007 aims to protect the rights of workers and professionals.

## Health

In Nepal the maternal mortality ratio is 229 deaths per 100,000 live births, which is quite high (PEHRC, 2016). The government's Second Long-Term Health Plan (1997–2017) gives high priority to improving neonatal and child health services, maternal and reproductive health care for women in the most remote and socially excluded, including a safer motherhood program, and a female community health volunteer program. These are quite successful.

Major health problems specific to women-specific are prolapsed uterus, undernutrition and anemia due to heavy workloads, combined with inadequate access to nutritious food during menstrual cycles and postpartum periods. This is prevalent in the far western areas, as well as in some parts of the Terai, where patriarchal and gender-based discrimination is high. The prevalence of anaemia among women level is very high. One study estimated that more than 600,000 women in Nepal are suffering from uterine prolapse (SCOMA, 2011).

## Education

The share of total enrolment for girls is 50.4 per cent at primary level, 50.5 per cent at lower secondary and 50.4 per cent at basic level. This clearly demonstrates that the gender parity has been achieved in basic education. However, there are still some disparities between boys and girls in terms of literacy rate: 1.23 at secondary level and 1.62 at basic level (WFP, 2018). However, in terms of literacy age ratio, there is wide gender disparity with a ratio of 4.5 for boys to one girl.

There is a huge disparity among boys and girls in terms of dropout rates from secondary school education. Early marriage is a main cause of girls dropping out of school; and economic condition and poverty are the other reason boys drop out.

## Human Trafficking

Trafficking of children and women in Nepal is a pressing issue. Around 16,500 Nepali children, mostly unmarried women and children, were trafficked in 2014–16, according to a national report released by the National Human Rights Commission (NHRC). The UNICEF estimates that there are around 37,000 children who indicate 1,233 women and children missing (i.e. likely trafficked) in a three-month period after the Gorakha earthquake of April 25, 2015. The Nepal government has identified human trafficking as a nexus with gender discrimination, illiteracy and poverty; in this case natural disaster and climate hazard is also a key trigger. The Human Trafficking and Transportation (Control) Act, 2007, provides for the punishment of traffickers and their associates.

## 2.3 Policy and Institutional Environment towards a Gender Equity-based Approach to Ecosystem Based Adaptation (EBA)

### 2.3.1 The Evolution of Climate Change Policy and Gender and Inclusion Policy in Nepal

There are a range of laws, policies and programs related to climate change adaptation in Nepal. This part of the report will provide an overview of these laws and policies which they take gender into account and future prospect for Gender Equity Based approach to EBA.

National Adaptation Plan of Action, NAPA (2016) Nepal submitted its NAPA to the UNFCCC Secretariat in September 2015. The NAPA is a strategic document that outlines the country’s priorities within the process, including: 1. Agriculture and food security (nutrition); 2. Climate induced disasters; 3. Forests and biodiversity; 4. Public Health (WASH); 5. Tourism; natural and cultural heritage 6. Urban settlements and infrastructure; 7. Water resources and storage; Gender; 8. Institutions and frameworks etc.


---
**Page 154**
---

### Improving Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Communities and Ecosystems in the Gandaki River Basin, Nepal

#### Local Adaptation Plan of Action (LAPA) (2011)
The Government of Nepal has approved the LAPA framework with the objective of supporting climate adaptation at the local level to ensure mainstreaming of climate change considerations into development planning. The LAPAs are a set of tools for local governments. LAPAs were introduced as a mechanism for formulating the National Adaptation Plan. Four directive principles guide their development: a bottom-up approach, inclusiveness, responsiveness, and accountability. LAPA is implemented by Local Development Committees (VDCs) and municipalities. Following the restructuring of local government under the newly federalized structure, LAPAs are now being developed at the district level.

#### Climate Policy Nepal (2013)
Nepal's Climate Change Policy of 2013 recognizes that there are differential impacts of climate change on communities. It acknowledges that the impact of climate change is gendered and addresses this by incorporating a gender perspective in all policies, programs, and projects. The policy provides for women’s participation in the implementation of climate adaptation programs. Section 8.4.2 of the Policy calls for “ensuring the participation of poor people, Dalits, marginalized indigenous communities, workers, youth, women, persons with disabilities, and other vulnerable groups” in all aspects of the climate change program.” The policy provides for capacity building of local bodies and the implementation of local level activities.

#### National Adaptation Plan (2017)
Nepal has initiated the NAP formulation process based on UNFCCC COP decisions and on initial guidelines (Decision SCP/37) and the technical guidelines developed by the GCF. The NAP is a strategic plan for climate change adaptation in Nepal.

The NAP has two key objectives: (i) reducing vulnerability to the impacts of climate change by building adaptive capacity and resilience, and (ii) facilitating the integration of climate change adaptation, in a coherent manner, into national development planning processes and strategies, within all relevant sectors and at different levels, as appropriate (IGNORANCE NAP 2017). The NAP has 7 thematic working groups and 2 cross-cutting working groups.

### Sector Specific Gender Policies and Guidelines
- **Ministry of Federal Affairs and Local Development (GEIS Policy):** The Ministry of Urban Development GEIS Operational Guidelines, the Ministry of Forest and Soil Conservation GEIS Operational Guidelines, and the Ministry of Water Resources and Environment GEIS Operational Guidelines recognize the need to address GEIS issues programatically in all sectors. However, there is no standalone GEIS strategy/policy or operational guidelines for climate change.
- **Ministry of Health GEIS Operational Guidelines:** Recognize the need to address GEIS issues programatically in all sectors. However, there is no standalone GEIS strategy/policy or operational guidelines for climate change.

At the national level, the NAP recognizes the importance of women’s and men's equal participation of Women, Dalits, Adivasi/Janjatis, Mathesis, Muslims, persons with disability, and excluded communities in the formulation, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of sector policies, plans and programs (GEIS Framework). The GEIS framework is a tool to ensure that all sectors are sensitive to the specific needs addressed by women, the poor, the vulnerable and the excluded in the concerned sector and plans to address it to the extent possible to achieve the overall sector objective.

# Improving Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Communities and Ecosystems in the Gangetic River Basin Nepal

## 2.4 Institutional Entry Points for Mainstreaming Gender Equality/Equity in Climate Change Adaptation

### 2.4.1 The Government of Nepal's Existing Institutional Mechanisms for GESI and Climate Change

The Climate Change Council: The government established the Climate Change Council in 2009. It is headed by the Prime Minister and it provides coordination, guidance and direction for the formulation and implementation of climate change policies.

Central Level: The National Planning Commission (NPC), the Ministry of Women, Children and Social Welfare (MOWCWS) and its Department of Women Development, the Ministry of Federal Affairs and Local Development (MoFALD) and its Dalti Unnati/Janajati coordination committees; constitutionally established:

- National Commissions for Women, Dalits, Indigenous Nationalities, Madhesis, Muslims, and Tharus
- A National Inclusion Commission that is mandated to protect the rights of Khas Aryas, and "backward" classes, persons with disabilities, senior citizens, laborers, peasants, minority and marginalized groups.

Gender/GEESI Focal Points are appointed in the NPC, MoFALD, MOWCWS and the ministries of Education, Health, Urban Development, Forest and Soil Conservation, Agriculture and Population and Environment. They are also part of the National Women's Welfare as a focal point for Gender and Social Inclusion thematic group under the NAP process.

District Level: At the district level there are numerous institutional structures including District Children's Forum (DCF), District Women's Forum (DWF), District Women's Development Committee, District Development Committees (DDCs), Indigenous/Janajati District Coordination Committees and Dalti Unnati District Coordination Committees. The Gender Mainstreaming Coordination Committees (GMAClimate changed its name to GESI) are also present.

Village/Municipality Level: At the village and municipality level there is a Representative Integrated Planning Committee in each VDC, Ward Citizens' Forum and Citizen Awareness Centers where women, disabled persons and other marginalized groups participate.

Climate specific, sector specific and local government level institutional frameworks provide the enabling environment and working conditions for gender equity/equity mainstreaming and its implementation through policy formulation, planning strategies and projects. They are designed for Ecosystem based adaptation projects etc.

### 2.4.2 Institutional Efforts for GESI mainstreaming in NRM including Agriculture

Agriculture and Forestry: The Ministry of Agriculture started integrating women into its training programs in 1980s. However, the Ministry has not yet made significant progress towards gender mainstreaming in its departments, divisions, policies, and programs. It has 6 per cent women's representation among staff, yet it's Gender Equity and Environment Division still faces challenges in implementing its responsibilities for gender equity and environmental sustainability. The Ministry is working on capacity building monitoring mechanisms. The Ministry plans to set up farmer consultative committees and village agricultural extension services with the participation of agriculture related activities and decision-making processes. Proportionate representation of Women, Dalits, Madhesis, and Adivasi Janajatis will be ensured in these committees (ADB, 2010).

The Ministry of Forest and Social Conservation has its own well-developed GESI strategy and Gender Focal points are adequately represented by a joint secretary as well as five gender focal points.

### Vulnerability status of proposed project sites of Gandaki River Basin

The NAPA analyzed the districts in the Gandaki River basin with respect to climate change vulnerability (JUN, 2018). The following table presents the status of the 39 districts in the GRB in terms of vulnerability (JUN, 2018).

| Vulnerability status | District |
| --- | --- |
| Very high | Mustang |
| High | Chitwan, Dhadh, Gorakha, Manang |
| Medium | Kaski, Lamjung, Makawanpur, Tulsikhel, Kaski, Parbat, Baglung, Myagdi, Rasuwa |
| Low | Gorkha, Gulmi, Arghakhanchi, Nawalparasi |
| Very low | Palpa |

#### Project Community

There are 1,172,550 households in the GRB (JUN, 2013). Among these households, the projects aim to cover about 648,912 (55.17 per cent) of households directly through project inputs and another 10 per cent indirectly through spill-over effects, with a focus on women-headed household.

In the following para brief status of socio-economic and environmental development of the project area is highlighted:

- **Social development status:** The GRB is inhabited by more than 40 ethnic groups, and indigenous communities and in the Terai there are mostly Tharus, an indigenous community, and Khas-Aryan groups. The average life expectancy at birth for the ethnic group was 69.8 years compared to national average of 71 years.
- **Economic development status:** The overall per capita income for people in GRB is Rs 46,362, while per capita income for ethnic groups is Rs 37,720 as compared to a national average of Rs 58,799 (NPC & UNDP, 2014).
- **Environmental context:** The basin is the biggest of Nepal’s four river basins in terms of total glacier area coverage. It contains 105 glaciers, 33 lakes and is susceptible to frequent glacial lake outburst floods (GLOFs). The basin has a high concentration of settlements, agriculture, forests, rivers, wetlands, and landfills (Shrestha, 2008). The basin is particularly vulnerable to water-induced hazards during the monsoon season. The basin experienced GLOF in 1975, 1986, 1993, 2004, 2005 and 2006 (between 2000 and 2014, 93% (3 per cent) occurred in the GRB), which has only 22 per cent of Nepal’s land area.

### Women, migration and their role in decision-making in GRB

Women participate in the household decision making processes in this area and they play an active role in common resource management, including drinking water management, natural resource management and conservation.

The GRB are listed within several districts with high household population, reflecting a high rate of male out-migration (up to 43 per cent) from households in Mardan. In KP, up to 50 per cent of total households in Gulmit and Arghandabhan districts, the highest in Nepal (CBS, 2012).

Migration has a direct implication on decision-making roles and responsibilities for women who become the de facto head of household in their absence of men. This brings both opportunity as well as challenges for women to adapt to climate risk and vulnerabilities.

Annie III: Gender Assessment Report


---
**Page 160**

With all three levels of election – national parliament, provincial and local bodies - completed under the federal system of government and around 33 per cent of women being represented in the local governing bodies as Mayors and deputy mayors as well as members, there is huge opportunity to promote the leadership of community women as well as to enhance the effectiveness of any engagement with them. The key challenge for this is to ensure that the political parties are able to accept women’s leadership amongst male leaders and most of the women being elected for the first time in the leadership positions are not only by doing and need support to enhance their effectiveness.

### 3.3 A gender equality assessment across the project areas

Vulnerability in GRB continues to be highly correlated with gender, caste, ethnicity, regional identity, geographic location and poverty. Women, because of gender based discrimination and ingrained patriarchal socio economic and political system and their lower socio-economic status in comparison to men, are more vulnerable than men.

On the other hand, poor Dalits, because of their poverty and caste-based discrimination and their settlement location usually near the landslide prone river banks, are more vulnerable. Similarly, certain poor ethnic groups such as the Khasi and Jaintia women who live in remote areas are also more vulnerable. These three groups are potential target groups for the project. Even within the better off districts of Meghalaya, there is a high degree of vulnerability among geographic and remote areas. These sites are vulnerable to climate change and Climate induced disaster.

Beliefs, Perception and Stereotypes related to gender

In the project area across mountains, hills and Terai (plains) beliefs, perception and stereotypes related to gender continue to be prevalent. Women in all these areas are perceived as less capable of achieving more equality between the sexes and women are engaged in tourism-related activities, hotel business, farming and animal husbandry. The level of education is also low among women.

This pattern is clear in hill areas. Women in hill areas are more literate and due to relatively easy access to roads, electricity, mobile phone, radio and television, to some extent internet, and proximity to urban centres and the outside world hence they are much aware about their disadvantaged position. However, within the hills there are remote areas and poverty pockets where women are discriminated against.

In the Terai certain caste groups e.g., Degangs, Tharus, Mahis, Muhans, and Madhesis practice early and child marriage and polygamy in some systems.

It is a commonly held perception in the GRB that women are caretakers and nurturers and their role is more appropriate within the household. However, with the new political system, and exposure to the outside world through media, women have become more aware of their rights and are becoming aware. Their engagement in various community groups helps them to understand about climate change risk and disaster risk.

Th division of labor is based on socially prescribed gender roles. Men do more of the outside work, professional work, trading and marketing, and paid jobs, whereas women are more engaged in household work, care giving and child rearing. In this GRB 60 per cent of females are economically active compared to 67 per cent of males.

1. "Gendered" vulnerability to climate and disaster risk -women and disadvantaged group are more vulnerable.
2. Negative impact on agriculture, food security and livelihoods
