The national climate change impact survey (CBS, 2016) indicates that about half of the surveyed households(48.33 per cent have heard about climate change. Most households in the mountain area (65.92 per cent) and female respondents(60.32 per cent) were found to have not heard about climate change.

Based on the country’s ecological belts, up to 80.37 per cent of the households in Mid-western mountain areas are aware that climate change is a major cause of drought and floods and tend to be more informed. Most households (59 per cent) noted that deforestation was the main cause for climate change. While 41.42 per cent of households believed it is caused by natural phenomenon, 33 per cent attributed it to human activities. The survey also revealed that 70.86 per cent of respondents cited major causes of climate change.

The survey reveals that about half the households have observed an increase in the incidence of drought (59.12 per cent) and 45 per cent were affected by disease/malnutrition(30.69 per cent) and sporadic rain (89.07 per cent). The survey shows that 74.29 per cent of total households have observed changes in water sources. The survey also reveals that 71.43 per cent of respondents noted that the climate change has impacted their livelihoods.

Differentiated impact of Climate Risk on women in GRB

In the following section, an attempt is made to understand how climate risk is having a differentiated impact on women.

In Nepal, agriculture accounts for nearly 70 per cent of total employment and contributes more than half of the country’s gross product (GDP). Women in Nepal constitute 72.8 per cent of the agricultural labor force, (MOAD, 2017). The impacts of climate change are not ‘gender neutral’ – women’s high dependence on agriculture means climate impacts on the sector have a disproportionate effect on women.

As high as 80 per cent of women are involved in agriculture and livestock sector in the GRB, and a trend of feminization is also observed. Women play an important role in agricultural production, the role of women and their informed and meaningful engagement in the sector is critical for food security. This issue needs to be taken into account seriously by the project when designing adaptation activities and interventions. The process to adopt their sustainable engagement in this society may not


---
**Page 162**
---

Improving Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Communities and Ecosystems in the Ganesh River Basin, Nepal

treated as farmers and they don’t have the same access to land, water, seeds, training and credit as men.
Only 10 per cent of the total farms of Nepal are owned by women or jointly owned (MDAIS, 2013).
People in the GRB are highly dependent on forest resources for their food, household energy, and livelihoods. As almost three quarters (72.4 per cent) of households solely depend on forest to fulfil their household needs, it is important that women have access to these resources. Women need to be more responsible towards collect firewood and fodder must travel longer and further to collect fire wood as well as fodder for their livestock. This gender specific role and need of women must be considered by the project.

A recently published, first of its kind, national household survey on impact of climate change in Nepal (CBS, 2016) reports that the highest percentage of sample households (92.0 per cent) observed invasive weeds as a major problem for their livelihoods and agriculture, which has reduced their income.
This is an area for consideration by the project - to enhance income and employment opportunity for women through eco-tourism and off-farm skill development training, in addition women responsive agricultural extension services.

The NARA 2010 states that women’s access to water resources would decrease leading to an increase in their workload, with detrimental effects on their reproductive health. As we explored earlier in this report at the household level, women are already responsible for collecting firewood and invasive weeds and inequitable division of labor.

The NARA states that climate induced resource conflicts increase socio violence, violence against women and gender based discrimination. This is a serious gender issue and the project will have to design interventions bearing in mind women’s role in water management, forest conservation and other natural resources. The project should also consider ways to reduce their work burden and engage them in effective management and governance of natural resources.

**Barriers/Challenges to Gender Responsive Climate-resilient Solutions**

There are several key barriers to the introduction and implementation of gender responsive climate resilient solutions:

1. **Lack of sex-disaggregated data, information, monitoring and evaluation**
   - Lack of sex-disaggregated data of climate vulnerability and risk resulting in poor understanding of gender-differentiated risks of climate change and climate induced disaster
   - Non-inclusion of gender specific indicators in the entire monitoring and evaluation cycle of climate change adaptation projects

2. **Women’s negligible representation in climate change policy, leadership and decision making**
   - Lack of conscious effort to increase representation of women in climate leadership and decision making
   - Lack of opportunity for women and their representative organisations’ views being represented in formal climate change policy, leadership and decision-making
   - Lack of planned and quality training on gender and climate change to women and men policy makers and other stakeholders

3. **Heavy unpaid workload especially of rural women and inequitable division of labor**


---
**Page 163**
---

### Improving Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Communities and Ecosystems in the Ganges River Basin, Nepal

- Women contribute their substantive labor (unpaid) in agriculture and natural resource management which does not reflect in corresponding increase in income, and meaningful representation at decision making forums and committees.
- Women reported during field visit difficulties in adapting agricultural practices to climate change patterns and impacts posed by the demand for increased agricultural productivity/ yields amidst changing climate patterns is taking its toll on women who are managing agriculture and livestock in the absence of males.
- Due to social norms and unequal gender division of labor, unpaid work is assigned to women and girls. Women’s ability to adapt to climate change is further hampered as they are more highly impacted by climate change. In addition to this, women must do domestic work like child care, cooking and fetching water and firewood which takes away their time for adaptation.
- 4. Low level of education (illiteracy)/ climate risk awareness and access to information:
    - Women’s limited access to climate risk information, early warning systems and training is making them more vulnerable and restricting their capacity to adapt.
    - Women's high level of illiteracy, low level of education and awareness is a barrier.

- Women’s high level of illiteracy, low level of education and awareness is a barrier.

### Gender discrimination, poverty, violence, and physical vulnerability of girls and women

- Early and child marriage, girls’ neglect, and opportunity for economic and social advancement, repetitive life-cycle of poverty and disempowerment.
- Climate induced disaster is increasing the vulnerability of girls and young women in terms of safety and security. Girls are more vulnerable to human trafficking and exploitative work in entertainment sector as seen after earthquake and floods.

### Women-headed households that have increased following male out-migration, are particularly vulnerable to climate change with the loss of family support networks. Girls and young boys are vulnerable to school drop outs.

### Limited livelihood options and access to productive assets

- Low access to finance, family property, productive assets and restricted access to markets due to gender bias, restrict women’s capacity to livelihood diversification which is a key activity for adaptation.
- 7. Migration/increase in fallow land and land degradation:
    - High out-migration of youth men and women induced disaster and poverty leads to a lack of labor force, resulting in increased fallow land and land degradation, mostly in rural areas.
    - Implications on food security, health and nutrition of women and children.

### 3.4 Recommendations: Key Gender-Related Activities to address gendered climate risk and barriers

1. Build Gender Equity, Climate Change Awareness and Adaptation Capacity
   - Develop GESI and climate change mainstreaming strategy for the Eco-System Based Adaptation (ESBA) project.
   - Develop GESI and climate change training manual for implementing partners and local governments.
   - 3) representatives:
     - Plan and conduct regular training on Gender Equity, Women’s empowerment and climate change at all levels central, provincial, local and community level; for both men and women.

1. Increase access of women to information and capacity building training on climate risk and adaptation measures.
2. Ensure women’s participation in workshops/meetings.
3. Train women to improve their capacity in weather observation and forecasting.
4. Activate women’s role in water resource conservation, management and its effective allocation for household use and irrigation.
5. Address gender gaps in the collection of change data and information
   - Develop gender-sensitive early warning systems
   - Develop gender-responsive maps and analyses of hazards and vulnerabilities
   - Systematically collect and analyze gender-disaggregated data on vulnerability and risk
6. Use of gender responsive climate change monitoring and evaluation indicators in the entire project cycle.
7. Livelihood diversification and alternative livelihood promotion for women and youth

8. Promotion of ecotourism and skill training in bakery, homestay, nature guide, handicrafts, cookery amongst women.

10. Introduce and promote credit schemes and other farm inputs and extension services appropriate for women

12. Engage Women in Climate Change Planning, Implementation and Monitoring
   - Promote the inclusion of women in climate change adaptation planning like LAPA and CAPA, for which Nepal is a pioneer.
   - Ensure women’s participation in planning, implementation, and disadvantaged communities in local adaptation planning, its implementation and monitoring
   - Tackle gender inequality at the broader level for adaptation financing to be effective and equitable for all genders.
13. Effective implementation of already existing provisions in constitution, law, sectoral programs and policies related to gender equality, women’s empowerment, social inclusion to enhance resilience
14. Knowledge Management and Documentation
   - Document and disseminate lessons learned from local knowledge of climate change and adaptation practices of women and disadvantaged people especially Chepang, Magh, Mushar, Bote, Tharus, Dalits, and indigenous people more dependent on forest and water for their livelihoods.
